# 📚 Pico 2500: Documentation (Projects 1301-1400)

## Batch 131: Advanced Infrared Remote 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1301: Advanced Infrared Remote Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Low-Level Protocol Auditing (Pulse-Width Timer)". At the end of this project, you will be able to measure the exact microsecond duration of Infrared (IR) carrier bursts to manually verify the "Start Bit" timing of the NEC protocol (9ms AGC burst followed by a 4.5ms space).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pulse Width Analysis**: Moving beyond simple high/low states to measuring temporal "Digital Fingerprints."
*   **Carrier Demodulation**: Understanding that an IR Receiver module strips away the 38kHz carrier, leaving behind a clean logic envelope for the Pico to measure.
*   **Microsecond Precision**: Using `ticks_us()` to capture events that happen too fast for standard `sleep()` commands.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver Module**
*   **NEC Protocol IR Remote**
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Receiver (OUT)** | GP15 | Signal Input |
| **IR Receiver (VCC)** | 3V3 | 3.3V Power |
| **IR Receiver (GND)** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_us`
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`
*   From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`

### 7. Variables
*   **start_ticks**: Microseconds (Stores the start timestamp of the pulse).
*   **duration**: Microseconds (Calculates the total time the pulse was active).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Initialization**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin to **15**. **Snap** into the `pico_start` block.
2.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** text to `"Waiting for IR..."`. **Snap** below previous block.

**Phase B: Detection & Timing**
3.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **1 (HIGH)**.
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **start_ticks**.
5.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_us`. **Snap** into the value slot of the variable.
6.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **0 (LOW)**.
7.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **duration**.
8.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`. 
    *   **Snap** current `ticks_us` into the first slot.
    *   **Snap** `start_ticks` into the second slot.

**Phase C: Output & Logic**
9.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. Use **Text** `join` to combine `"Pulse Width: "` and the **duration** variable.
10. From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **0.1 seconds**. **Snap** at the end of the loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters a standby state where it polls the IR pin for a falling edge.
2.  **Process**: When a button is pressed, the IR Receiver pulls GP15 LOW. The code breaks the first loop and captures the microsecond timestamp.
3.  **Output**: The second loop holds until the pulse ends, calculates the time difference, and prints the raw data (e.g., `9000` for an NEC header).
4.  **Repeat**: The script waits 100ms and returns to the standby loop to wait for the next pulse.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Initialization
ir_sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
print("Waiting for IR...")

while True:
    # Phase B: Detection & Timing
    # 1. Wait for signal to go LOW
    while ir_sensor.value() == 1:
        pass
    
    # 2. Mark the Start
    start_ticks = utime.ticks_us()
    
    # 3. Wait for signal to go HIGH
    while ir_sensor.value() == 0:
        pass
        
    # Phase C: Output & Logic
    # 4. Final calculation
    duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_us(), start_ticks)
    
    if duration > 100:
        print("Pulse Width: " + str(duration) + " us")
    
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Idle Logic**: Forgetting that IR Receivers are "Active Low." They output a constant 1 when *nothing* is happening. Your logic must search for the transition to 0.
*   **Blocking Problems**: If the remote button is held down, the Pico will stay inside the second `while` loop, potentially missing the rest of the message.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Gap Measurement**: Measure the time between the first and second pulses. The gap after the 9ms header is usually 4.5ms for a new command.

## 1. Project 1302: Advanced Infrared Remote Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Protocol Signal Cloning (Universal Remote)". At the end of this project, you will be able to 'learn' raw timing patterns from different remotes (TV, DVD, AC) and re-transmit them on command, creating a customized software-defined universal remote using 38kHz PWM carrier modulation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Cloning**: The process of capturing a raw waveform and replaying it regardless of the underlying protocol logic.
*   **Carrier Modulation**: Using 38,000Hz (38kHz) PWM to "carry" the digital data through the air, making it visible to standard IR receivers.
*   **UART Command Interfacing**: Triggering physical hardware actions via text commands sent from a PC Terminal.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (Capture)
*   **IR LED** (Transmit)
*   **100 Ohm Resistor**
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Receiver (OUT)** | GP15 | Input Signal |
| **IR LED (Anode)** | GP16 | Via 100Ω Resistor |
| **IR LED (Cathode)** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list`
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_uart_listener`
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_us`

### 7. Variables
*   **captured_pattern**: List (A sequence of microsecond durations representing the IR signal).
*   **cmd**: String (Stores incoming UART/Terminal text commands like "PLAY").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Setup & Signal Capture**
1.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **OP** to **Set Freq (Hz)**. **Set** Value to **38000**.
2.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **OP** to **Set Duty (%)**. **Set** Value to **0**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **captured_pattern**.
4.  From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list`. **Snap** into the variable slot.
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Wrap** around the sensing logic on Pin **15**.

**Phase B: External Control**
6.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_uart_listener`. **Set** Variable to **cmd**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Snap** inside the main loop.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_compare`. **Set** Comparison to **"=="**. **Snap** `cmd` and the text `"PLAY"` into it.

**Phase C: Retransmission**
9.  From **Loops**, drag `for_each_item`. **Set** List to **captured_pattern**.
10. From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Duty** to **50** (to transmit) or **0** (to pause).
11. From **Time**, drag `utime_sleep_us`. **Snap** the current list value into the time slot.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes GP16 with a 38kHz PWM carrier (set to 0% duty) and clears its signal memory.
2.  **Process**: When you point a remote at the sensor, the Pico monitors the logic transitions on GP15 and populates the `captured_pattern` list with timing data.
3.  **Output**: You type "PLAY" into your computer's terminal.
4.  **Repeat**: The Pico loops through the saved list, toggling the 38kHz carrier on the IR LED to recreate the exact signal pattern captured earlier.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Setup & Signal Capture
ir_in = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
ir_out = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_out.freq(38000)
ir_out.duty_u16(0)

captured_pattern = [9000, 4500, 560, 560, 560, 1690] # Example Buffer

def transmit_signal():
    print("Transmitting cloning sequence...")
    # Phase C: Retransmission
    for i, duration in enumerate(captured_pattern):
        if i % 2 == 0:
            ir_out.duty_u16(32768) # 50% Duty
        else:
            ir_out.duty_u16(0)
        utime.sleep_us(duration)
    ir_out.duty_u16(0) # Safety Stop

while True:
    # Phase B: External Control
    cmd = input("Type 'PLAY' to repeat signal: ")
    if cmd == "PLAY":
        transmit_signal()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **PWM Without modulation**: If you just turn GP16 HIGH instead of using a 38kHz PWM, the TV's IR receiver will filter it out as "background light noise."
*   **List Indexing**: Remember that the first entry in your list (index 0) is a "Pulse," the second is a "Space," and so on.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Command**: Store two different patterns (e.g., VOLUME UP and VOLUME DOWN) and trigger them with "VUP" and "VDN" commands.

## 1. Project 1303: Advanced Infrared Remote Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Software-Defined Noise Rejection (Glitch Filtering)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement multi-stage validation logic to reject false triggers caused by electromagnetic interference or fluorescent light flickering, ensuring only precise IR protocol packets are processed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Glitch Filtering**: The process of programmatically ignoring pulses that do not meet minimum temporal requirements for a known protocol.
*   **Temporal Constraints**: Validating signals based on "Duration Windows" (e.g., if a bit is supposed to be 560us, we ignore anything less than 100us).
*   **Protocol Integrity**: Balancing sensitivity and robustness to prevent "Ghost Triggers" in noisy environments.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver**
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Receiver (OUT)** | GP15 | Data Signal (Active Low) |
| **IR Receiver (VCC)** | 3V3 | Power |
| **IR Receiver (GND)** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_and`
*   From **Math**, drag `approx_equal`
*   From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`

### 7. Variables
*   **header_pulse**: Microseconds (Stores the measured duration of the first IR burst).
*   **is_valid**: Boolean (A flag that is set to True only if the pulse meets protocol standards).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Acquisition**
1.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **1 (HIGH)**.
2.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_us`. **Snap** into a variable named `start_time`.
3.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **0 (LOW)**.
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **header_pulse**.
5.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`. 
    *   **Snap** current `ticks_us` into the first slot.
    *   **Snap** `start_time` into the second slot.

**Phase B: Verification**
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Snap** inside the main loop.
7.  From **Math**, drag `approx_equal`. **Set** Target to **9000** and Tolerance to **500**.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_valid** to **True**.

**Phase C: Filter Execution**
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** Condition to **header_pulse < 500**.
10. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** value to `"Glitch Rejected: Too Short"`. **Snap** inside the `if` block.
11. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** value to `"SUCCESS: Valid NEC Header Detected"`.
12. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_valid** to **False** at the end of the loop to reset.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico polls the IR receiver pin and records the exactly timing of the first falling edge.
2.  **Process**: The script calculates the length of the pulse and runs it through a two-stage filter (Glitch check and Protocol check).
3.  **Output**: If the pulse is < 500us, it is logged as a "glitch." If it is ~9000us, it is logged as a "success."
4.  **Repeat**: The logic resets the **is_valid** flag and returns to the top of the acquisition loop.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Acquisition
ir_sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def check_header():
    # Setup state
    while ir_sensor.value() == 1: 
        pass
    start = utime.ticks_us()
    while ir_sensor.value() == 0: 
        pass
    duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_us(), start)
    
    # Phase B: Verification
    is_valid = False
    
    # 1. Reject micro-glitches
    if duration < 500:
        print("GLITCH REJECTED: Pulse too short (" + str(duration) + " us)")
        return False
        
    # Phase C: Filter Execution
    # 2. Check for NEC Signature (9000us)
    if 8500 < duration < 9500:
        is_valid = True
        
    return is_valid

while True:
    if check_header():
        print("SUCCESS: Valid NEC Protocol Header Found!")
    
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Too Strict Tolerance**: If you check for *exactly* 9000us, the project will always fail. Remotes fluctuate by 5-10% due to battery levels and temperature.
*   **Blocking in noise**: If the receiver is flooded with continuous sunlight, it might stay inside a `while` loop forever.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Data Count Validation**: Add a secondary filter that counts the total number of bits in the message. For NEC, a valid packet must have exactly 32 bits after the header.

## 1. Project 1304: Advanced Infrared Remote Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Intelligent Protocol Sniffer (Header Classification)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a tool that identifies different remote control brands (NEC, Sony, Philips) by automatically analyzing their unique microsecond header signatures and displaying the protocol type on an I2C OLED screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Protocol Classification**: Using unique timing "Signatures" (specific durations of high/low pulses) to identify different hardware manufacturers.
*   **Protocol Standards**: Understanding that Sony (SIRC) uses a 2400us header, while NEC uses a 9000us header.
*   **Visual Status Feedback**: Updating a 128x64 display with real-time diagnostic results to provide a professional user interface.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver**
*   **SSD1306 OLED (I2C)**
*   **Assorted Remote Controls** (NEC and Sony protocol)
*   **Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock |
| **IR Receiver (OUT)** | GP15 | Pulse Input |
| **VCC / GND** | 3.3V / GND| Power Link |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_init`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_if`
*   From **Math**, drag `range_check`
*   From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`

### 7. Variables
*   **header_width**: Microseconds (The measured duration of the first incoming pulse).
*   **proto_name**: String (The text label identifying the brand, e.g., "NEC").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Initialize Visual Interface**
1.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_init`. **Set** SDA to **4** and SCL to **5**. **Snap** into `pico_start`.
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to `"SNIFFER READY"`.

**Phase B: Protocol Analysis**
3.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **1 (HIGH)**.
4.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_us`. **Snap** into a variable named `capture_start`.
5.  From **Loops**, drag `while_pin_is`. **Set** Pin to **15** and **State** to **0 (LOW)**.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **header_width**.
7.  From **Time**, drag `ticks_diff`. **Snap** current `ticks_us` and `capture_start` into it.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_if`.
9.  From **Math**, drag `range_check`. **Set** Range to **8500** to **9500**. **Snap** `header_width` into it. **Set** `proto_name` to `"NEC / APPLE"`.
10. From **Math**, drag `range_check`. **Set** Range to **2200** to **2600**. **Snap** `header_width` into it. **Set** `proto_name` to `"SONY SIRC"`.

**Phase C: Feedback & Display**
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. Use **Text** `join` keyword to combine `"BRAND: "` and the **proto_name** variable.
13. From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **1 second** to ensure the result is readable.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The OLED display initializes and shows a "READY" message, while the Pico enters a waiting loop.
2.  **Process**: When an IR signal is received, the script measures the header pulse width and compares it against known manufacturer "Signatures."
3.  **Output**: The identified protocol name (NEC, Sony, or Unknown) is printed to the OLED screen alongside the raw pulse duration.
4.  **Repeat**: The script clears the result, displays "READY" again, and returns to the top of the signal capture loop.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Initialize Visual Interface
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
ir_sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

oled.fill(0)
oled.text("SNIFFER READY", 10, 20)
oled.show()

while True:
    # Phase B: Protocol Analysis
    if ir_sensor.value() == 0:
        capture_start = utime.ticks_us()
        while ir_sensor.value() == 0: 
            pass 
        header_width = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_us(), capture_start)
        
        proto_name = "Unknown"
        
        # Protocol Decision Matrix
        if 8500 < header_width < 9500:
            proto_name = "NEC / APPLE"
        elif 2200 < header_width < 2600:
            proto_name = "SONY SIRC"
            
        # Phase C: Feedback & Display
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text("PROTOCOL FOUND", 0, 0)
        oled.text(proto_name, 0, 25)
        oled.text(str(header_width) + " us", 0, 45)
        oled.show()
        
        utime.sleep(1) # Visibility delay
        
        # Reset to ready
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text("READY...", 10, 20)
        oled.show()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **I2C Blocking**: Drawing to the OLED takes time (approx 30-50ms). If you start drawing while the IR remote is still sending data bits, you will miss the entire rest of the message. Always measure the time first, then draw.
*   **Inverted Logic**: Remember the VS1838B is "Active Low." The header pulse starts when the pin goes to `0`, not `1`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Data Bit Counter**: Modify the code to count how many pulses follow the header. NEC remotes should have 32 bits, while Sony remotes vary (12, 15, or 20 bits).

## 1. Project 1305: Automated Advanced Infrared Remote Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Voice-to-IR Smart Bridge (Cross-Platform Automation)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a gateway device that receives text commands from a PC over USB-Serial (simulating a Voice Assistant like Alexa or Siri) and translates them into IR signals to control non-smart appliances like older Air Conditioners.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Bridge Architecture**: Acting as a translator between high-level human-readable commands (UART/USB) and low-level hardware protocols (IR).
*   **Command Mapping (Dictionaries)**: Creating a data structure where words like "COOL" uniquely trigger specific hex payloads like `0xFFAA00`.
*   **Signal Reconstruction**: Using a microcontroller to "Blast" complex timing patterns on demand.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR LED**
*   **100 Ohm Resistor**
*   **USB Cable** (For Serial Power/Data)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR LED (Anode)** | GP16 | Modulated Output |
| **IR LED (Cathode)**| GND | Ground |
| **Pico USB** | PC | Serial Command Data |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_uart_listener`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_dictionary_contains`
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print`

### 7. Variables
*   **voice_cmd**: String (The text string received via the UART/Serial bridge).
*   **ir_library**: Dictionary (A lookup table mapping names like "COOL" to hex codes like `0xFFAA00`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Library Definition**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **ir_library**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** pairs to `"HEAT": 0xFF1234` and `"COOL": 0xFF5678`. **Snap** into the variable slot.

**Phase B: Command Listening**
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_uart_listener`. **Set** Variable to **voice_cmd**.
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** Condition to **if_dictionary_contains**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag **ir_library** and **voice_cmd** into the dictionary check slots.

**Phase C: Translation & Blasting**
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **hex_code**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `get_value_from_key`. **Snap** the `voice_cmd` (Key) and `ir_library` (Dictionary) into it.
8.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`. **Snap** the **hex_code** variable into the value slot.
9.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** value to `"Status: Command Transmitted"`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes a "Codebook" in memory that acts as a translator between human words and infrared light pulses.
2.  **Process**: The system waits for a command from the computer terminal. When "COOL" is received, it verifies the word exists in the dictionary and retrieves the corresponding hex code.
3.  **Output**: The IR LED converts the hex code into a 38kHz modulated signal, mimicking the original appliance remote.
4.  **Repeat**: The system sends a confirmation message to the computer and returns to listening for the next command.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import sys

# Phase A: Library Definition
ir_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_led.freq(38000)
ir_led.duty_u16(0)

# The "Smart" Codebook
ir_library = {
    "AC_ON":  0xFF4321,
    "AC_OFF": 0xFF8765,
    "COOL":   0xFFAA00,
    "HEAT":   0xFFBB11
}

print("--- SMART HOME BRIDGE ONLINE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Command Listening
    # (Simulated UART block listener check)
    if sys.stdin in utime.select.select([sys.stdin], [], [], 0)[0]:
        voice_cmd = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        
        # Phase C: Translation & Blasting
        if voice_cmd in ir_library:
            hex_code = ir_library[voice_cmd]
            print(f"TRANSLATING: '{voice_cmd}' -> {hex(hex_code)}")
            
            # IR Blast Execution
            ir_led.duty_u16(32768)
            utime.sleep_ms(100) # Duration of header bit
            ir_led.duty_u16(0)
            
            print("OK: Infrared Blast Sent.")
        else:
            print(f"ERROR: '{voice_cmd}' not in codebook.")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Matching Strings**: Computer terminals often add a newline character (`\n`) to your text. If your dictionary says "COOL" but the input is "COOL\n", the match will fail. Always `.strip()` your input!
*   **Library Overhead**: Large dictionaries consume RAM. Keep your command list focused on essential functions.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Macro Commands**: Create a "GOODBYE" command that sends three IR codes in a row (TV OFF, AC OFF, LIGHTS OFF) with 1-second delays between them.

## 1. Project 1306: Advanced Infrared Remote Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Cloning Security (Rolling Code Handshake)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a synchronized rolling code system between two Picos that prevents "Replay Attacks" (where an attacker records and plays back a signal) by using a strictly incrementing sequence counter.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Replay Attack Vulnerability**: Understanding why static IR codes (like TV remotes) are fatal for security applications—a simple "Logic Sniffer" can steal and reuse the key.
*   **Rolling Code Synchronization**: The cryptographic principle where the sender and receiver both track a shared "Counter" that changes after every successful event.
*   **Payload Construction**: Building a digital packet that contains both a Static Signature (Identity) and a Dynamic Counter (Freshness).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2x Raspberry Pi Picos** (Sender and Receiver)
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (Receiver Pico)
*   **IR LED** (Sender Pico)
*   **100 Ohm Resistor**
*   **Button** (Sender Pico)
*   **Green LED** (Receiver Pico - "Lock")

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico 1 (Sender) | Pico 2 (Recv) | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Button** | GP12 | - | Unlock Trigger |
| **IR LED** | GP16 | - | Modulated Blast |
| **IR Receiver**| - | GP15 | Signal Capture |
| **Green LED** | - | GP14 | Lock Status |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `addition`
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable_by`
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`

### 7. Variables
### 7. Variables
*   **tx_counter**: Integer (Increments after every transmission to ensure code uniqueness).
*   **rx_last_valid**: Integer (Stores the highest rolling code number received to block replays).
*   **SECRET_ID**: Hexadecimal (A unique identifier that links the specific remote to the lock).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Sender Token Generation**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **SECRET_ID** to **43707** (0xAABB). **Snap** into `pico_start`.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **tx_counter** to **100**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** input to Pin **12**.
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **rolling_code**.
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **+**. **Snap** `SECRET_ID` and `tx_counter` into it.
6.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`. **Snap** the **rolling_code** variable into the value slot.
7.  From **Variables**, drag `change_variable_by`. **Set** variable to **tx_counter** and value to **1**.

**Phase B: Receiver Signal Capture**
8.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `on_ir_receive`. **Set** Pin to **15**.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **incoming_val**.
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **new_count**.
11. From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Snap** `incoming_val` and `SECRET_ID` into it.

**Phase C: Security Handshake**
12. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. Create a condition using **Logic** `if_greater_than`.
13. **Check** if **new_count** is greater than **rx_last_valid**.
14. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **14** to **1 (HIGH)**.
15. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **rx_last_valid** to **new_count**.
16. From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **2 seconds**. **Set** Pin **14** back to **0 (LOW)**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Both the Sender and Receiver Picos are initialized with a matching SECRET_ID and an initial counter value.
2.  **Process**: Every time the button is pressed, the Sender creates a "Fresh" pulse sequence by adding 1 to its counter and blasting the result via IR.
3.  **Output**: The Receiver captures the pulse, extracts the counter, and performs a "Security Audit." If the counter is higher than the last one seen, it unlocks the green LED.
4.  **Repeat**: If a malicious user replays an old signal, the Receiver's audit identifies the "stale" counter and refuses to unlock.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)

#### Sender Code (Pico 1)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Token Generation
btn = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
ir_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_led.freq(38000)

SECRET_ID = 0xAAAA00
tx_counter = 100

while True:
    if btn.value() == 0:
        # Construct Packing
        rolling_code = SECRET_ID + tx_counter
        
        # Fire IR Blast
        ir_led.duty_u16(32768)
        utime.sleep_ms(100)
        ir_led.duty_u16(0)
        
        tx_counter += 1 # Advance sequence
        utime.sleep(1) # Debounce
```

#### Receiver Code (Pico 2)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase B: Signal Capture
lock_led = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
ir_sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
SECRET_ID = 0xAAAA00
rx_last_valid = 100

def validate_signal(signal):
    global rx_last_valid
    # Phase C: Security Handshake
    new_count = signal - SECRET_ID
    
    if new_count > rx_last_valid:
        print("ACCESS GRANTED: Token " + str(new_count))
        rx_last_valid = new_count
        lock_led.value(1)
        utime.sleep(2)
        lock_led.value(0)
    else:
        print("ALERT: REPLAY ATTACK DETECTED")

while True:
    if ir_sensor.value() == 0:
        incoming = 0xAAAA65 # Mock signal
        validate_signal(incoming)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Overflow**: If your counter reaches 65535, it might reset to 0. Real systems use 32-bit or 64-bit numbers to prevent this "rollover" for decades.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Window of Acceptance**: Allow the counter to be up to 100 values ahead (in case the remote was pressed out of range), but never "behind."

## 1. Project 1307: Advanced Infrared Remote Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Interface Analytics (Usage Statistics Logging)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a data logger that captures remote control interactions, calculates the frequency of specific button presses (like "Volume Up" vs "Volume Down"), and serializes this histogram into a structured JSON file on the Pico’s internal flash storage.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **JSON (JavaScript Object Notation)**: A lightweight data-interchange format that is easy for humans to read and machines to parse. In MicroPython, we use `ujson` to turn dictionaries into strings.
*   **Frequency Histograms**: A statistical method used to track how often specific events occur. Each unique button code becomes a "Key," and its count becomes the "Value."
*   **Flash Wear-Leveling (Flash Preservation)**: Understanding that writing to memory too often (e.g., Every millisecond) can physically damage the Pico. We implement "Lazy Saving" to preserve hardware life.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (Connected to GP15)
*   **Any IR Remote Control** (TV, DVD, etc.)
*   **Micro USB Cable** (For console monitoring)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Data (Out)** | GP15 | Pulse Stream |
| **IR VCC** | 3V3 | Power |
| **IR GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `on_ir_receive`
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`
*   From **Variables**, drag `update_dictionary`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_chain`
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator` (Addition)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `file_write_json`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_1s`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print`

### 7. Variables
*   **usage_stats**: Dictionary (Stores the tally of button presses for specific remote commands).
*   **raw_code**: String (The temporary hex value of the most recently received IR signal).
*   **save_timer**: Seconds (A counter used to determine when to commit RAM data to flash memory).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Database Initialization**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **usage_stats**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** keys to `"VOL_UP": 0` and `"VOL_DOWN": 0`. **Snap** into the variable slot.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **save_timer**. **Set** value to **0**.

**Phase B: Interaction Capture**
4.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `on_ir_receive`. **Set** Pin to **15**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **raw_code**. **Snap** the captured signal value into it.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** condition to check if **raw_code** is equal to your target hex code.
7.  From **Variables**, drag `update_dictionary`. **Set** Key to `"VOL_UP"`. 
8.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **+**. **Snap** the current dictionary value and **1** into it.

**Phase C: Flash Persistence**
9.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **1 second**.
10. From **Variables**, drag `change_variable_by`. **Set** Variable to **save_timer** and value to **1**.
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** condition to **save_timer >= 60**.
12. From **Smart IO**, drag `file_write_json`. **Set** Filename to `"usage.json"` and data to **usage_stats**.
13. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **save_timer** back to **0**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico allocates memory for a usage dictionary and initializes a "Lazy Save" timer set to 60 seconds.
2.  **Process**: When a signal arrives, the script checks if it matches a relevant button and increments the count inside the Pico's RAM for speed.
3.  **Output**: Every 60 seconds, the Pico realizes it is time to backup the data. It serializes the dictionary into a JSON string and writes it to the internal flash drive.
4.  **Repeat**: The Pico clears its save timer and returns to monitoring the IR frequency.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import ujson

# Phase A: Database Initialization
ir_sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
VOL_UP_CODE = 0xFFAABB
VOL_DOWN_CODE = 0xFFCCDD

usage_stats = {"VOL_UP": 0, "VOL_DOWN": 0}
save_timer = 0
last_tick = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    # Phase B: Interaction Capture
    # (Simulated decoding logic)
    raw_code = 0xFFAABB # Mock 
    
    if raw_code == VOL_UP_CODE:
        usage_stats["VOL_UP"] += 1
    elif raw_code == VOL_DOWN_CODE:
        usage_stats["VOL_DOWN"] += 1
        
    # Phase C: Flash Persistence
    if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_tick) > 1000:
        save_timer += 1
        last_tick = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    if save_timer >= 60:
        with open("usage.json", "w") as f:
            f.write(ujson.dumps(usage_stats))
        save_timer = 0
        print("Status: JSON Updated")
            
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flash Wear**: Writing to the internal Pico flash too many times can physically damage it. Only save data once an hour or after a long period of inactivity!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Time-of-Day Logging**: If you have an RTC (Real Time Clock), log *when* the buttons were pressed to see if you watch TV more in the morning or evening.

## 1. Project 1308: Smart Advanced Infrared Remote System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Active Signal Conditioning (IR Repeater & Range Extender)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a high-performance IR repeater that intercepts weak, noisy signals from an IR remote and "Regenerates" a crisp, high-power carrier wave to control devices hidden inside closed cabinets or around corners.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Conditioning**: The process of "Cleaning" an input signal (removing noise and jitter) before processing or re-transmitting it.
*   **Demodulation vs. Remodulation**: An IR receiver "Demodulates" (removes the 38kHz carrier) to give us clean bits. To repeat it, the Pico must "Remodulate" it by generating a new 38kHz PWM wave.
*   **Latency & Jitter**: Understanding that any delay (Latency) in the repeater could break the timing of sensitive protocols like NEC or RC5. We use high-speed polling or hardware interrupts to minimize this.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (The "Ear")
*   **IR LED** (The "Voice")
*   **2N2222 Transistor** (Optional: For range boosting/High current drive)
*   **100Ω Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Receiver (Data Out)**| GP15 | Demodulated Pulse Input |
| **IR LED (Gate/Anode)** | GP16 | 38kHz PWM Remodulator |
| **Power Supply** | 3V3 / GND | System Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_frequency` (Set to 38000)
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_digital_input_low`
*   From **Loops**, drag `repeat_forever`

### 7. Variables
*   **signal_detected**: Boolean (A flag that is True when the IR receiver pin is pulled LOW by an external signal).
*   **carrier_freq**: Hertz (The target frequency of 38000Hz used to remodulate the outgoing infrared blast).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: I/O Configuration**
1.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **OP** to **Set Freq (Hz)**. **Set** Value to **38000**.
2.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **OP** to **Set Duty (%)**. **Set** Value to **0** (Start Silent).
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin to **15**. **Snap** into `pico_start`.

**Phase B: Real-Time Monitoring**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** onto the workspace.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** inside the loop.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_compare`. **Set** Comparison to **"=="**. **Snap** input from **GP15** and the value **0** into it.

**Phase C: Pulse Regeneration**
7.  **In the IF slot (Signal Found)**: From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Duty** to **50**.
8.  **In the ELSE slot (Signal Lost)**: From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Duty** to **0**.
9.  From **Time**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Set** to **1** (Optional: Minimal debounce delay).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes GP16 with a 38kHz PWM carrier and checks GP15 for incoming infrared activity.
2.  **Process**: The script monitors the receiver pin at high speeds. When the pin is LOW, it instantly turns on the local 38kHz transmitter.
3.  **Output**: The IR LED regenerates the exact timing of the incoming message, blasting it at full power to reach distant or hidden appliances.
4.  **Repeat**: The script continuously toggles the transmitter to stay perfectly synchronized with the remote's pulse envelope.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: I/O Configuration
# The "Ear" (Receiver)
ir_recv = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

# The "Voice" (Transmitter) - PWM conditioned at 38kHz
ir_blast = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_blast.freq(38000)
ir_blast.duty_u16(0)

print("--- ACTIVE IR REPEATER ONLINE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Real-Time Monitoring
    # We use a tight loop for sub-millisecond response
    if ir_recv.value() == 0:
        # Phase C: Pulse Regeneration
        # Detected IR Pulse (Active Low) - Remodulate with 50% duty cycle
        ir_blast.duty_u16(32768) 
    else:
        # Silence when no signal is present
        ir_blast.duty_u16(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Feedback Loop"**: If your IR LED is pointed directly back at your IR Receiver, the Pico will hear its own transmit, trigger again, and get stuck in a "Continuous Blast" loop. Always place a piece of cardboard or a cabinet door between the "Ear" and the "Voice."
*   **Duty Cycle**: If you set the PWM duty to 100%, the LED will stay on constantly and won't be seen as a 38kHz carrier by the TV. It MUST be 50% to properly "Blink" at the target frequency.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Frequency Repeater**: Some devices use 40kHz (Sony) and some use 38kHz (NEC). Add a button that toggles the `ir_blast.freq()` so you can support different brands of appliances.

## 1. Project 1309: Advanced Infrared Remote Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Receiver Bandwidth & Carrier Optimization (Hardware Profiling)". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a "Carrier Frequency Sweep" to determine the exact resonant frequency of an IR receiver and identify the "De-tuning Point" where signal reliability drops below 50% due to bandpass filter limitations.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Resonant Frequency**: Every IR receiver is physically "Tuned" to a center frequency (standard is 38kHz). Efficiency and range drop sharply if the transmitter is even 2kHz off.
*   **Bandpass Filtering**: Understanding the internal electronic filter inside the IR sensor that ignores light flickering at non-target frequencies (like sunlight or lightbulbs).
*   **Diagnostic Sweeping**: Systematically changing a variable (Frequency) to observe the output response, a common technique in RF and electronic engineering.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (The Target)
*   **IR LED** (The Signal Generator)
*   **100Ω Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR LED (Anode)** | GP16 | Variable Frequency Output |
| **IR Receiver (Out)**| GP15 | Success Indicator |
| **Micro USB** | PC | Analytics Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag `for_loop_integer`
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_digital_input_low`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print`

### 7. Variables
*   `test_freq`: The specific frequency being tested (Range: 30000 to 45000Hz).
*   `hit_count`: Total successful detections at a specific frequency.
*   `peak_freq`: The frequency that resulted in the most reliable triggers.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
**A. Sweep Initialization**
1.  **Define the Test Bounds**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag a `for_loop_integer`.
    *   Set Range: **30000** to **46000**, Step: **500**.
2.  **Calibrate Carrier**:
### 7. Variables
*   **test_freq**: Hertz (The specific carrier frequency being generated for the audit).
*   **hit_count**: Integer (The number of successful detections logged at the current frequency).
*   **peak_freq**: Hertz (The frequency that produced the strongest and most reliable response).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Sweep Initialization**
1.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_for_loop`. **Set** Range from **30000** to **46000** with a step of **500**.
2.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **OP** to **Set Freq (Hz)**. **Snap** the **test_freq** variable into the value slot.

**Phase B: Diagnostic Burst**
3.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Duty** to **50**.
4.  From **Time**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Set** to **100**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Snap** onto the workspace.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_compare`. **Set** Comparison to **"=="**. **Snap** input from **GP15** and the value **0** into it.
7.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** value to combine **test_freq** and the text `" - SUCCESS"`. **Snap** inside the `if` block.

**Phase C: Analytic Reporting**
8.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_pwm`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Duty** to **0** (Reset carrier).
9.  From **Time**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Set** to **200** to allow the sensor to settle.
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **peak_freq** to the value with the most consecutive successful hits.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins a systematic frequency sweep starting at 30,000Hz (well outside the target range).
2.  **Process**: The script fires a 100ms burst at each frequency and listens for a response on the receiver pin.
3.  **Output**: Data is printed to the console, showing exactly where the receiver's internal filter begins to accept and reject signals.
4.  **Repeat**: The script increments the frequency by 500Hz and continues until it reaches the 46,000Hz ceiling, providing a full "Sensitivity Map."

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Sweep Initialization
ir_gen = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_recv = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

START_FREQ = 30000
END_FREQ = 46000
STEP = 500

print("--- IR CARRIER SENSITIVITY SWEEP ---")

for test_freq in range(START_FREQ, END_FREQ + STEP, STEP):
    # Phase B: Diagnostic Burst
    ir_gen.freq(test_freq)
    
    # Fire Test Probe
    ir_gen.duty_u16(32768)
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
    
    # Audit Response
    if ir_recv.value() == 0:
        status = "PASSED"
        visual = "[######]"
    else:
        status = "FAILED"
        visual = "[......]"
        
    print(f"Testing {test_freq}Hz: {visual} {status}")
    
    # Phase C: Analytic Reporting (Settling)
    ir_gen.duty_u16(0)
    utime.sleep_ms(200)

print("--- SWEEP COMPLETE ---")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Proximity Error"**: If your IR LED is touching the receiver, it might trigger even at the "wrong" frequency due to pure intensity overwhelming the filter. Move the LED 2-3 meters away for an accurate frequency test.
*   **Duty Cycle Jitter**: Standard IR receivers expect a 30% to 50% duty cycle. If you set it to 1%, it won't trigger even at the perfect frequency.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sensitivity vs. Distance**: Run the test at 1 meter, then 3 meters, then 6 meters. Does the "Frequency Window" get narrower as the distance increases? (Hint: Yes, it does!).

## 1. Project 1310: Advanced Advanced Infrared Remote Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Laser Tag Paradigm (ID Modulation & Vest Logic)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a complete IR Combat System prototype where a "Gun" transmits a unique Player ID and the "Vest" (Receiver) processes hits, handles team-based exclusion logic, and manages a multi-life health system.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Unique ID Modulation**: Using specific binary signatures (e.g., `0x01` for Team Red) to distinguish between friendly fire and enemy hits.
*   **State Machine Management**: Controlling the "Game Flow" by moving through different logical states: `READY`, `STUNNED`, and `ELIMINATED`.
*   **Persistence & Latching**: Ensuring that once a hit is registered, the player cannot fire their gun for a "Cool-down" period (e.g., 2 seconds).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2x Raspberry Pi Picos** (For Gun and Vest simulation)
*   **IR LED with Focus Tube** (The Gun)
*   **VS1838B IR Receiver** (The Vest)
*   **Buzzer** (Hit Indicator)
*   **RGB LED** (Health Status)
*   **Button** (Trigger)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Trigger Button** | GP12 | Start Transmission |
| **IR LED (Gun)** | GP16 | Team ID Blaster |
| **IR Receiver (Vest)**| GP15 | Hit Listener |
| **RGB LED (Red/Grn)**| GP14 | Life Indicator |
| **Buzzer** | GP13 | Audio Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `on_ir_receive_packet`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_player_id_not_team`
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`

### 7. Variables
*   **my_team_id**: Integer (Identifier for the player's team, e.g., 1 for Red).
*   **player_lives**: Integer (Remaining health points before elimination).
*   **is_stunned**: Boolean (A flag that disables the gun temporarily after being hit).
*   **enemy_id**: Integer (The team identifier extracted from an incoming IR signal).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Gun Logic**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **my_team_id** to **1**. **Snap** into `pico_start`.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **GP12** (Trigger) is **0 (LOW)** AND **is_stunned** is **FALSE**.
3.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `ir_send_nec`. **Snap** the **my_team_id** variable into the value slot.
4.  From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **0.5 seconds** (Fire rate limit).

**Phase B: Vest Logic**
5.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `on_ir_receive_packet`. **Set** Pin to **15**.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **enemy_id**. **Snap** the packet value into it.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** condition to check if **enemy_id** is **NOT EQUAL** to **my_team_id**.

**Phase C: Hit Response**
8.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Snap** **player_lives** and **1** into it.
9.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **13** (Buzzer) to **1** for **0.2 seconds**.
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_stunned** to **TRUE**.
11. From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **2 seconds**.
12. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_stunned** to **FALSE**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The player is assigned to Team 1 and starts with 5 lives. The "Gun" and "Vest" modules are initialized.
2.  **Process**: When the player's vest captures an IR signal, it compares the incoming ID to its own. If they differ, a hit is registered.
3.  **Output**: The buzzer sounds, the player loses a life, and the "Stun" flag is raised to prevent the player from firing back for 2 seconds.
4.  **Repeat**: The script monitors the health count. If lives reach zero, the vest locks the system and displays a "Game Over" color on the RGB LED.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Gun Logic Setup
gun_trigger = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
ir_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ir_led.freq(38000)

# Phase B: Vest Logic Setup
vest_rx = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
buzzer = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)
health_led = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Game State
MY_TEAM = 1
player_lives = 5
is_stunned = False

def process_hit(enemy_id):
    global player_lives, is_stunned
    # Phase C: Hit Response
    if enemy_id != MY_TEAM:
        player_lives -= 1
        is_stunned = True
        print(f"HIT! Lives Remaining: {player_lives}")
        
        # Audio/Visual Feedback
        buzzer.value(1)
        utime.sleep(0.2)
        buzzer.value(0)
        
        if player_lives <= 0:
            print("GAME OVER")
            health_led.value(1) # Solid Red
        
        utime.sleep(2) # Stun duration
        is_stunned = False

while True:
    # Handle Shooting
    if gun_trigger.value() == 0 and not is_stunned and player_lives > 0:
        # Blast IR ID
        ir_led.duty_u16(32768)
        utime.sleep_ms(100)
        ir_led.duty_u16(0)
        utime.sleep(0.5) 
        
    # Handle Receiving (Simulated hit)
    # enemy_id = ir_recv.capture()
    # process_hit(enemy_id)
    
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Narrow Beam**: IR LEDs naturally spread light in a wide cone. For "Laser Tag," you MUST put the LED inside a black tube or behind a lens to create a narrow "In-visible Beam."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ammo Count**: Limit the player to 10 shots. They must find an "Ammo Box" IR beacon to reload!

## Batch 132: Advanced Keypad Security 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1311: Advanced Keypad Security Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Interrupt-Driven Matrix Scanning (Low-Power Architecture)". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a battery-efficient keypad interface that uses "Pin Change Interrupts" (IRQs) to wake the Raspberry Pi Pico from "Lightsleep" only when a button is pressed, significantly extending the operational life of security devices.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polling vs. Interrupts**: Polling consumes constant energy by checking the keypad thousands of times per second. Interrupts allow the Pico to "Sleep" and only consume power when the hardware pins detect a physical change (falling edge).
*   **Lightsleep Mode**: A power-saving state where the Pico's CPU is paused but RAM is preserved, allowing for instant wake-up when an external event occurs.
*   **Falling Edge Detection**: Configuring row pins as inputs with pull-up resistors, so that dragging them to GND (by pressing a key) triggers a "Falling" interrupt event.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Membrane Keypad**
*   **Internal Pull-up Resistors** (Built into the Pico)
*   **LED** (Wake-up Indicator)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Signal Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Row 1** | GP2 | Input (Pull-Up) | Matrix Row |
| **Row 2** | GP3 | Input (Pull-Up) | Matrix Row |
| **Row 3** | GP4 | Input (Pull-Up) | Matrix Row |
| **Row 4** | GP5 | Input (Pull-Up) | Matrix Row |
| **Column 1-4** | GP6-GP9 | Output (Driven LOW)| Matrix Columns |
| **Status LED** | GP15 | Output | Wake Visual |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pin_interrupt` (Trigger: FALLING)
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_sleep_light`
*   From **Logic**, drag `set_variable` (Flag management)
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print`

### 7. Variables
*   **key_detected**: Boolean (A flag that is set to True by the hardware interrupt when a physical keypress is measured).
*   **active_row**: Integer (Tracks the specific row pin identifier that triggered the wake-up event).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Matrix Preparation**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pins **6, 7, 8, 9** (Columns) as outputs.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_value`. **Set** all Column Pins to **0 (LOW)** to create a voltage sink.
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pins **2, 3, 4, 5** (Rows) as inputs with **Internal Pull-Up** enabled.

**Phase B: Interrupt Configuration**
4.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Set** Pin to **2** and Trigger to **Falling Edge**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **key_detected** to **TRUE** inside the interrupt block.
6.  **Repeat** the interrupt attachment for GP Pins **3, 4, and 5**.

**Phase C: Low-Power Cycle**
7.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** onto the workspace.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Set** condition to check if **key_detected** is **FALSE**.
9.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_sleep_light`. **Snap** inside the `if` block.
10. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **15** to **1** (Wake LED).
11. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **key_detected** back to **FALSE** at the end of the scan.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the keypad matrix and enters a "Lightsleep" state, pausing the CPU to conserve power while waiting for a hardware event.
2.  **Process**: When you press a key, the connection pulls a Row pin to ground. This triggers a "Pin Change Interrupt," which flips the `key_detected` flag and instantly wakes the CPU.
3.  **Output**: The Pico turns on a status LED and prints a wake-up notification to the console before scanning the matrix to identify the key.
4.  **Repeat**: The script resets the detection flag and returns the Pico to hibernation, ensuring battery life is maximized.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Matrix Preparation
rows = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP) for i in range(2, 6)]
cols = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.OUT) for i in range(6, 10)]
wake_led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

key_detected = False

# ISR (Interrupt Service Routine)
def wake_isr(pin):
    global key_detected
    key_detected = True

# Phase B: Interrupt Configuration
for r in rows:
    r.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handler=wake_isr)

# Force columns LOW to trap the row signals
for c in cols:
    c.value(0)

while True:
    # Phase C: Low-Power Cycle
    if not key_detected:
        print("Zzz... Pico entering lightsleep.")
        utime.sleep_ms(100) 
        machine.lightsleep()
        # Pico freezes here until IRQ fires
    
    if key_detected:
        wake_led.value(1)
        print("WAKE-UP: Keypress registered!")
        
        utime.sleep_ms(300) # Debounce
        key_detected = False
        wake_led.value(0)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "IRQ Print" Trap**: Never use `print()` or `utime.sleep()` inside the `wake_isr` function. This will cause the Pico to crash or hang because interrupts cannot use complex services. Always use a flag.
*   **Floating Pins**: If your keypad columns are set to "Input" instead of "Output LOW", the rows will never fall to ground, and the interrupt will never trigger.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Battery Benchmark**: If you have a multimeter, measure the current (Amps) while the Pico is awake vs. when it is in `lightsleep`. You should see the power usage drop by over 80%!

## 1. Project 1312: Advanced Keypad Security Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Predictive Text Algorithms (T9 & Multi-Tap Logic)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "T9 Predictive" text engine on a manual 12-button keypad, allowing the Pico to translate numeric sequences (e.g., "4-3-5-5-6") into their most likely word candidates (e.g., "HELLO") using a pre-calculated dictionary.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **T9 (Text on 9 Keys)**: A legacy mobile technology that maps multiple letters to a single digit (2=ABC, 3=DEF). Instead of tapping "2" three times for 'C', you tap it once, and the software "Predicts" the word based on the full sequence.
*   **Sequence Matching**: The logic of converting a word (like "DOG") into its numeric code ("364") and searching for that code in a database.
*   **Data Structures for Search**: Using Python Dictionaries to map strings of numbers to specific words for O(1) instantaneous lookup.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display** (I2C)
*   **Buzzer** (Typing click sound)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Text Output |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Text Output |
| **Keypad** | GP6-GP13 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | PWM | Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `concatenate_strings`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_dictionary_contains`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_text`
*   From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`

### 7. Variables
*   **tap_buffer**: String (The sequence of physical digits typed by the user, e.g., "43556").
*   **t9_dictionary**: Dictionary (A lookup table mapping numeric strings to their predicted words).
*   **active_guess**: String (The word currently identified as the "best fit" for the typed sequence).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Linguistic Database**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **t9_dictionary**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** entries like `"228": "CAT"` and `"364": "DOG"`. **Snap** into the variable slot.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **tap_buffer** to an empty text string `""`.

**Phase B: Input & Prediction**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **6-13**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **tap_buffer** to combine its current value and the **key** value using **Text** `concatenate`.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** inside the press event.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `if_dictionary_contains`. **Pass** **t9_dictionary** and **tap_buffer** into the check slots.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **active_guess** to **get_value_from_key** (If found) or `"???"` (If missing).

**Phase C: Visual Feedback**
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Snap** after logic.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 1 to **tap_buffer**.
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 2 to **active_guess**.
12. From **Wait**, drag `pico_beep`. **Set** to **50ms** for a tactile click sound.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico loads a vocabulary of common words into its RAM, indexing them by their numeric T9 key equivalents.
2.  **Process**: Every time a key is pressed, the system appends the number to a buffer and cross-references that specific sequence against the database.
3.  **Output**: The OLED screen displays the typed numbers on the top line and instantly updates the predicted word on the bottom line.
4.  **Repeat**: If the user presses the 'Clear' key, the buffer resets to empty, and the system waits for a new word.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Linguistic Database
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

t9_dictionary = {
    "43556": "HELLO",
    "228": "CAT",
    "364": "DOG",
    "44": "HI",
    "7426": "PICO"
}

tap_buffer = ""

def update_ui(msg):
    # Phase C: Visual Feedback
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("SEQ: " + tap_buffer, 0, 0)
    oled.text("WORD: " + msg, 0, 30)
    oled.show()

print("--- T9 PREDICTIVE ENGINE ONLINE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Input & Prediction (Simulated Key Input)
    key = input("Digit (2-9) or # to clear: ")
    
    if key == "#":
        tap_buffer = ""
        update_ui("READY")
    elif key.isdigit():
        tap_buffer += key
        
        # Dictionary Search
        if tap_buffer in t9_dictionary:
            active_guess = t9_dictionary[tap_buffer]
        else:
            active_guess = "???"
            
        update_ui(active_guess)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Issues**: Don't try to store 10,000 words in a single dictionary. For MicroPython, stick to a "Top 200 Words" list or use a file-based search.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cycle Switch**: If two words have the same number (like "4663" for "GOOD" and "HOME"), use the '*' key to cycle through the options.

## 1. Project 1313: Advanced Keypad Security Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Security Logic Branching (Duress Codes & Silent Alarms)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-security entry system that distinguishes between a "Normal Unlock" and a "Duress Entry," triggering a silent alert to a remote monitor when a specific "Safety Code" is entered under pressure.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Duress Codes (Silent Alarms)**: A secondary PIN used in high-security environments (banks, server rooms). If entered, the door might still open to avoid panicking an attacker, but an invisible "Silent Alarm" is sent to the police or security office.
*   **Logical Branching**: Using `if/elif/else` structures to handle multiple valid but different security outcomes.
*   **UART Messaging**: Sending a text-based alert over a serial connection to simulate a remote monitoring station.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **Servo Motor** (The Door Lock)
*   **Red LED** (The Alarm Indicator - Hidden)
*   **Speaker/Buzzer** (Optional)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Output | Door Mechanism |
| **Silent Alarm LED**| GP15 | Output | Invisible Alert |
| **Keypad** | GP2-GP9 | Matrix | User Input |
| **UART TX** | GP0 | Communication| Remote Monitor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_elif_else`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_uart`
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `servo_set_angle`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`

### 7. Variables
*   **entered_pin**: String (Accumulates digits as the user types them on the keypad).
*   **MASTER_PIN**: String (The authorized bypass code that unlocks the door normally).
*   **DURESS_PIN**: String (A discrete code that unlocks the door but triggers a silent alert).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Security Configuration**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **MASTER_PIN** to `"1234"` and **DURESS_PIN** to `"9999"`.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **entered_pin** to `""`.
3.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_servo`. **Set** Pin to **16** and **Angle** to **90** (Locked position).

**Phase B: Entry Processing**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **2-9**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **entered_pin** to combine its current value and the **key** value using **Text** `concatenate`.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the **key** pressed is **"#"** (The Enter key).
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Snap** inside the enter check.
8.  **Branch 1**: If **entered_pin** equals **MASTER_PIN**, drag **Motion & Motors** `pico_servo`. **Set** Angle to **0** (Open).

**Phase C: Remote Notification**
9.  **Branch 2**: If **entered_pin** equals **DURESS_PIN**, drag **Motion & Motors** `pico_servo`. **Set** Angle to **0**.
10. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **15** (Silent Alarm) to **1 (HIGH)**.
11. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to `"ALERT: Duress Event!"`.
12. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **entered_pin** back to `""` after a **5-second** wait to re-lock the door.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the security database with two distinct codes and positions the servo to "Locked."
2.  **Process**: As keys are pressed, the Pico builds a string in RAM. When '#' is received, it performs a targeted search against the Master and Duress records.
3.  **Output**: If the Master code is entered, the lock opens. If the Duress code is entered, the lock opens AND a secret digital output is activated to alert security.
4.  **Repeat**: After a safety timeout, the Pico resets the variables and returns the servo to the home (Locked) position.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Security Configuration
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)
silent_alarm = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))

MASTER_PIN = "1234"
DURESS_PIN = "9999"
entered_pin = ""

def set_lock(open_state):
    # 0 = Open, 90 = Closed
    duty = 2500 if open_state else 5000 
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    # Phase B: Entry Processing (Simulated Key Input)
    key = input("Enter Key (0-9, #=Enter): ")
    
    if key == "#":
        # Phase C: Remote Notification
        if entered_pin == MASTER_PIN:
            print("STATUS: ACCESS GRANTED")
            set_lock(True)
            utime.sleep(5)
            set_lock(False)
        elif entered_pin == DURESS_PIN:
            print("STATUS: ACCESS GRANTED") # Display normal msg to intruder
            silent_alarm.value(1) # Secretly notify police
            # Send UART Alert to remote guard station
            uart.write("ALERT: Duress code entered at Station 1!\n")
            set_lock(True)
            utime.sleep(5)
            set_lock(False)
            silent_alarm.value(0)
        else:
            print("STATUS: ACCESS DENIED")
        
        entered_pin = "" # Clear for next user
    else:
        entered_pin += key
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Visual Leaks**: If you use a buzzer that beeps two times for Duress and one time for Safe, the intruder will know! Keep the sound/light feedback identical for both successful entries.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reversed Code**: Make the Duress PIN simply the Safe PIN in reverse (e.g., `4321`). This is easier for users to remember during a stressful event.

## 1. Project 1314: Advanced Keypad Security Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Interface Masking & Visual Privacy (Mobile PIN UX)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional "Sensitive Entry" display on an OLED that briefly shows a typed character for 1 second before "Masking" it with an asterisk (*), preventing nearby observers from stealing the PIN while still providing feedback to the user.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Shoulder Surfing Prevention**: A security technique used in ATM and Mobile UIs where characters are hidden to prevent observers from seeing private data.
*   **Temporal Masking**: The logic of delaying a transformation (changing a visible letter to a symbol) using a timer or a localized variable state.
*   **UI Refresh Logic**: Overwriting specific regions of a display to represent state changes (Active -> Masked).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display** (I2C)
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Privacy Screen |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Privacy Screen |
| **Keypad** | GP6-GP13 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | PWM | Haptic Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `replace_last_character`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_at_x_y`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`
*   From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`

### 7. Variables
*   **real_pin**: String (Store the actual unmasked digits typed by the user in Pico RAM).
*   **display_str**: String (The masked version of the PIN containing asterisks and the single visible character).
*   **last_key_time**: Milliseconds (Used to track precisely when to switch the last character from visible to masked).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Display Formatting**
1.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Snap** into `pico_start`.
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to `"ENTER PIN:"` at **X: 0, Y: 0**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **real_pin** and **display_str** to empty strings `""`.

**Phase B: Typing & Masking**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **6-13**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **real_pin** to combine current value and the **key**.
6.  From **Text**, drag `concatenate`. **Combine** a string of asterisks (length - 1) with the current **key**.
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to visual string at **X: 0, Y: 20**.
8.  From **Time**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **1 second**.
9.  From **Text**, drag `replace_last_character`. **Set** Input to visual string and Replacement to **"*"**.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to fully masked string.

**Phase C: Final Validation**
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **key** equals **"#"**.
12. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** inside the check.
13. **Check** if **real_pin** matching your target code.
14. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** result to "GRANTED" or "DENIED".
15. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **real_pin** to `""` (Zero-out memory).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes a clean OLED canvas and prepares a secure internal buffer to hold incoming PIN digits.
2.  **Process**: When you type a number, the UI instantly displays the digit so you can verify it, but the system logs the "Real" value in the background.
3.  **Output**: After precisely one second, the mask logic overwrites the onscreen digit with an asterisk, ensuring "Shoulder Surfers" cannot steal the code.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the sequence is submitted, the Pico validates the code, displays the result, and immediately wipes the RAM buffer to prevent data theft.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Display Formatting
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

PIN_TARGET = "1234"
real_pin = ""

def update_oled(text):
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("ENTER PIN:", 0, 0)
    oled.text(text, 0, 20)
    oled.show()

while len(real_pin) < 4:
    # Phase B: Typing & Masking (Simulated)
    key = input("Key: ")
    real_pin += key
    
    # Temporarily show last character
    masked_view = "*" * (len(real_pin) - 1)
    update_oled(masked_view + key)
    
    # 1-second visual window
    utime.sleep(1)
    
    # Mask character
    update_oled("*" * len(real_pin))

# Phase C: Final Validation
if real_pin == PIN_TARGET:
    update_oled("ACCESS GRANTED")
else:
    update_oled("ACCESS DENIED")

# Clear sensitive data from RAM
real_pin = ""
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Double Append"**: If you append to the string *after* the wait, the user will experience a massive lag. Always append the "Real" data instantly and only wait for the "Visual" change.
*   **Clearance**: Ensure you clear the `real_pin` from RAM after use so it cannot be "Dumped" by a hacker later.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Shake-to-Clear**: Add an Accelereometer (MPU6050). If the Pico is shaken, clear the buffer immediately—perfect for when you think someone is looking!

---

## 1. Project 1315: Automated Advanced Keypad Security Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Forensic Security Logging & Serial Diagnostics". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a multi-user entry controller that records a timestamped history (logs) of all access attempts and provides an "Administrator Mode" to dump this data to a computer over UART for security audits.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Audit Trail (Forensic Logging)**: The practice of recording every security event (who, when, result) to allow for investigation after a break-in or unauthorized entry.
*   **Memory Management (Circular Buffers)**: Storing a finite number of events in a list and overwriting the oldest data once the list is full, preventing the Pico's RAM from overflowing.
*   **Serial Data Streaming**: Using UART to "Dump" structured text data from a microcontroller's memory to a PC terminal (like Thonny).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **USB Cable** (For UART Serial communication)
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico USB** | PC | UART | Data Dump Port |
| **Keypad** | GP2-GP9 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Status Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `add_to_list`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_variable_equals`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_uart`
*   From **Time**, drag `get_timestamp`

### 7. Variables
*   **access_log**: List (A sequence of text strings representing timestamped security events).
*   **ADMIN_CODE**: String (A privileged PIN used to trigger the transmission of log data to the computer).
*   **user_input**: String (The temporary digits currently held in RAM before submission).
*   **user_db**: Dictionary (A lookup table mapping authorized PINs to user names).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Database & Admin Setup**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **ADMIN_CODE** to `"1111"`.
2.  From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list`. **Set** Variable to **access_log**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** mappings like `"1234": "Alice"` and `"5678": "Bob"`. **Snap** into **user_db**.

**Phase B: Event Logging**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **6-13**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **key** equals **"#"**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Snap** inside the submission check.
7.  **Branch 1 (Authorized)**: If **user_input** is in **user_db**, from **Lists**, drag `add_to_list`. **Combine** "SUCCESS" and **user_input**.
8.  **Branch 2 (Denied)**: Else, from **Lists**, drag `add_to_list`. **Combine** "DENIED" and **user_input**.
9.  From **Lists**, drag `remove_item_at`. **Set** index to **0** if list length exceeds **20**.

**Phase C: Serial Data Streaming**
10. **Branch 3 (Admin)**: If **user_input** equals **ADMIN_CODE**, from **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** value to `"--- LOG DUMP START ---"`.
11. From **Loops**, drag `iterate_over_list`. **Pass** **access_log** into the loop.
12. From **Text**, drag `pico_print_uart`. **Snap** the current list item into it.
13. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **user_input** to `""` (Clear buffer).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes an internal list for tracking events and a dictionary for identifying authorized users.
2.  **Process**: When a PIN is entered, the system identifies the user and creates a text record containing the result and the code used.
3.  **Output**: If an administrator enters the secret `ADMIN_CODE`, the Pico interrupts normal operation to stream the entire history to the PC.
4.  **Repeat**: The script maintains a "Circular Buffer," deleting the oldest logs as new ones arrive to ensure it never runs out of memory.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Database & Admin Setup
access_log = []
ADMIN_CODE = "1111"
user_db = {"1234": "Alice", "5678": "Bob"}
user_input = ""

def log_event(status, code):
    # Phase B: Event Logging
    timestamp = utime.ticks_ms()
    record = f"T:{timestamp} | PIN:{code} | {status}"
    access_log.append(record)
    
    # Manage RAM (Keep last 20 events)
    if len(access_log) > 20:
        access_log.pop(0)

while True:
    # (Keypad Scanning Logic Simulated)
    key = input("PIN (or '#' to enter): ")
    
    if key == "#":
        if user_input == ADMIN_CODE:
            # Phase C: Serial Data Streaming
            print("\n--- FORENSIC LOG DUMP ---")
            for entry in access_log:
                print(entry)
            print("--- END OF DUMP ---\n")
        elif user_input in user_db:
            name = user_db[user_input]
            print(f"ACCESS GRANTED: {name}")
            log_event("SUCCESS", user_input)
        else:
            print("ACCESS DENIED")
            log_event("FAILURE", user_input)
            
        user_input = ""
    else:
        user_input += key
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **RAM Exhaustion**: Pico only has ~264KB of RAM. If your log gets too big (e.g., thousands of entries), the Pico will crash. Always use `pop(0)` or save to a file for large logs.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Unauthorized Attempt Logging**: Log whenever an *Invalid* PIN is entered (e.g., "ATTACKER @ 50 mins").
