## 1. Project 1316: Advanced Keypad Security Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Hammering Protection (Security Lockout Logic)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Anti-Hammering" safety trigger that detects brute-force attacks and automatically disables the system for a specified "Cool-down" period after 3 consecutive failed attempts.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Brute-Force Attack**: A method where an attacker tries every possible PIN combination. "Anti-Hammering" makes this impossible by adding massive time delays between failures.
*   **Persistent Failure Counters**: Using a variable to track consecutive errors that only resets to zero after a *successful* entry.
*   **Countdown Timers**: Displaying a live, decreasing time value to the user to indicate when the system will be safe to use again.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Buzzer** (Alarm Tone)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP4, GP5) | UI | Status Display |
| **Keypad** | GP6-GP13 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Warning Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `addition`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_1s`
*   From **Loops**, drag `while_loop`

### 7. Variables
*   **fail_count**: Integer (Counts consecutive incorrect PIN entries to detect brute-force attempts).
*   **lock_timer**: Seconds (The remaining duration of the security lockout period).
*   **is_locked**: Boolean (A flag that disables keypad input while the lockout is active).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Failure Monitoring**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **fail_count** to **0**.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_locked** to **FALSE**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **entered_pin** is **NOT** equal to **Secret_PIN**.
4.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **+**. **Snap** **fail_count** and **1** into it.

**Phase B: Lockout Trigger**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **fail_count** is **greater than or equal to 3**.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_locked** to **TRUE**.
7.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **14** (Buzzer) to **1** for **1 second**.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **lock_timer** to **60**.

**Phase C: Countdown Recovery**
9.  From **Loops**, drag `while_loop`. **Check** if **lock_timer** is **greater than 0**.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to combine `"LOCKED: "` and **lock_timer**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **1 second**.
12. From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract 1** from **lock_timer**.
13. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_locked** to **FALSE** once the loop completes.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the failure counter and stands ready for a secure PIN entry.
2.  **Process**: If a user enters an incorrect PIN three times in a row, the logic identifies a potential brute-force attack and raises the `is_locked` flag.
3.  **Output**: The buzzer screams a warning, and the OLED screen displays a mandatory 60-second countdown, blocking all further inputs.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the penalty timer reaches zero, the Pico resets the failure history and re-enables the keypad for another attempt.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Setup Environment
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

fail_count = 0
is_locked = False
lock_time_limit = 60
SECRET_PIN = "1234"

def trigger_lockout():
    global is_locked, fail_count
    # Phase B: Lockout Trigger
    is_locked = True
    buzzer.value(1)
    utime.sleep(1)
    buzzer.value(0)
    
    # Phase C: Countdown Recovery
    for remaining in range(lock_time_limit, 0, -1):
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text("SYSTEM LOCKED", 10, 10)
        oled.text(f"Wait: {remaining}s", 10, 30)
        oled.show()
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    is_locked = False
    fail_count = 0
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("System Ready", 10, 10)
    oled.show()

def process_pin(entered):
    global fail_count
    # Phase A: Failure Monitoring
    if is_locked:
        return

    if entered == SECRET_PIN:
        print("ACCESS GRANTED.")
        fail_count = 0
    else:
        fail_count += 1
        print(f"ACCESS DENIED. Fails: {fail_count}/3")
        
        if fail_count >= 3:
            trigger_lockout()

# Simulation
process_pin("0000")
process_pin("0000")
process_pin("0000")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Volatile Reset"**: If you reset the `fail_count` even when the system is locked, a hacker could just wait 1 second and try again. The counter must stay high until the *full* penalty time has passed.
*   **No Feedback**: Locking the system without telling the user "Why" makes them think the Pico is broken. Always show a countdown on the OLED.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Exponential Penalty**: Update the code so the first lockout is 30 seconds, the second is 5 minutes, and the third is 1 hour! This is how modern iPhones protect data.
        # 4. Trigger Backoff
        if fail_count >= 3:
            penalty = 5 * (2**(fail_count - 3))
            lock_until = now + penalty
            print(f"BRUTE FORCE DETECTED. Blocking for {penalty}s")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Time Reset**: If you use `utime.sleep()` for the lockout, the Pico cannot do anything else (like blink a status light). Using a `lock_until` timestamp is better for "Non-blocking" security.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Persistent Penalty**: Save the `failure_count` to the Pico's flash memory so that even if the attacker reboots the Pico, the lockout timer remains active!

## 1. Project 1317: Advanced Keypad Security Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Attendance Tracking (Time Clock Systems)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional "Clock-In / Clock-Out" system using a keypad and RTC, where the Pico maintains a database of active user sessions and automatically calculates shift durations in seconds or hours.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Session Management**: Using an internal dictionary to track which users are currently "Active" (Checked-in) versus which ones are "Clocked Out."
*   **Time Delta Calculation**: Calculating the difference between two timestamps (`Current Time - Start Time`) to measure the exact length of a work session.
*   **Tick-Based Precision**: Using `utime.ticks_ms()` to measure time intervals with millisecond accuracy, even without a persistent real-time clock.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP0, GP1) | UI | Status Feedback |
| **Keypad** | GP2-GP9 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Confirmation Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_key_in_dictionary`
*   From **Wait**, drag `ticks_diff`
*   From **Math**, drag `division` (to convert ms to seconds)
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_scrolling`

### 7. Variables
*   **active_shifts**: Dictionary (A lookup table where keys are Employee IDs and values are their initial clock-in timestamps).
*   **employee_id**: String (The 4-digit numeric code captured from the physical keypad).
*   **duration**: Integer (The total number of seconds calculated between a clock-in and a clock-out event).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Session Initialization**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **active_shifts**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** to an empty dictionary `{}`. **Snap** into `pico_start`.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **employee_id** to `""`.

**Phase B: Check-In Monitoring**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **2-9**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **employee_id** to combine current value and the **key**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **length of employee_id** equals **4**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** inside the length check.
8.  **Check** if **employee_id** is **NOT** in **active_shifts**.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `update_dictionary`. **Set** Key to **employee_id** and Value to **Ticks (ms)**.

**Phase C: Duration Processing**
10. **In the ELSE slot (Clocking Out)**: From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** the stored dictionary value from the current **Ticks (ms)**.
11. From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **/**. **Divide** result by **1000** to find **duration** in seconds.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to combine `"SHIFT: "` and **duration**.
13. From **Logic**, drag `remove_key_from_dictionary`. **Set** Key to **employee_id**.
14. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **employee_id** back to `""`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes an internal attendance register and stands ready to capture employee identification numbers.
2.  **Process**: When an ID is entered, the system checks if that specific user is already on the clock. If not, it marks their entry time in RAM.
3.  **Output**: If the user is already active, the Pico calculates the time difference, converts the milliseconds into readable seconds, and displays the shift total on the OLED.
4.  **Repeat**: After every transaction, the Pico resets the ID buffer and prepares for the next employee check-in.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Session Initialization
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

active_shifts = {}
employee_id = ""

def process_attendance(emp_id):
    # Phase B: Check-In Monitoring
    global active_shifts
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    if emp_id not in active_shifts:
        # User is checking in
        active_shifts[emp_id] = now
        print(f"[{emp_id}] Clocked IN")
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text(f"ID: {emp_id}", 0, 0)
        oled.text("SHIFT STARTED", 0, 20)
        oled.show()
    else:
        # Phase C: Duration Processing
        # User is checking out
        start_time = active_shifts[emp_id]
        diff_ms = utime.ticks_diff(now, start_time)
        seconds = diff_ms // 1000
        
        print(f"[{emp_id}] Clocked OUT. Time: {seconds}s")
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text(f"ID: {emp_id}", 0, 0)
        oled.text(f"TIME: {seconds}s", 0, 20)
        oled.show()
        
        # Remove from active register
        del active_shifts[emp_id]

# Simulated Loop
while True:
    # (Assume keypad scanning logic provides ID)
    test_id = input("Enter 4-digit ID: ")
    if len(test_id) == 4:
        process_attendance(test_id)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Power Loss" Wipe**: Because dictionaries are stored in RAM, if the Pico loses power, every employee is "deleted" from the shift list. In a real-world scenario, you should save `active_shifts` to a CSV file on the Pico's flash storage.
*   **Duplicate Entries**: If an employee double-taps their ID by accident, the Pico will "Clock them out" 1 second after they clocked in! Add a "Minimum Session" check (e.g., must be 1 minute apart) to prevent accidental check-outs.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **CSV Reporting**: Every time someone clocks out, use the `file_write` block to append their ID and Duration to a file named `attendance.csv` for later analysis in Excel!

## 1. Project 1318: Smart Advanced Keypad Security System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Challenge-Response Authentication (2FA Systems)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a two-factor authentication (2FA) mechanism where the Pico generates a random "Challenge" code on the OLED, and the user must enter a corresponding "Response" based on a secret lookup table.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Challenge-Response**: A security protocol where one party presents a question ("Challenge") and the other must provide the valid answer ("Response") to prove their identity.
*   **Random Seed Generation**: Using `machine.rng()` or `urandom` to ensure that every security challenge is different and unpredictable.
*   **Static Lookup Tables**: Pre-defining a set of pairs (e.g., Challenge "A" maps to Response "5") to simulate a security token or "Code Card."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Challenge Screen |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Challenge Screen |
| **Keypad** | GP6-GP13 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Sound Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `random_integer`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_get_char_at`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_dictionary_contains`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_huge_text`

### 7. Variables
*   **access_codes**: Dictionary (A secure lookup table mapping unique numeric "challenges" to their specific "responses").
*   **active_challenge**: String (The current random code displayed on the OLED that the user must answer).
*   **user_answer**: String (The text string built from sequential keypad presses provided by the user).
*   **challenges_list**: List (A helper sequence derived from dictionary keys to allow for easy random indexing).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Security Table Setup**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** Variable to **access_codes**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag the dictionary block. **Set** entries like `"111": "999"` and `"222": "888"`. **Snap** into the variable slot.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **user_answer** to `""`.

**Phase B: Challenge Generation**
4.  From **Math**, drag `random_integer`. **Set** Range from **0** to **length of access_codes**.
5.  From **Lists**, drag `get_item_at`. **Set** List to **keys of access_codes** and index to the random value.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **active_challenge** to the selected key.
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to **active_challenge** in a large font size.

**Phase C: Response Verification**
8.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **2-9**.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **user_answer** to combine current value and the **key**.
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **length of user_answer** is **3**.
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** inside the check.
12. **Check** if **user_answer** equals **access_codes[active_challenge]**.
13. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** msg to "SUCCESS" or "FAILURE".
14. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **user_answer** to `""` and **repeat** Phase B to cycle the challenge.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico loads a static "Code Card" dictionary into memory and converts its keys into a list for random access.
2.  **Process**: The system picks a random entry and displays it as a "Challenge" on the screen, forcing the user to provide the cryptographic partner.
3.  **Output**: Upon receiving a 3-digit response, the system performs a lookup. If the answer matches the dictionary value, it grants access.
4.  **Repeat**: Regardless of the outcome, the system instantly cycles to a new challenge, ensuring a used and observed PIN cannot be reused.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import urandom
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Security Table Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

access_codes = {
    "111": "999",
    "222": "888",
    "333": "777",
    "444": "666"
}

challenges_list = list(access_codes.keys())
active_challenge = ""
user_answer = ""

def generate_new_challenge():
    # Phase B: Challenge Generation
    global active_challenge
    idx = urandom.getrandbits(8) % len(challenges_list)
    active_challenge = challenges_list[idx]
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("CHALLENGE:", 0, 0)
    oled.text(active_challenge, 0, 30)
    oled.show()
    print(f"OLED: {active_challenge}")

def verify_response(answer):
    # Phase C: Response Verification
    global user_answer
    correct = access_codes[active_challenge]
    
    if answer == correct:
        print("RESULT: SUCCESS")
        oled.text("ACCESS GRANTED", 0, 50)
    else:
        print("RESULT: FAILURE")
        oled.text("ACCESS DENIED", 0, 50)
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(1)
    
    user_answer = ""
    generate_new_challenge()

# Initial Cycle
generate_new_challenge()

# Simulation Loop
while True:
    key = input("3-Digit Response: ")
    if len(key) == 3:
        verify_response(key)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Predictable Randomness**: If you don't "Seed" the random number generator (using a floating ADC pin), the Pico might show the exact same challenges in the same order every time you turn it on.
*   **OLED Burn-in**: Static text on an OLED can cause permanent damage. Make sure the challenge "vibrates" or moves slightly on the screen every few seconds.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Time-Sensitive Tokens**: Add an RTC. Make the response depend on the Challenge + the Current Minute (e.g., If Challenge is 100 and Minute is 05, the answer is 105). This is how RSA key fobs work!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Remote Unlock**: Allow the Pico to *Receive* a message from the cloud to open the door (e.g., if you want to let a friend in while you are away).

## 1. Project 1319: Advanced Keypad Security Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Matrix Signal Diagnostics & Ghost Key Detection". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a hardware diagnostic tool that identifies "Ghosting"—a phenomenon in matrix keypads where multiple simultaneous keypresses create phantom signals—and use this data to determine if your hardware requires protective diodes.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Keypad Ghosting**: Understanding how electricity "loops" through a grid of switches. If three corners of a rectangle are pressed, the fourth corner (the "Ghost") may appear to be pressed even if it isn't.
*   **Multi-Scan Logic**: Designing a diagnostic loop that independently checks every row/column intersection to map out the exact state of the physical matrix.
*   **Hardware Limitations**: Learning why professional gaming keyboards and high-security keypads use "Diodes" at every switch to prevent back-flow current.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad** (Standard membrane or mechanical)
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Breadboard & Jumper Wires**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP0, GP1) | UI | Diagnostic Map |
| **Keypad Rows** | GP2 - GP5 | Input | Pulse Listeners |
| **Keypad Cols** | GP6 - GP9 | Output | Pulse Drivers |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_variable_in_list`
*   From **Lists**, drag `append_to_list`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_rectangle` (for the grid map)

### 7. Variables
*   **pressed_list**: List (A sequence of coordinate pairs representing all active intersections in the matrix).
*   **ghost_warning**: Boolean (A flag that triggers when the signal pattern indicates a phantom keypress).
*   **active_row**: Integer (The specific row identifier currently being used as a voltage source/sink during the scan).
*   **n_key_limit**: Integer (The maximum number of simultaneous keys handled before ghosting occurs).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Matrix Mapping**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pins **6, 7, 8, 9** (Columns) as outputs.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pins **2, 3, 4, 5** (Rows) as inputs with **Internal Pull-Up** enabled.
3.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
4.  From **Loops**, drag `iterate_over_list`. **Set** range to **Column Pins**.
5.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_value`. **Set** current Column to **0 (LOW)**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Read** all Row values to identify intersections.

**Phase B: Ghosting Recognition**
7.  From **Lists**, drag `add_to_list`. **Append** the (Row, Col) ID to **pressed_list** if the signal is LOW.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **length of pressed_list** is greater than **3**.
9.  From **Logic**, drag a custom check block. **Check** if the active points form a rectangle grid (e.g., Row 1/Col 1 and Row 2/Col 2).
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **ghost_warning** to **TRUE** if a rectangle is detected without 4 physical fingers used.

**Phase C: Stress Test**
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Snap** inside the loop.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_rectangle`. **Draw** a box for every coordinate in **pressed_list**.
13. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** text to `"GHOST DETECTED"` if **ghost_warning** is TRUE.
14. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **100ms** to stabilize the visual output.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico configures the matrix as a grid of intersections, setting row pins as receivers and column pins as drivers.
2.  **Process**: The system pulses each column one-by-one. If electricity loops back through multiple pressed keys, it identifies the "Ghost" where a return signal shouldn't exist.
3.  **Output**: The OLED displays a real-time topographic map of the keypad. Physical presses show as solid boxes, while potential ghosts trigger a flashing warning.
4.  **Repeat**: The tool continuously updates, allowing the user to press multiple button combinations to test the "N-Key Rollover" hardware limits.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Matrix Mapping
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

rows = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP) for i in range(2, 6)]
cols = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.OUT) for i in range(6, 10)]

def scan_keys():
    pressed = []
    for c_idx, c_pin in enumerate(cols):
        c_pin.value(0) # Logic Sink
        for r_idx, r_pin in enumerate(rows):
            if r_pin.value() == 0:
                pressed.append((r_idx, c_idx))
        c_pin.value(1) # Deactivate
    return pressed

while True:
    pressed_list = scan_keys()
    oled.fill(0)
    
    # Phase C: Stress Test Visuals
    for r, c in pressed_list:
        oled.fill_rect(c*30, r*15, 25, 12, 1)
    
    # Phase B: Ghosting Recognition Logic
    if len(pressed_list) >= 4:
        # Simplified Ghost Logic: If 4 points form a 2x2 grid
        row_set = len(set([p[0] for p in pressed_list]))
        col_set = len(set([p[1] for p in pressed_list]))
        if row_set == 2 and col_set == 2:
            oled.text("GHOST!", 80, 0)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```total_time = utime.ticks_diff(end, start)
print(f"Sample Period: {total_time} microseconds")

# Count transitions
flips = 0
for i in range(len(samples)-1):
    if samples[i] != samples[i+1]:
        flips += 1

print(f"Electrical Spikes Detected: {flips}")
print(f"Recommended Debounce: {total_time // 1000} ms")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sampling Speed**: Python is slower than C. A `for` loop in MicroPython might miss the fastest bounces (which happen in < 1us). This tool focuses on "Macro-bounces" common in cheap membrane keypads.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Health**: Plot the number of "Flips" for different buttons. Usually, 'Enter' and 'Space' buttons wear out first and get noisier!

## 1. Project 1320: Advanced Advanced Keypad Security Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Mathematical Parsing & Floating-Point Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to transform a simple security keypad into a functional Scientific Calculator, capable of parsing multi-digit floating-point numbers and executing basic arithmetic operations (+, -, *, /) with decimal precision.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Tokenization**: The process of taking a long string of inputs (e.g., `1`, `2`, `.`, `5`) and grouping them into a single mathematical "Token" (the float `12.5`).
*   **Operator Handlers**: Using variables to store a pending operation (e.g., "Add") while the user types the second number.
*   **LCD Character Mapping**: Re-purposing keypad symbols (like `*` and `#`) to represent mathematical symbols (like `.` and `=`) on a screen.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x4 Matrix Keypad**
*   **16x2 LCD Display** (I2C)
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Output |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Output |
| **Keypad** | GP6-GP13 | Matrix | User Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Button Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Math**, drag `pico_parse_float`
*   **From Logic**, drag `switch_case` (for operations)
*   **From Text**, drag `concatenate`
*   **From Displays**, drag `lcd_clear`

### 7. Variables
*   **current_val**: String (Builds the numeric sequence being typed, including decimals, e.g., "12.5").
*   **first_operand**: Float (Stores the first number of the equation after an operator is pressed).
*   **last_op**: String (The mathematical operator chosen by the user, such as "+", "-", "*", or "/").
*   **result**: Float (The final calculated solution displayed after the equals command).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Input Mapping**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **current_val** to an empty string `""`.
2.  From **Loops**, drag `on_keypad_press`. **Set** Pin range to **6-13**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `switch_case`. **Check** the value of **key**.
4.  **Case (0-9)**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Append** the **key** to **current_val**.
5.  **Case (*)**: **Append** a decimal point `.` to **current_val** only if it doesn't already contain one.

**Phase B: Storage & Execution**
6.  **Case (A/B/C/D)**: From **Math**, drag `pico_parse_float`. **Convert** **current_val** and save to **first_operand**.
7.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **last_op** to the operator sign (e.g., `+`).
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **current_val** back to `""` for the next number.
9.  **Case (#)**: From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Run** the calculation using **first_operand**, **last_op**, and the current float value of **current_val**.

**Phase C: Visual Output**
10. From **Displays**, drag `lcd_clear`. **Snap** into the equals logic.
11. From **Displays**, drag `lcd_print`. **Set** Line 1 to show the full equation (e.g., `1.5 + 2`).
12. From **Displays**, drag `lcd_print`. **Set** Line 2 to show the **result**.
13. From **Wait**, drag `pico_beep`. **Set** to **100ms** for success feedback.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes a parsing engine that treats the keypad as a mathematical input device rather than a security pin pad.
2.  **Process**: As the user types, the system builds a string that it converts into high-precision Floating Point numbers when an operator is selected.
3.  **Output**: Upon pressing the "Equals" button, the Pico executes the pending math operation and formats the result for the 16x2 LCD display.
4.  **Repeat**: The script clears the internal registers after the result is shown, allowing the user to start a new calculation immediately.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from i2c_lcd import I2cLcd

# Phase A: Input Mapping Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
lcd = I2cLcd(i2c, 0x27, 2, 16)

current_val = ""
first_operand = 0.0
last_op = ""

def solve():
    global current_val, first_operand, last_op
    # Phase B: Storage & Execution
    try:
        second_num = float(current_val)
        if last_op == "+":
            res = first_operand + second_num
        elif last_op == "-":
            res = first_operand - second_num
        elif last_op == "*":
            res = first_operand * second_num
        elif last_op == "/":
            res = first_operand / second_num if second_num != 0 else "ERROR"
        
        # Phase C: Visual Output
        lcd.clear()
        lcd.putstr(f"{first_operand}{last_op}{second_num}")
        lcd.move_to(0, 1)
        lcd.putstr(f"ANS: {res}")
        current_val = str(res)
    except:
        lcd.putstr("PARSE ERROR")
    last_op = ""

while True:
    # (Keypad scanning simulation)
    key = input("Key (A=+, B=-, C=*, D=/, *=., #==): ")
    
    if key in "0123456789":
        current_val += key
        lcd.clear()
        lcd.putstr(current_val)
    elif key == "*":
        if "." not in current_val:
            current_val += "."
    elif key in "ABCD":
        first_operand = float(current_val)
        last_op = {"A":"+", "B":"-", "C":"*", "D":"/"}[key]
        current_val = ""
    elif key == "#":
        solve()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Dividing by Zero**: If the user enters `5 / 0`, the Pico will crash! Always add an `if` statement to check if the second number is zero before dividing.
*   **Integer vs Float**: If you don't use `float()`, the Pico will treat `1.5` as a string and the addition will fail.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Memory Function**: Use a separate button to "Store" a result in a variable called `memory`, and another to "Recall" it later for a second calculation.

## Batch 133: Advanced Joystick Control 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1321: Advanced Joystick Control Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Software-Defined Zeroing (Adaptive Calibration)". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a self-correcting analog interface that automatically identifies hardware "Drift"—the phenomenon where a joystick's rest position isn't perfectly centered (2048/32768)—and mathematically "Nulls" that offset on startup for professional precision.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Mechanical Tolerance**: Understanding that mass-produced potentiometers vary in resistance, meaning two joysticks rarely output the exact same "Center" voltage.
*   **Baseline Calibration (Taring)**: A data processing step performed during a "Quiet State" (idle) to establish what "Zero" actually looks like on the current hardware.
*   **Normalized Coordinates**: Converting raw 16-bit ADC values (0-65535) into a more usable range (like -100 to +100) where 0 is always the absolute center.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick** (vcc, gnd, vrx, vry, sw)
*   **Internal ADC** (Pico GP26/GP27)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick VCC** | 3.3V(OUT)| Power | Steady 3.3V |
| **Joystick GND** | GND | Power | Common Ground |
| **Joystick VRX** | GP26 | Analog (ADC0) | X-Axis Input |
| **Joystick VRY** | GP27 | Analog (ADC1) | Y-Axis Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print`

### 7. Variables
*   **baseline_x**: Integer (The resting voltage level for the X-axis captured during the boot sequence).
*   **baseline_y**: Integer (The resting voltage level for the Y-axis captured during the boot sequence).
*   **raw_reading**: Integer (The immediate, unscaled ADC value read directly from the joystick pins).
*   **calibrated_x**: Integer (The final coordinate value after the baseline offset and deadzone filters are applied).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Boot Stabilization**
1.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **1000ms** to allow the power rails to settle and the user to release the hardware.
2.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** and **Set** **baseline_x** to this value.
3.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **27** and **Set** **baseline_y** to this value.
4.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to confirm calibration completion.

**Phase B: Real-Time Correction**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
6.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** into **raw_reading**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** **baseline_x** from **raw_reading** and **Set** to **calibrated_x**.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **calibrated_x** is between **-500** and **500**.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **calibrated_x** to **0** (Deadzone filtering).

**Phase C: Precision Reporting**
10. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to display **calibrated_x**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **50ms** for smooth serial feedback.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico pauses for 1 second on boot to ensure the joystick is at rest, then captures the current ADC values as the "Zero" reference baseline.
2.  **Process**: In a continuous loop, the Pico performs a mathematical subtraction to remove the baseline drift from the raw hardware signal.
3.  **Output**: Small fluctuations (noise) are filtered out via a +/- 500 unit deadzone, providing a stable "Zero" when the stick is untouched.
4.  **Repeat**: The script updates 20 times per second, reporting precise movement offsets relative to the initial power-on position.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Boot Stabilization
joy_x = machine.ADC(26)
joy_y = machine.ADC(27)

print("--- ADAPTIVE CALIBRATION ACTIVATED ---")
print("Stabilizing...")
utime.sleep(1) 

baseline_x = joy_x.read_u16()
baseline_y = joy_y.read_u16()
print(f"Zeroes set at: X={baseline_x}, Y={baseline_y}")

while True:
    # Phase B: Real-Time Correction
    raw_x = joy_x.read_u16()
    raw_y = joy_y.read_u16()
    
    cal_x = raw_x - baseline_x
    cal_y = raw_y - baseline_y
    
    # Deadzone Filter
    if -500 < cal_x < 500: cal_x = 0
    if -500 < cal_y < 500: cal_y = 0
        
    # Phase C: Precision Reporting
    print(f"JOYSTICK CALIBRATED: X={cal_x} | Y={cal_y}")
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Holding During Boot**: If the user is holding the joystick "Forward" when the Pico turns on, that forward position becomes the new "Zero." This will make the stick unusable until a reboot.
*   **Unsigned Error**: Ensure your logic can handle negative numbers (`cal_x` will go negative when you pull the stick backwards).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Average Calibration**: Instead of taking 1 reading for the baseline, take 100 readings in a fast loop and average them. This creates a much more stable "Zero" point.

---

## 1. Project 1322: Advanced Joystick Control Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Polar Vector Transformation (Speed & Heading)". At the end of this project, you will be able to convert raw X,Y coordinates into 'Polar Coordinates' (Magnitude and Angle), allowing you to drive a robot or game character with a specific "Speed" and "Direction" (0-359 degrees).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Cartesian vs. Polar**: Moving from a grid system (X,Y) to a vector system (Strength, Direction).
*   **Trigonometry (Atan2)**: Using the `math.atan2` function to find the angle of the stick even when X is zero.
*   **Normalization**: Scaling values from 0-65535 to a unified -1.0 to +1.0 float range.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Joystick**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP0, GP1) | Feedback Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`sqrt`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`atan2`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`to_degrees`**

### 7. Variables
*   **norm_x**: Float (The normalized X-axis position scaled between -1.0 and 1.0).
*   **norm_y**: Float (The normalized Y-axis position scaled between -1.0 and 1.0).
*   **mag_strength**: Float (The mathematical 'Magnitude' representing how far the stick is pushed from center).
*   **angle_degrees**: Float (The 'Heading' or direction of the joystick expressed in 0-359 degrees).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Value Normalization**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** (X) and **Set** to **norm_x**.
2.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** **32768** from **norm_x**.
3.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **/**. **Divide** by **32768** to produce a value between **-1.0 and 1.0**.
4.  **Repeat** the mapping process for Pin **27** (Y) to generate **norm_y**.

**Phase B: Vector Calculation**
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **^2**. **Square** both **norm_x** and **norm_y**.
6.  From **Math**, drag `sqrt`. **Calculate** the square root of the sum and **Set** to **mag_strength**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **mag_strength** is greater than **1.0**. **Set** to **1.0** (Circular Clamping).

**Phase C: Coordinate Translation**
8.  From **Math**, drag `atan2`. **Pass** **norm_y** and **norm_x** into the function to find the angle in radians.
9.  From **Math**, drag `to_degrees`. **Convert** the result and **Set** to **angle_degrees**.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 1 to **mag_strength** and Line 2 to **angle_degrees**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico reads the raw analog voltages from the joystick and shifts the range so that the mechanical center point is treated as 0.0.
2.  **Process**: The system applies the Pythagorean theorem to find the straight-line distance (Magnitude) and uses trigonometry (atan2) to find the rotation (Angle).
3.  **Output**: The calculated vector data is formatted into "Throttle Percentage" and "Compass Heading" for the OLED display.
4.  **Repeat**: The script loops continuously, transforming physical motion into complex navigational data for robots or game engines.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import math
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Value Normalization Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)

def get_polar_vector():
    # Normalize inputs to float range -1.0 to 1.0
    nx = (adc_x.read_u16() - 32768) / 32768
    ny = (adc_y.read_u16() - 32768) / 32768
    
    # Phase B: Vector Calculation
    # Calculate Magnitude (Distance from zero)
    mag_strength = math.sqrt(nx**2 + ny**2)
    if mag_strength > 1.0: mag_strength = 1.0 # Circular Clamp
    
    # Phase C: Coordinate Translation
    # Calculate Heading in degrees
    angle_rad = math.atan2(ny, nx)
    angle_degrees = math.degrees(angle_rad)
    if angle_degrees < 0: angle_degrees += 360 # Convert to 0-359 range
    
    return mag_strength, angle_degrees

while True:
    speed, heading = get_polar_vector()
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("POLAR NAVIGATION", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"THR: {int(speed * 100)}%", 0, 20)
    oled.text(f"DIR: {int(heading)} DEG", 0, 40)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Circle vs Square**: A square joystick reaches `1.0, 1.0` in corners. Magnitude at corners is `sqrt(1^2 + 1^2) = 1.41`. Always "Clamp" your magnitude to 1.0 to prevent robot speed errors.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Vector Arrow**: Use the `oled.line` function to draw an actual arrow on the screen that points in the direction of the stick.

---

## 1. Project 1323: Advanced Joystick Control Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Gesture Recognition & Pattern Matching (Intelligent UI)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Gesture-Aware" interface that recognizes complex joystick movements like "Double-Tap Up" or "Full Circle Clockwise" and uses them to trigger specific software macros, effectively turning a 2-axis joystick into a multi-function command center.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Temporal Pattern Analysis**: Tracking the *Sequence* of events over time (e.g., Up, then Center, then Up again) to distinguish between a simple movement and a intended "Double Tap."
*   **Vector Quadrant Mapping**: Dividing the 360-degree range of the joystick into 4 zones (Up, Down, Left, Right) to simplify gesture detection logic.
*   **Window of Opportunity**: Defining a "Timeout" period (e.g., 500ms) within which a sequence must be completed to be recognized as a valid gesture.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Buzzer** (Confirmation sound)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | X-Axis Input |
| **Joystick VRY**| GP27 | ADC1 | Y-Axis Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Visual Feedback |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Visual Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `ticks_ms`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_and`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_oled`
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`

### 7. Variables
*   **gesture_state**: Integer (A numeric stage counter tracking the progress of the double-tap pattern: 0=Idle, 1=First Flick, 2=Released).
*   **last_event_time**: Timestamp (The CPU tick count recorded during the previous movement, used to calculate timing gaps).
*   **current_dir**: String (The current physical direction of the stick, such as 'UP', 'DOWN', or 'CENTER').
*   **MAX_GAP**: Milliseconds (The maximum allowable time between movements before the gesture pattern resets).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Quadrant Mapping**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **27** (Y) and **Set** to **raw_y**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** **raw_y** against the calibrated baseline.
3.  **Check** if **raw_y** is greater than **baseline_y** + **20000**. **Set** **current_dir** to **"UP"**.
4.  **Check** if **absolute_difference** is less than **2000**. **Set** **current_dir** to **"CENTER"**.

**Phase B: Temporal Pattern Analysis**
5.  From **Wait**, drag `ticks_ms`. **Store** current time in **now**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **ticks_diff** between **now** and **last_event_time** is greater than **MAX_GAP**.
7.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **gesture_state** to **0** (Timeout reset).
8.  From **Logic**, drag `switch_case`. **Check** **gesture_state**.
9.  **Case 0**: If **current_dir** equals **"UP"**, **Set** state to **1** and **Mark** **last_event_time**.
10. **Case 1**: If **current_dir** equals **"CENTER"**, **Set** state to **2**.

**Phase C: Command Execution**
11. **Case 2**: If **current_dir** equals **"UP"**, **Trigger** the macro logic.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** message to `"MACRO: JUMP TRIGGERED"`.
13. From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_beep`. **Set** frequency to **2000Hz** for **50ms**.
14. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **gesture_state** to **0**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico continuously monitors the joystick's vertical axis, translating raw voltages into logical directions (UP, CENTER, DOWN).
2.  **Process**: The system tracks the sequence of movements. If it sees an "UP" flicker followed by a "CENTER" release within 500ms, it stands ready for the final confirmation.
3.  **Output**: Upon detecting the second "UP" movement within the time window, the logic validates the "Double-Tap UP" gesture and executes a virtual macro.
4.  **Repeat**: If any sequence step takes longer than the `MAX_GAP`, the system discards the progress and reverts to listening for a fresh gesture attempt.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Quadrant Mapping Setup
joy_y = machine.ADC(27)
buzzer = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

baseline_y = joy_y.read_u16()
gesture_state = 0
last_event_time = utime.ticks_ms()
MAX_GAP = 500 # ms window for double-tap

def sound_chirp():
    buzzer.freq(2000)
    buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
    buzzer.duty_u16(0)

while True:
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    raw_y = joy_y.read_u16()
    
    # Analyze direction
    is_up = (raw_y - baseline_y) > 20000
    is_centered = abs(raw_y - baseline_y) < 2000
    
    # Phase B: Temporal Pattern Analysis
    # Reset if too much time passes
    if utime.ticks_diff(now, last_event_time) > MAX_GAP:
        gesture_state = 0
        
    if is_up and gesture_state == 0:
        gesture_state = 1
        last_event_time = now
    elif is_centered and gesture_state == 1:
        gesture_state = 2
    elif is_up and gesture_state == 2:
        # Phase C: Command Execution
        print("MACRO TRIGGERED: DOUBLE-TAP UP")
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text("JUMP!", 40, 30)
        oled.show()
        sound_chirp()
        
        gesture_state = 0
        utime.sleep(0.5) # Protection delay

    utime.sleep_ms(20)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Bouncing**: Potentiometers can "noise" at the edge. A small `utime.sleep_ms(20)` debounces the movement so one flick isn't counted as two.
*   **Threshold too tight**: If you set the "UP" threshold too high (e.g. 32000), you have to slam the stick to trigger it. 15000-20000 is usually the sweet spot for light flicks.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Circle Detection**: Add X-axis tracking. Detect a sequence of **Up -> Right -> Down -> Left**. This is how fighting games recognize complex moves!

---

## 1. Project 1324: Advanced Joystick Control Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Sensor Signal Chain Diagnostics". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade diagnostic tool that visualizes the "Signal Lifecycle"—from Raw ADC counts to Normalized floats and finally to Deadzoned/Clamped control values—allowing for deep debugging of joystick hardware.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **The Signal Lifecycle**: Understanding how data is transformed at each stage: (1) Raw -> (2) Normalized -> (3) Calibrated -> (4) Filtered.
*   **Deadzoning (Schmitt Trigger Concept)**: Implementing an intentional "Null Zone" to prevent the tiny electrical noise (jitter) of center-parked sensors from causing unwanted movement.
*   **Side-by-Side Comparison**: Using a screen to compare the raw sensor noise against the "Clean" software output in real-time.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 | I2C0 | Diagnostic Readout |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 | I2C0 | Diagnostic Readout |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | Raw Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `absolute_value`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_table`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_format_float`

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_val**: Integer (The direct 16-bit ADC count read from the joystick pin, ranging from 0 to 65535).
*   **norm_val**: Float (The raw value scaled to a unified range of -1.0 to 1.0, where 0.0 is center).
*   **clean_val**: Float (The final output value after the deadzone filter has removed electrical noise).
*   **DEADZONE**: Float (A constant defining the 'Null Zone' threshold, e.g., 0.10 for a 10% movement buffer).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Signal Acquisition**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** and **Set** to **raw_val**.
2.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** **32768** from **raw_val**.
3.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **/**. **Divide** by **32768** to **Set** **norm_val**.

**Phase B: Differential Analysis**
4.  From **Math**, drag `absolute_value`. **Check** if the absolute of **norm_val** is less than **DEADZONE**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** into the processing loop.
6.  **If TRUE**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **clean_val** to **0.0**.
7.  **If FALSE**: **Set** **clean_val** to the current **norm_val**.

**Phase C: Diagnostic Visualization**
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Snap** after calculations.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 1 to display **raw_val** with prefix `"RAW: "`.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 2 to display **norm_val** with prefix `"NORM: "`.
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 3 to display **clean_val** with prefix `"CLEAN: "`.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_horizontal_line`. **Draw** a reference axis at Y=60.
13. From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_rectangle`. **Set** X position relative to **clean_val** to show movement.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the signal chain, defining the raw ADC source and the desired deadzone sensitivity for the specific hardware.
2.  **Process**: The system performs a multi-stage transformation: capturing the raw voltage, centering it around zero, and then suppressing any values that fall within the "Jitter" threshold.
3.  **Output**: All three stages of the signal lifecycle are printed to the OLED in a tabular format, providing a real-time audit of the software's filtering logic.
4.  **Repeat**: The diagnostic tool refreshes at 20 frames per second, allowing the user to physically observe how the software "Cleans" the noisy analog input into a stable control value.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Signal Acquisition Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
adc = machine.ADC(26)

DEADZONE = 0.1 # 10% Movement Buffer

while True:
    # 1. RAW
    raw_val = adc.read_u16()
    
    # 2. NORMALIZED (-1.0 to 1.0)
    norm_val = (raw_val - 32768) / 32768
    
    # Phase B: Differential Analysis
    if abs(norm_val) < DEADZONE:
        clean_val = 0.0
    else:
        clean_val = norm_val
        
    # Phase C: Diagnostic Visualization
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("SIGNAL LIFECYCLE", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"RAW:   {raw_val}", 0, 15)
    oled.text(f"NORM:  {norm_val:.3f}", 0, 30)
    oled.text(f"CLEAN: {clean_val:.3f}", 0, 45)
    
    # Center-oriented tracking bar
    oled.hline(10, 60, 108, 1)
    bar_x = int(64 + (clean_val * 54))
    oled.fill_rect(bar_x, 57, 4, 6, 1)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Screen Flickering**: Clearing and redrawing the screen too fast can cause a headache. A 50ms (20 FPS) update rate is usually the "Sweet Spot" for human eyes.
*   **Precision Loss**: If you convert values to integers too early in the chain, you lose all the detail of the movement! Keep them as floats until the final output.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Min/Max Tracking**: Add two variables `peak_min` and `peak_max`. Update them whenever a higher or lower value is seen. This shows you the total "Physical Range" of your specific joystick.

## 1. Project 1325: Automated Advanced Joystick Control Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Inertial Modeling & Slew Rate Limiting". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional "Motion Ramp" controller where a joystick doesn't directly set the output speed, but instead sets a "Target" speed that the system gradually approaches, simulating the momentum of a heavy vehicle or drone.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Velocity Ramping (Slew Rate)**: Constraining the maximum change allowed per frame to ensure smoothness. This prevents "Jerky" movements that can damage mechanical gears.
*   **Virtual Momentum**: Simulating physical weight in software by adding "Inertia" to digital inputs.
*   **Proportional Chase**: Making a variable "Chasing" another value at a fixed rate until they match.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **Servo Motor** (To visualize the smooth sweep)
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Output | Mechanical Sweep |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | Speed Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Sound Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `increment_value_towards`
*   From **Servo**, drag `set_servo_angle`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`

### 7. Variables
*   **target_pos**: Float (The desired mechanical angle calculated from the current joystick displacement).
*   **actual_pos**: Float (The current interpolated position of the servo that 'chases' the target).
*   **RAMP_STEP**: Float (The maximum number of degrees the system is allowed to move in a single 20ms frame).
*   **error**: Float (The mathematical difference between the actual position and the target position).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Goal Identification**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **actual_pos** to **90.0** (Center).
2.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** and **Set** to **raw_x**.
3.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **/**. **Divide** **raw_x** by **65535**.
4.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to *****. **Multiply** result by **180** and **Set** to **target_pos**.

**Phase B: Proportional Chase**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Snap** into the loop to check for alignment.
6.  **Check** if **actual_pos** is less than **target_pos**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **+**. **Increment** **actual_pos** by **RAMP_STEP**.
8.  **Repeat** the logic for the opposite direction using the **-** operator to decrement.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Clamp** the value so it does not "Overshoot" the target.

**Phase C: Mechanical Execution**
10. From **Motors**, drag `set_servo_angle`. **Set** Pin **16** to **actual_pos**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **20ms**.
12. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to display the current smooth position.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the servo to its default center position and calculates the initial joystick target based on the current physical stick angle.
2.  **Process**: Instead of snapping the motor to the target, the system calculates the "Error" and moves the actual position in small `RAMP_STEP` increments.
3.  **Output**: The resulting movement is a fluid, cinematic sweep that simulates the weight and inertia of a massive hydraulic crane or vehicle.
4.  **Repeat**: By adjusting the wait time and step size, the user can tune the controller to feel either "Light and Snappy" or "Heavy and Precise."

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Goal Identification Setup
joy = machine.ADC(26)
servo_pin = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo_pin.freq(50)

def set_angle(angle):
    # Standard PWM conversion (0-180 to duty cycle)
    duty = int((angle / 180 * 8000) + 1638)
    servo_pin.duty_u16(duty)

actual_pos = 90.0
RAMP_STEP = 2.0  # Degrees per 20ms frame
print("--- INERTIAL RAMPER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Capture Target from Joystick
    raw = joy.read_u16()
    target_pos = (raw / 65535) * 180
    
    # Phase B: Proportional Chase (The Slew Loop)
    error = target_pos - actual_pos
    
    if abs(error) > RAMP_STEP:
        if actual_pos < target_pos:
            actual_pos += RAMP_STEP
        else:
            actual_pos -= RAMP_STEP
    else:
        # Precision finish: Snap to target if closer than one step
        actual_pos = target_pos
        
    # Phase C: Mechanical Execution
    set_angle(actual_pos)
    print(f"TARGET: {int(target_pos)} | SMOOTHED: {int(actual_pos)}")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(20)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Loop Speed**: Slew rates are tied to the `utime.sleep_ms` value. if you slow down the loop, you slow down the ramp! Always calculate your ramp based on a fixed interval.

## 1. Project 1326: Advanced Joystick Control Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Deadman's Switch (Operator Presence Detection)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial-grade safety protocol where the joystick is only active while a separate 'Enable' button is held down, preventing accidental machine movement if the controller is bumped or dropped.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Interlock Logic**: Requiring two independent inputs to be true before an action is taken.
*   **Fail-Safe Design**: Ensuring the system defaults to a "Safe" (Zero) state if any part of the connection fails or the user lets go.
*   **Stateful Masking**: Using a boolean flag to "Mask" or block sensor data from reaching the output stage.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Joystick**
*   **Momentary Push Button** (The "Deadman" Grip)
*   **Red/Green LED Pair** (Status indicators)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Safety Button** | GP14 | Input (Pull-Up) |
| **Safe LED (GRN)** | GP13 | System Ready |
| **Active LED (RED)**| GP12 | Motion Enabled |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`and_gate`**
*   From **Input**, drag **`is_button_pressed`**
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set_enabled_flag`**

### 7. Variables
*   **is_safe**: Boolean (A master interlock flag that determines if joystick signals are permitted to control actuators).
*   **output_x**: Integer (The processed X-axis value, forced to zero if the safety interlock is open).
*   **output_y**: Integer (The processed Y-axis value, forced to zero if the safety interlock is open).
*   **is_armed**: Boolean (A status indicator reflected by the hardware LED state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Interlock Configuration**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin **14** (Safety Button) as an input with **Internal Pull-Up**.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pins **12** (RED) and **13** (GRN) as status indicators.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **is_safe** to **FALSE**.

**Phase B: Stateful Masking**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
5.  From **Smart IO**, drag `get_digital_input`. **Read** Pin **14**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** Pin **14** to **0 (Pressed)**.
7.  **If Pressed**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_safe** to **TRUE** and **ARM** the system.
8.  **If Released**: **Set** **is_safe** to **FALSE** and **LOCK** the system.

**Phase C: Status Indication**
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_safe** is **FALSE**.
10. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_value`. **Set** Pin **13** (GRN) to **1** and **Force** all motion outputs to **0**.
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_safe** is **TRUE**.
12. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_value`. **Set** Pin **12** (RED) to **1** and **Pass** the raw ADC values to **output_x/y**.
13. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to report the current safety state.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes with the "Deadman's Switch" interlock active, lighting the Green LED to indicate the machine is in a safe, locked state.
2.  **Process**: The system ignores all joystick movement until the safety button is actively held down, preventing accidental triggering from bumps or drops.
3.  **Output**: While the button is held, the Red LED lights up, and the joystick signals are "Masked" onto the output pins for real-time control.
4.  **Repeat**: Releasing the button instantly drops the `is_safe` flag, cutting power/signals to zero and returning the system to a fail-safe idle state.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Interlock Configuration Setup
button = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
led_safe = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)   # Green
led_armed = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.OUT)  # Red

adc_x = machine.ADC(26)
adc_y = machine.ADC(27)

print("--- FAIL-SAFE INTERLOCK ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Stateful Masking Logic
    # Button is Active-Low (0 when pressed)
    operator_present = (button.value() == 0)
    
    if operator_present:
        # Phase C: Status Indication (ARMED)
        led_safe.value(0)
        led_armed.value(1)
        
        # Pass signals through the gate
        x_val = adc_x.read_u16() - 32768
        y_val = adc_y.read_u16() - 32768
        print(f"CONTROL ACTIVE: X={x_val}, Y={y_val}")
    else:
        # Phase C: Status Indication (SAFE)
        led_safe.value(1)
        led_armed.value(0)
        
        # Kill signals instantly
        print("!! SAFETY LOCK: MOTORS DISABLED !!")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Logic Inversion**: If you use a Pull-Up, the button is `0` when pressed. Forgetting this will make the joystick work only when you *aren't* holding it!

        # FORCED STOP
        drive_pwr = 0
        print("!! POWER CUT: OPERATOR NOT PRESENT !!")
    
    # Phase C: Final Control
    motor.duty_u16(min(65535, drive_pwr))
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Logic Shortcut**: Don't put the `is_safe` check inside the joystick processing. Keep it as the "Master Gate" for the entire loop so that nothing can happen if the button is loose.
*   **Floating Pins**: If you forget the `PULL_UP`, GP14 might flip randomly, causing the motor to stutter dangerously.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Button Interlock**: Add a second button. Require both buttons to be held (Two-Hand Control) before the joystick works. This is standard in heavy hydraulic machinery.

## 1. Project 1327: Advanced Joystick Control Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Session Recording & Replay (Telemetry Systems)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a telemetry recorder that captures joystick movement at a fixed 10Hz frequency, serializes the data into a CSV file on the Pico's internal flash memory, and implements a 'Replay' engine that drives a servo motor by reading back the stored coordinates.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **File Stream Management**: Learning how to `open`, `write`, and `close` files in MicroPython without causing memory leaks.
*   **Serialization (CSV Format)**: Organizing data into Comma-Separated Values for easy interpretation by both the Pico and external programs like Excel.
*   **Time-Locked Sampling**: Using a fixed interval (100ms) to ensure the recorded "Movie" of the joystick movements plays back at the correct speed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **Servo Motor** (To observe the replay)
*   **Momentary Push Button** (Record Start/Stop)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | Movement Source |
| **Record Button**| GP14 | Input | Start/Stop Trigger |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Output | Replay Actuator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Files**, drag `file_open_write`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`

### 7. Variables
*   **log_file**: File Object (The reference handle for the open CSV file on the flash storage).
*   **is_recording**: Boolean (A flag that controls whether ADC data is currently being written to the file).
*   **sample_rate**: Milliseconds (The 100ms interval between data points for a 10Hz log).
*   **session_id**: String (A unique filename, e.g., 'flight_01.csv', to distinguish between recording sessions).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: File Handle Management**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin **14** (Button) with **Pull-Up**.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_recording** to **FALSE**.
3.  From **Files**, drag `file_open_append`. **Open** `"session.csv"` and **Set** to **log_file**.
4.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to "LOG READY".

**Phase B: Real-Time Serialization**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_recording** is **TRUE**.
7.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** into **raw_x**.
8.  From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`. **Combine** **ticks_ms**, `,`, and **raw_x**.
9.  From **Files**, drag `file_write_line`. **Write** the string to **log_file**.

**Phase C: Storage Optimization**
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the record button is pressed.
11. From **Logic**, drag `toggle_variable`. **Toggle** **is_recording**.
12. From **Files**, drag `file_flush`. **Commit** data to the flash memory so it isn't lost if power is cut.
13. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **100ms** (10Hz).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the internal file system and prepares a CSV header, standing ready to capture telemetry data.
2.  **Process**: When the record button is pressed, the system begins a high-speed loop, capturing the raw joystick position and attaching a millisecond timestamp to every entry.
3.  **Output**: Data is streamed directly to the onboard flash storage. Once recording stops, the system flushes the buffer to ensure the file is valid and readable.
4.  **Repeat**: The user can then switch the Pico into "Replay Mode," where the script reads the CSV lines back and moves the servo to mimic the original physical movements.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: File Handle Management Setup
joy = machine.ADC(26)
btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
is_recording = False

print("--- TELEMETRY RECORDER ---")

def record_session():
    global is_recording
    # Phase B: Real-Time Serialization logic
    with open("joystick_log.csv", "w") as f:
        print("RECORDING STARTED...")
        while is_recording:
            # Capture data at 10Hz
            val = joy.read_u16()
            timestamp = utime.ticks_ms()
            
            # Serialize to CSV
            f.write(f"{timestamp},{val}\n")
            
            # Check for stop trigger
            if btn.value() == 0:
                utime.sleep_ms(300) # Debounce
                is_recording = False
            
            utime.sleep_ms(100) # 10Hz sampling
        
        # Phase C: Storage Optimization
        f.flush()
    print("RECORDING SAVED.")

while True:
    if btn.value() == 0:
        utime.sleep_ms(300) # Debounce
        is_recording = True
        record_session()
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

## 1. Project 1328: Smart Advanced Joystick Control System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Flight Control Signal Mixing (Drone Remotes)". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a professional dual-stick drone controller "Mixer" that takes two independent 2-axis joysticks and combines their signals into a unified "Flight Packet" containing Throttle, Yaw, Pitch, and Roll data, ready for transmission to a quadcopter.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Control Axis Mixing**: Understanding the standard drone control layout: Left Stick (Throttle/Yaw) and Right Stick (Pitch/Roll).
*   **Struct Serialization**: Organizing multiple variables into a single "Packet" or string format for efficient communication.
*   **Input Clamping (Saturation)**: Ensuring that mixed signals never exceed the maximum allowable range (e.g., -100 to +100) even when multiple inputs are pushed to their limits simultaneously.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Analog Joysticks** (Total of 4 ADC Axes)
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Potentiometer** (For Master Throttle Trim)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick L (Y)**| GP26 | ADC0 | Throttle |
| **Joystick L (X)**| GP27 | ADC1 | Yaw |
| **Joystick R (Y)**| GP28 | ADC2 | Pitch |
| **Potentiometer** | GP29 | ADC3 | Master Trim |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `map_to_range`
*   From **Lists**, drag `create_json_packet`
*   From **Comm**, drag `pico_uart_send`

### 7. Variables
*   **throttle**: Integer (The vertical axis of the left joystick, mapped to -100 to 100 for lift control).
*   **yaw**: Integer (The horizontal axis of the left joystick, mapped for rotational heading control).
*   **pitch**: Integer (The vertical axis of the right joystick, mapped for forward/backward tilt).
*   **roll**: Integer (The horizontal axis of the right joystick, mapped for left/right tilt).
*   **packet_str**: String (The concatenated command string formatted for transmission to the flight controller).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Normalization & Map**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pins **26, 27, 28, 29** for all 4 flight axes.
2.  From **Math**, drag `map_to_range`. **Scale** the raw **0-65535** values to a signed range of **-100 to 100**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the absolute value is less than **5**.
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Force** the value to **0** (Deadzone stabilization).

**Phase B: Packet Serialization**
5.  From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`. **Combine** the character `"T"` with the **throttle** variable.
6.  **Continue** concatenation with `"Y"`, `"P"`, and `"R"` identifiers for the other axes.
7.  From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`. **Wrap** the entire sequence in start `$` and end `#` delimiters.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** result in **packet_str**.

**Phase C: Visual & Comm Link**
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** Line 1 to Line 4 to show all 4 flight values for the pilot.
10. From **Comm**, drag `pico_uart_send`. **Send** **packet_str** over **UART0** at **115200 baud**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **50ms** to maintain a 20Hz transmission frequency.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes two dual-axis joysticks and a high-speed UART link, preparing to act as the primary "Brain" for a drone remote.
2.  **Process**: The system captures movements from both sticks, translates them into the Standard Flight Package (T/Y/P/R), and applies precision deadzones to prevent accidental drift.
3.  **Output**: The logically mixed signals are serialized into a single condensed string packet and beamed to the aircraft's flight controller over the serial bus.
4.  **Repeat**: The loop cycles at 20Hz, providing near-instantaneous control response and real-time Telemetry feedback on the built-in OLED HUD.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Normalization & Map Setup
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=115200, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
i2c = machine.I2C(1, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

ax_throttle = machine.ADC(26) 
ax_yaw      = machine.ADC(27) 
ax_pitch    = machine.ADC(28) 
ax_roll     = machine.ADC(29)

def scale_axis(raw):
    # Map 0-65535 to -100 to 100
    val = int((raw - 32768) / 327.68)
    if abs(val) < 5: return 0 # Deadzone suppression
    return val

while True:
    t = scale_axis(ax_throttle.read_u16())
    y = scale_axis(ax_yaw.read_u16())
    p = scale_axis(ax_pitch.read_u16())
    r = scale_axis(ax_roll.read_u16())
    
    # Phase B: Packet Serialization
    # Format: $T[val],Y[val],P[val],R[val]#
    packet_str = f"$T{t},Y{y},P{p},R{r}#"
    
    # Phase C: Visual & Comm Link
    uart.write(packet_str + "\n")
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("FLIGHT TELEMETRY", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"THR: {t} | YAW: {y}", 0, 20)
    oled.text(f"PIT: {p} | ROL: {r}", 0, 40)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Baud Rate Mismatch**: If the flight controller expects 115,200 baud but you send at 9,600, all it will see is "Garbage" characters. Always verify your communication speed.
*   **Input Overload**: If you have too many I2C/ADC readings in a single loop, your "Refresh Rate" might drop below what is safe for flying (100Hz+ is ideal). Keep the loop light.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Inversion Toggles**: Add a set of DIP switches. If Switch 1 is ON, invert the `Pitch` value (`pitch = pitch * -1`). Some pilots prefer "Pull back to go up," while others like the opposite!

---

## 1. Project 1329: Advanced Joystick Control Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dynamic Range & Limit Discovery". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a scientific calibration tool that discovers the "True Hardware Limits" of a joystick (which are often oval or irregular rather than perfectly circular) and automatically scales those readings to a standardized 0-100% map.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Peak Signal Detection**: Continuously monitoring a stream of data to remember the highest and lowest values ever seen.
*   **Non-Linear Mapping**: Understanding that a joystick might reach "Full Power" at 90% of its physical travel, and adjusting the software to compensate.
*   **Interactive Calibration**: Designing a workflow where the user must perform specific actions (like "Drawing a Circle") to prepare the hardware for use.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **A Button** (To signal "Finish")

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | Discovery Source |
| **Finish Button**| GP14 | Input | UI Control |
| **OLED** | I2C (GP0, GP1) | UI | Visual Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `min_max_tracker`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_button_clicked`
*   From **Text**, drag `concatenate`

### 7. Variables
*   **d_min**: Integer (The lowest ADC value discovered during the calibration session, initialized to 65535).
*   **d_max**: Integer (The highest ADC value discovered during the calibration session, initialized to 0).
*   **thrown_range**: Integer (The total calculated 'throw' or physical distance between the discovered min and max).
*   **is_calibrated**: Boolean (A status flag that becomes true once the user locks in the discovery results).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Discovery Phase**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **d_min** to **65535** and **d_max** to **0**.
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** message to `"ROTATE STICK NOW"`.
3.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Check** if Pin **14** (Button) is **NOT Pressed**.
4.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** into **raw_val**.

**Phase B: Peak Limit Tracking**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare** **raw_val** to **d_min**.
6.  **If Lower**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **d_min** to **raw_val**.
7.  **Repeat** the comparison logic for **d_max** to find the highest peak.
8.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to show live min/max bounds.

**Phase C: Final Scaling Test**
9.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** **d_min** from **d_max** to **Set** **thrown_range**.
10. From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** into **test_val**.
11. From **Math**, drag `normalize_value`. **Map** **test_val** using the discovered **d_min** and **thrown_range** to a **0-100%** scale.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Set** result to display on the HUD.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters a calibration mode, waiting for the user to physically explore the full mechanical range of the joystick.
2.  **Process**: The system tracks the "Peaks" of the incoming signal. Every time a new high or low is reached, the software updates its internal map of the joystick's throw.
3.  **Output**: Once the user confirms the discovery, the logic calculates the scientific range and creates a perfectly linear map from 0 to 100%.
4.  **Repeat**: This ensures that even "Loose" or irregular hardware reaches its full potential (100%) exactly at the plastic circular limits of the stick housing.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Discovery Setup
joy = machine.ADC(26)
btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

d_min = 65535
d_max = 0

print("--- DISCOVERY START ---")
oled.fill(0)
oled.text("ROTATE STICK", 20, 20)
oled.text("PRESS BTN TO LOCK", 10, 40)
oled.show()

# Phase B: Peak Limit Tracking
while btn.value() == 1: # While button NOT pressed
    val = joy.read_u16()
    
    if val < d_min: d_min = val
    if val > d_max: d_max = val
    
    # Live Debug
    print(f"TRACKING -> MIN: {d_min} | MAX: {d_max}", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(10)

print("\n--- SCALE LOCKED ---")
thrown_range = d_max - d_min

# Phase C: Final Scaling Test
while True:
    raw = joy.read_u16()
    # Normalize from discovered range to 0-100%
    if thrown_range > 0:
        norm = ((raw - d_min) / thrown_range) * 100
        norm = max(0, min(100, norm)) # Safety Clamp
    else:
        norm = 0
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("CALIBRATED OUTPUT", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"RANGE: {thrown_range}", 0, 20)
    oled.text(f"POWER: {int(norm)}%", 0, 40)
    oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Poor Wiggling**: If you only move the stick Left and Right, the Up and Down calibration will be wrong. You must move in a wide circular pattern.
*   **Static Values**: Don't hardcode discovery values. Use a button to "Reset" the calibration if the hardware is swapped.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Profile Saving**: Use the `file_write` block to save `d_min` and `d_max` to a file called `calibration.dat`. On boot, the Pico can read this file and avoid needing a manual calibration every time!
