## 1. Project 1330: Advanced Advanced Joystick Control Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Bitmapped Graphics & Pixel Persistence". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a digital "Etch-A-Sketch" where the joystick controls a virtual pen across an OLED display, and the Pico maintains a persistent drawing by selectively lighting up pixels without clearing the screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Coordinate Persistence**: Understanding that if you don't call `oled.fill(0)`, the pixels you turned on will stay on, allowing for "Drawing."
*   **Coordinate Clamping**: Preventing the virtual pen from "Falling off the edge" of the 128x64 display buffer by using `min()` and `max()` logic.
*   **Digital Shake**: Implementing a "Clear Screen" function triggered by a physical button or a specific rapid movement of the joystick.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog 2-Axis Joystick**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**
*   **Momentary Button** (The "Shake" button)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 | I2C0 | Canvas |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 | I2C0 | Canvas |
| **Joystick VRX**| GP26 | ADC0 | Pen X-Axis |
| **Joystick VRY**| GP27 | ADC1 | Pen Y-Axis |
| **Clear Button**| GP14 | Input | Shake to Clear |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_pixel`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_button_pressed`
*   From **Math**, drag `constrain_value`

### 7. Variables
*   **cursor_x**: Float (The horizontal pixel coordinate of the pen, ranging from 0 to 127).
*   **cursor_y**: Float (The vertical pixel coordinate of the pen, ranging from 0 to 63).
*   **pen_speed**: Float (A multiplier that determines how many pixels the pen travels per movement frame).
*   **is_clear_active**: Boolean (A flag triggered by the hardware button to reset the canvas memory).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Canvas Initialization**
1.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Snap** into the setup block.
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Show** the initial blank buffer.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **cursor_x** to **64.0** and **cursor_y** to **32.0**.

**Phase B: Motion Drawing**
4.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** (X) and **27** (Y).
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Scale** the raw input to a range of **-2.0 to 2.0**.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `increment_variable`. **Add** the X displacement to **cursor_x**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `clamp_value`. **Force** **cursor_x** between **0 and 127** and **cursor_y** between **0 and 63**.

**Phase C: Persistence Management**
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_pixel`. **Set** coordinates to **cursor_x** and **cursor_y**. **Set** Color to **1 (White)**.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Update** the screen (Do NOT clear the buffer).
10. From **Smart IO**, drag `get_digital_input`. **Check** if Pin **14** is **0 (Pressed)**.
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Flush** the pixels and **Home** the pen if pressed.
12. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **10ms** for high-resolution sketching.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico clears the OLED's virtual memory but holds the pen at the center, waiting for user input to create the first mark.
2.  **Process**: The system tracks the joystick's trajectory. Because the `oled.fill()` command is bypassed, every pixel that the pen touches remains "On," creating a continuous line.
3.  **Output**: The user "Sketches" by navigating through the 128x64 coordinate grid. Small flickers are minimized by the persistent nature of the display buffer.
4.  **Repeat**: Pressing the physical button acts as a "Digital Shake," completely wiping the OLED buffer and resetting the artist's canvas to its original black state.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Canvas Initialization Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
jx = machine.ADC(26)
jy = machine.ADC(27)
clear_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# State Management
px, py = 64.0, 32.0
oled.fill(0)
oled.show()

print("--- ETCH-A-SKETCH ENGINE ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Motion Drawing Logic
    # Scale raw ADC to 4-pixel movement range
    mx = ((jx.read_u16() - 32768) / 32768) * 2.0
    my = ((jy.read_u16() - 32768) / 32768) * 2.0
    
    # Update position (Y inverted for screen coordinates)
    px += mx
    py -= my
    
    # Boundary Clamping
    px = max(0, min(127, px))
    py = max(0, min(63, py))
    
    # Phase C: Persistence Management
    # Write to buffer without filling background
    oled.pixel(int(px), int(py), 1)
    oled.show()
    
    # Clear Canvas Trigger
    if clear_btn.value() == 0:
        oled.fill(0)
        px, py = 64.0, 32.0
        oled.show()
        print("CANVAS RESET")
        utime.sleep_ms(300)

    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Wipeout**: If you accidentally put `oled.fill(0)` inside your main drawing loop, you will only see a single moving dot instead of a line.
*   **Slow Refresh**: `oled.show()` takes time. If you move the pen too fast between frames, the drawing will look like "Dotted Lines." Keep the speed low or draw lines between points.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Brush Size**: Use the joystick's internal switch (pushing the stick down) to change the brush from a single pixel to a `2x2` square for thicker lines!

## Batch 134: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1331: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"PWM Drive & Directional Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to interface the L9110S dual-channel motor driver with the Raspberry Pi Pico, controlling a DC motor's speed using PWM and its rotation direction by toggling bridge pins, while executing a full-range torque ramp from -100% (Reverse) to +100% (Forward).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **H-Bridge Architecture**: Understanding how the L9110S uses four transistors to flip the polarity of the voltage reaching the motor terminals.
*   **Duty Cycle Scaling**: Learning how to convert a percentage (-100 to 100) into a 16-bit PWM value (0-65535) for the Pico's hardware.
*   **Torque vs. RPM**: Observing how low PWM values may provide enough voltage to make the motor "Hum" but not enough torque to move the shaft (Project 1339 teaser).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver Module**
*   **Small DC Toy Motor** (3V - 6V)
*   **External Power Supply** (4x AA Batteries Recommended)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **L9110 IA** | GP16 | Digital/PWM | Direction A |
| **L9110 IB** | GP17 | Digital/PWM | Direction B |
| **Driver VCC**| VBUS/BATT | Power | 5V - 12V |
| **Driver GND**| GND | Ground | Must share Pico GND |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Motion & Motors**, drag `set_motor_speed`
*   **From Motion & Motors**, drag `stop_all_motors`
*   **From Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`

### 7. Variables
*   **motor_speed**: Integer (The commanded power level ranging from -100 for Full Reverse to 100 for Full Forward).
*   **duty_val**: Integer (The calculated 16-bit PWM duty cycle value required by the Pico hardware).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Driver Grounding**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Connect** Pin **16** (IA) and Pin **17** (IB) to the driver.
2.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`. **Set** to **1000Hz** for smooth shaft rotation.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **motor_speed** to **0**.

**Phase B: Motion Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** **motor_speed** to **0**.
5.  **If Positive (Forward)**: From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Set** Pin **16** to **duty_val** and Pin **17** to **0**.
6.  **If Negative (Reverse)**: From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Set** Pin **16** to **0** and Pin **17** to **duty_val**.
7.  **If Zero**: **Force** both pins to **0** for a full electrical stop.

**Phase C: Testing Phase**
8.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_range`. **Step** **motor_speed** from **0 to 100** by **10**.
9.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **200ms** to **Observe** the torque ramp.
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the loop has reached the limit, then **Step** from **0 to -100** for reverse testing.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the H-Bridge driver, setting the PWM carrier frequency to 1kHz for efficient motor induction.
2.  **Process**: The system executes a linear torque ramp, gradually increasing the voltage to the motor terminals to verify that the shaft spins smoothly in both directions.
3.  **Output**: Physical motion is observed as the motor accelerates to full RPM. The bridge pins alternate between PWM and Digital LOW to flip the magnetic polarity.
4.  **Repeat**: The script cycles through a full Forward-Stop-Reverse-Stop diagnostic pattern to confirm the wiring and power supply integrity.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Driver Grounding & Initialization
ia = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ib = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17))

# Standard motor frequency (1kHz)
ia.freq(1000)
ib.freq(1000)

def drive_motor(speed):
    # Phase B: H-Bridge Directional Logic
    # Forward: IA=PWM, IB=0 | Reverse: IA=0, IB=PWM
    if speed > 0:
        # Calculate 16-bit duty cycle (0-65535)
        duty = int(abs(speed) * 655.35)
        ia.duty_u16(duty)
        ib.duty_u16(0)
    elif speed < 0:
        duty = int(abs(speed) * 655.35)
        ia.duty_u16(0)
        ib.duty_u16(duty)
    else:
        # Emergency Stop / Neutral
        ia.duty_u16(0)
        ib.duty_u16(0)

# Phase C: Testing Phase Execution
print("COMMENCING RAMP TEST...")

# Forward 0-100%
for s in range(0, 101, 10):
    print(f"FORWARD: {s}%")
    drive_motor(s)
    utime.sleep_ms(200)

drive_motor(0)
utime.sleep(1)

# Reverse 0-100%
for s in range(0, -101, -10):
    print(f"REVERSE: {abs(s)}%")
    drive_motor(s)
    utime.sleep_ms(200)

drive_motor(0)
print("DIAGNOSTICS COMPLETE")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Floating Ground**: If you use an external battery, its Ground MUST be connected to the Pico's Ground, or the control signals won't work.
*   **Pin Overload**: Trying to drive a motor directly from Pico pins without the L9110S driver will likely damage your Pico. Always use the driver bridge.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Soft Start**: Implement a function that slowly "Ramps" the speed up every time you change direction to prevent mechanical wear.

## 1. Project 1332: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Power Telemetry (Watts & Efficiency)". At the end of this project, you will be able to calculate the real-time power consumption of a motor by multiplying the bus voltage by the estimated current draw (derived from PWM duty cycle and Ohm's Law), displaying the results as "Watts" on an OLED dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Ohm's Law in Motion**: Understanding the relationship `Power (W) = Voltage (V) * Current (I)`.
*   **Current Estimation**: Learning that the current drawn by a motor is roughly proportional to the PWM duty cycle, allowing us to estimate power without a dedicated shunt resistor.
*   **Telemetry Dashboards**: Designing a user interface that provides critical engineering data (Voltage, Amps, Watts) in a structured layout.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display (I2C)**
*   **Potentiometer** (Throttle Control)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 | ADC0 | Throttle Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 | I2C0 | Dashboard |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 | I2C0 | Dashboard |
| **Motor PWM** | GP16 | Output | Power Control |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Math**, drag `multiplier`
*   **From Math**, drag `map_range`
*   **From Smart Display**, drag `oled_print_table`

### 7. Variables
*   **v_bus**: Float (The static battery voltage value, used as the baseline for watt calculations).
*   **i_max**: Float (The maximum possible current draw of the motor at 100% duty cycle).
*   **estimated_watts**: Float (The real-time power consumption calculated by multiplying volts and estimated amps).
*   **duty_percent**: Float (The current throttle position expressed as a value from 0.0 to 1.0).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Normalization Phase**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** (Potentiometer) into **raw_pot**.
2.  From **Math**, drag `map_range`. **Scale** **raw_pot** to a **0.0 to 1.0** range and **Set** to **duty_percent**.
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Update** Pin **16** using **duty_percent** to drive the motor.

**Phase B: Physics Calculation**
4.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **\***. **Multiply** **duty_percent** by **i_max** to **Calculate** the estimated **current_amps**.
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **\***. **Multiply** **current_amps** by **v_bus** to **Calculate** the **estimated_watts**.
6.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Set** message to report the live energy stats to the terminal.

**Phase C: Visualization Phase**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Clear** the HUD buffer for the next frame.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Display** the string `"POWER DATA"` at the top.
9.  From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`. **Join** labels with the **current_amps** and **estimated_watts** variables.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Show** the updated telemetry dashboard.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Wait** for **100ms** to provide a steady refresh rate.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the motor driver and the OLED HUD, assuming a standard battery voltage (e.g., 6V) for the engineering calculations.
2.  **Process**: As the user turns the throttle knob, the software calculates the theoretical electrical current passing through the H-bridge based on the PWM duty cycle.
3.  **Output**: Real-time power telemetry (Watts) is generated using Ohm's Law and rendered to the screen as a live diagnostic feed for the operator.
4.  **Repeat**: The high-speed physics loop ensures that every change in RPM is immediately reflected in the power consumption readout, simulating a professional cockpit display.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Setup & Normalization
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
pot = machine.ADC(26)
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
motor.freq(1000)

V_BUS = 6.0  # Assumed battery voltage
I_MAX = 0.8  # Max current of motor in Amps

print("--- TELEMETRY SYSTEM ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Scale raw ADC to 0.0 - 1.0
    pot_val = pot.read_u16()
    duty_p = pot_val / 65535
    motor.duty_u16(int(pot_val))
    
    # Phase B: Physics Calculation Logic
    # Estimation using Ohm's Law: P = V * I
    amps = duty_p * I_MAX
    watts = V_BUS * amps
    
    # Phase C: Visualization Phase
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("MOTOR TELEMETRY", 5, 0)
    oled.hline(0, 10, 128, 1)
    
    oled.text(f"Voltage: {V_BUS}V", 0, 20)
    oled.text(f"Current: {amps:.2f}A", 0, 35)
    oled.text(f"POWER:   {watts:.2f}W", 0, 50)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Static Values**: In a real system, `V_BUS` would drop as the battery drains. For better accuracy, use a Voltage Divider (Project 1333) to measure actual volts instead of assuming 6.0V.
*   **Inductive Spikes**: Motors are noisy components. Ensure the OLED and Pico have a decoupling capacitor if the display flickers or resets during motor startup.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Energy Meter**: Integrate the Watts over time to calculate **Watt-Hours (Wh)**. This will tell you exactly how much total energy you have consumed during a drive session!

---

## 1. Project 1333: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Active Dynamic Braking (Kinetic Dissipation)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Active Brake" system using the L9110S driver, understanding how short-circuiting a spinning motor's terminals (setting both IA and IB to LOW) converts kinetic energy into heat to stop the motor instantly.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Coasting vs. Braking**: Understanding that cutting power (0% duty) lets a motor "Coast" to a slow stop, while shorting the pins creates "Magnetic Drag" for an emergency stop.
*   **Back Electromotive Force (Back-EMF)**: Learning that a spinning motor acts as a generator. If we short it, the Back-EMF fights the rotation.
*   **Threshold Triggering**: Using a high-speed logic loop to detect when the user "Slammed" the throttle to zero and automatically engaging the Brake.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor with heavy flywheel/propeller** (To see the coasting effect)
*   **Momentary Button** (The "E-Brake")

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **L9110S IA** | GP16 | Digital/PWM | High-Side |
| **L9110S IB** | GP17 | Digital/PWM | Low-Side |
| **E-Brake Button**| GP14 | Input | Active LOW |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_else_chain`
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `stop_motor_hard`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`

### 7. Variables
*   **is_braking**: Boolean (A master flag that determines if the H-bridge is actively shorting the motor terminals).
*   **brake_threshold**: Integer (The minimal digital value required to engage the emergency deceleration circuit).
*   **last_speed**: Integer (The power level directed to the motor immediately before the brake was triggered).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Braking Configuration**
1.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`. **Initialize** Pin **16** and Pin **17** to **1000Hz**.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin **14** (Button) to **Internal Pull-Up**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **is_braking** to **FALSE**.

**Phase B: Magnetic Drag Trigger**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
5.  From **Smart IO**, drag `get_digital_input`. **Read** Pin **14**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** Pin **14** to **0 (Pressed)**.
7.  **If Pressed**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_braking** to **TRUE**.
8.  **If Released**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_braking** to **FALSE**.

**Phase C: Emergency Stop Loop**
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_braking** is **TRUE**.
10. From **Motion & Motors**, drag `stop_motor_hard`. **Short** Pins **16** and **17** by setting both to **HIGH (1)**.
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_braking** is **FALSE**.
12. From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_motor_speed`. **Set** Pin **16** to **40%** to resume normal operation.
13. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **10ms** to stabilize the feedback loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the motor in a normal "Drive" state, pulsing current through the L9110S driver to maintain steady rotation.
2.  **Process**: The system high-speed monitors the digital "E-Brake" input. When triggered, the software instantly interrupts the PWM signal.
3.  **Output**: By forcing both motor terminals to HIGH simultaneously, the system creates a low-resistance path for the Back-EMF generated by the spinning rotor.
4.  **Repeat**: This magnetic resistance converts kinetic energy into an immediate physical stop. The process repeats instantly once the safety button is released.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Braking Configuration Setup
ia_pwm = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
ib_pwm = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17))
ia_pwm.freq(1000)
ib_pwm.freq(1000)

brake_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("--- ACTIVE BRAKING CONTROLLER READY ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Magnetic Drag Trigger Logic
    if brake_btn.value() == 0:
        # Phase C: Emergency Stop Execution (Brake Active)
        # To brake an L9110S effectively, set both IA and IB HIGH
        # This creates the necessary short-circuit for magnetic drag
        ia_pwm.duty_u16(65535)
        ib_pwm.duty_u16(65535)
        print("!! EMERGENCY BRAKE ENGAGED !!")
    else:
        # Phase C: Resuming Normal Forward Motion
        ia_pwm.duty_u16(25000) # ~40% Speed
        ib_pwm.duty_u16(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **IA=0, IB=0 Misconception**: On the L9110S, setting both to 0 is often "Floating" or "Coast." To get the strongest magnetic brake, setting both to 1 (HIGH) is usually required to push current through the high-side transistors. Always check your specific driver datasheet.
*   **Heat Pulse**: Don't leave the "Brake" on forever if the motor is being forced to spin by an external force (like a car rolling down a hill). The driver chip will get very hot.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Anti-Lock Braking (ABS)**: Write a loop that pulses the brake (`ON` for 50ms, `OFF` for 50ms). This prevents the wheels from completely locking up, allowing for controllable slides!

## 1. Project 1334: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Precision Angular Velocity (Tachometer Logic)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade tachometer that utilizes a high-speed infra-red (IR) speed sensor to measure a motor's "True RPM" (Revolutions Per Minute), processing hardware interrupts to capture micro-second pulses without missing a single rotation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hardware Interrupts (IRQ)**: Understanding how the Pico can pause its main program for a few micro-seconds to count an incoming sensor pulse, ensuring 100% accuracy even at high speeds.
*   **Time-Window Sampling**: Calculating velocity by counting pulses over a fixed 1-second interval (`Speed = Pulses / Time`).
*   **Rotational Coefficients**: Learning how to account for the number of "Slots" in an encoder disc (e.g., 20 slots per revolution) to derive accurate RPM.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **LM393 Optical Speed Sensor (IR Slot)**
*   **Encoder Disc (20 Slots)**
*   **SSD1306 OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **IR Sensor OUT** | GP15 | Digital (IRQ) | Pulse Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP0 | I2C0 | Gauge Display |
| **OLED SCL** | GP1 | I2C0 | Gauge Display |
| **Motor PWM** | GP16 | Output | Variable Speed |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Comm**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_1s`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `oled_draw_arc_gauge`

### 7. Variables
*   **pulse_count**: Integer (A persistent global accumulator that stores the raw number of electrical pulses received from the IR sensor).
*   **rpm_actual**: Integer (The final calculated velocity of the motor shaft in Revolutions Per Minute).
*   **last_time**: Integer (The microsecond timestamp of the previous sampling window).
*   **slots_per_rev**: Integer (The specific mechanical constant of the encoder disc, typically 20 notches).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Interrupt Configuration**
1.  From **Comm**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Connect** Pin **15** to the IR sensor output and **Initialize** a **Falling Edge** trigger.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **16** to **50% PWM** to provide steady motor rotation.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** **pulse_count** to **0**.

**Phase B: Rotational Timing**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
5.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **1.0 Seconds** to create a fixed sampling window.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Calculate** the difference between the current **pulse_count** and the **previous_count**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **/**. **Divide** the difference by **slots_per_rev** to find revolutions per second.
8.  **Multiply** by **60** to **Calculate** the **rpm_actual**.

**Phase C: Dashboard Rendering**
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Clear** the screen buffer.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Display** `"ENGINE TACHOMETER"` at the top of the HUD.
11. From **Text**, drag `pico_concatenate`. **Join** the label `"SPEED:"` with **rpm_actual** and `"RPM"`.
12. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Show** the gauge to the operator.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The motor begins spinning, forcing the encoder disc through the infrared slot and generating a stream of high-speed electrical pulses.
2.  **Process**: The Pico's hardware interrupt engine counts every single notch without blocking the main program, ensuring 100% data integrity even at 10,000+ RPM.
3.  **Output**: Every second, the mathematical engine converts the raw pulse stream into a stabilized angular velocity reading.
4.  **Repeat**: The OLED HUD updates its digital needle and numeric readout, providing a professional-grade telemetry feed of the motor's true physical performance.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Phase A: Interrupt Configuration Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(0), scl=machine.Pin(1))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
motor.freq(1000)
sensor = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

pulse_count = 0
SLOTS = 20

# Hardware Interrupt Handler
def irq_handler(pin):
    global pulse_count
    pulse_count += 1

sensor.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handler=irq_handler)

print("--- DIGITAL TACHOMETER ONLINE ---")
motor.duty_u16(32768) # Constant 50% Power

while True:
    # Phase B: Rotational Timing Logic
    # Capture pulses over a 1-second window
    start_pulses = pulse_count
    utime.sleep(1.0)
    end_pulses = pulse_count
    
    # Calculate Velocity
    diff = end_pulses - start_pulses
    rpm = (diff / SLOTS) * 60
    
    # Phase C: Dashboard Rendering
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("ENGINE STATUS", 10, 0)
    oled.hline(0, 10, 128, 1)
    
    oled.text(f"SPEED: {int(rpm)} RPM", 0, 30)
    
    # Render Dynamic Progress Bar
    bar_w = int((rpm / 5000) * 120)
    oled.rect(4, 50, 120, 10, 1)
    oled.fill_rect(4, 50, bar_w, 10, 1)
    
    oled.show()
    print(f"RPM Update: {int(rpm)} (Pulse Delta: {diff})")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Logic**: If you use `time.sleep(1)` to wait for the pulse count, ensure your interrupt is running in the *background*. If you try to "poll" the pin in a loop, you will miss 90% of the pulses at high speed.
*   **Integer Division**: In Python 3 (MicroPython), `1200 / 20` results in a float `60.0`. If you need an integer for the display, use `int(rpm)`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Stall Alarm**: Add an `if` statement: if the motor power is HIGH but the RPM is 0, the motor is jammed! Flash a "STALL" warning on the OLED and cut the power.

## 1. Project 1335: Automated Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Thermal Governing & Safety Throttling". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated safety governor that monitors the Pico's internal chip temperature and dynamically reduces (throttles) the motor's power output if the system exceeds 45°C, preventing thermal runaway and protecting the driver electronics.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Thermal Throttling**: The process of intentionally slowing down a system or reducing power to dissipate heat.
*   **Internal Temperature Sensing**: Accessing the dedicated ADC channel (ADC4) inside the RP2040 chip to monitor its thermal state without external sensors.
*   **Dynamic Constraint Logic**: Learning how to apply a "Cap" or "Ceiling" to a variable based on a secondary environmental factor.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **Red LED** (Over-temperature Warning)
*   **10k Potentiometer** (User Throttle)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | ADC(4) | Internal | Temperature Source |
| **Red LED** | GP14 | Output | Alarm Indicator |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 | ADC0 | User Intent |
| **Motor IA** | GP16 | Output | Governed Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_internal_temp`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_greater_than`
*   From **Math**, drag `constrain_value`

### 7. Variables
*   **current_temp**: Float (The real-time temperature of the RP2040 chip, measured via the internal ADC channel 4).
*   **throttle_cap**: Integer (The software-imposed ceiling on motor power, ranging from 0 to 100 based on thermal state).
*   **user_request**: Float (The desired speed percentage requested by the operator via the potentiometer).
*   **actual_duty**: Integer (The final governed 16-bit PWM value sent to the motor driver after safety checks).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Thermal Calibration**
1.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_internal_temp`. **Initialize** the internal sensor loop.
2.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Calculate** the formula: `Temp = 27 - (Reading - 0.706) / 0.001721`.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **throttle_cap** to **100** (No limit).

**Phase B: Governing Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Compare** **current_temp** to thermal thresholds.
5.  **If > 45°C**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **throttle_cap** to **0** (Emergency Lockdown).
6.  **If > 40°C**: **Set** **throttle_cap** to **50** (Safety Throttling).
7.  **Else**: **Set** **throttle_cap** to **100** (Full Operation).

**Phase C: Safety Execution**
8.  From **Analog**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Read** Pin **26** for **user_request**.
9.  From **Math**, drag `min_value`. **Compare** **user_request** and **throttle_cap**. **Set** to the **Lower** of the two.
10. From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_motor_speed`. **Set** Pin **16** to the governed power level.
11. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **current_temp** is high. **Flash** Pin **14** (LED) as a visual warning.
12. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **200ms** to allow for thermal dissipation.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins monitoring its own internal temperature while simultaneously serving as an H-bridge motor controller.
2.  **Process**: The system constantly compares its thermal health against a 40°C warning limit and a 45°C critical shutdown limit.
3.  **Output**: If the chip gets too hot, the governor "Squeezes" or caps the power available to the motor, regardless of where the physical knob is set.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the system cools down, the safety latch is lifted, and the user regains full linear control over the motor's performance.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Thermal Calibration Setup
temp_sensor = machine.ADC(4)
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
motor.freq(1000)
pot = machine.ADC(26)
warning_led = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

def get_core_temp():
    # Read raw 16-bit and convert to Celsius
    reading = temp_sensor.read_u16() * (3.3 / 65535)
    temperature = 27 - (reading - 0.706) / 0.001721
    return temperature

print("--- THERMAL GOVERNOR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Sensor Acquisition
    curr_t = get_core_temp()
    user_pwr = (pot.read_u16() / 655.35) # 0-100%
    
    # Phase B: Governing Logic Process
    if curr_t > 45.0:
        throttle_cap = 0
        warning_led.value(1) # Solid Alarm
    elif curr_t > 40.0:
        throttle_cap = 50
        # Warning Pulse
        warning_led.value(not warning_led.value())
    else:
        throttle_cap = 100
        warning_led.value(0)
        
    # Phase C: Safety Execution (Output)
    # The 'min' function ensures the governor has the final say
    final_pwr = min(user_pwr, throttle_cap)
    motor.duty_u16(int(final_pwr * 655.35))
    
    print(f"CORE: {curr_t:.1f}C | GOVERNOR: {throttle_cap}% | OUTPUT: {int(final_pwr)}%")
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Formula Errors**: The Pico Internal Temp formula is very specific. Using the wrong constants will result in temperatures like -200°C or 5000°C.
*   **Oscillation**: If you set the throttle cap to 0 at 45.0C exactly, the motor might "Stutter" as the temp bounces between 44.9 and 45.1. Adding a 1-degree buffer (Hysteresis) fixes this.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Active Cooling**: Add a second L9110 channel driving a small fan. If the temperature exceeds 35°C, turn on the fan to try and cool the Pico down before throttling is required!

## 1. Project 1336: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Inertial Collision Detection (Impact Shutdown)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build an intelligent safety system that utilizes an MPU6050 accelerometer to detect high-G "Impact Events" (collisions), automatically cutting all motor power and locking the system until a manual reset is performed to prevent further damage.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **G-Force Monitoring**: Understanding how to measure linear acceleration in units of "G" (where 1G = 9.8 m/s²).
*   **Impact Thresholds**: Identifying the difference between normal vibration and a collision (e.g., > 3.0G).
*   **Stateful Latching (Lockdown)**: Implementing a software "Latch" that prevents the system from restarting after a crash, even if the user tries to use the controls.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **MPU6050 Accelerometer (I2C)**
*   **Buzzer** (Siren)
*   **Momentary Button** (Safety Reset)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MPU6050 SDA** | GP4 | I2C0 | Motion Sense |
| **MPU6050 SCL** | GP5 | I2C0 | Motion Sense |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Output | Siren |
| **Reset Button** | GP15 | Input | Safety Release |
| **Motor IA** | GP16 | Output | Drive Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `mpu6050_get_accel`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_absolute_greater_than`
*   From **Audio**, drag `pico_play_siren`

### 7. Variables
*   **is_locked**: Boolean (A safety latch flag that permanently disables motor output once a collision is detected).
*   **accel_magnitude**: Float (The calculated total G-force exerted on the system, derived from X, Y, and Z vector components).
*   **impact_threshold**: Float (The G-force limit, typically 3.0G, representing a high-speed collision rather than normal vibration).
*   **alert_state**: Integer (A state variable that alternates between 0 and 1 to control the siren oscillation).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: I2C Motion Setup**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `mpu6050_init`. **Initialize** the I2C bus on Pins **4** (SDA) and **5** (SCL).
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Set** Pin **14** (Buzzer) and Pin **16** (Motor IA).
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Set** Pin **15** (Reset Button) to **Internal Pull-Up**.
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **is_locked** to **FALSE**.

**Phase B: Vector Force Analysis**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`. **Snap** into the workspace.
6.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `mpu6050_get_accel`. **Read** the current X, Y, and Z values into **ax, ay, az**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Calculate** the absolute value of each axis.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare** any axis against **impact_threshold** (3.0G).
9.  **If Triggered**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_locked** to **TRUE** and **Kill** all motor signals.

**Phase C: Latching Lockdown**
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_locked** is **TRUE**.
11. From **Audio**, drag `pico_play_siren`. **Toggle** Pin **14** to create an audible emergency tone.
12. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the reset button on Pin **15** is **0 (Pressed)**.
13. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** **is_locked** to **FALSE** to restore control only after operator intervention.
14. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Wait** for **10ms** to provide a high-fidelity impact monitoring loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system initializes the MPU6050 inertial sensor and enters a standby state, monitoring for sudden mechanical shocks while the motor drives the robot.
2.  **Process**: The software high-speed samples the G-forces. If a collision occurs (e.g., > 3.0G), the system immediately "Latches" into a lockdown state.
3.  **Output**: All motor power is cut instantly to prevent further damage, and a high-pitched siren is activated to alert personnel of the impact event.
4.  **Repeat**: The Pico remains in the lockdown state, ignoring all movement commands, until an operator manually presses the physical reset button to confirm the robot is safe to proceed.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
from mpu6050 import MPU6050

# Phase A: I2C Motion Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
imu = MPU6050(i2c)
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
motor.freq(1000)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

is_locked = False
G_LIMIT = 3.0

print("--- IMPACT PROTECTION HUB READY ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Vector Force Analysis Logic
    accel = imu.get_accel()
    # Read absolute values to catch hits from any direction
    ax, ay, az = abs(accel['x']), abs(accel['y']), abs(accel['z'])
    
    if not is_locked:
        if ax > G_LIMIT or ay > G_LIMIT or az > G_LIMIT:
            print(f"!!! CRASH DETECTED: FORCE={max(ax, ay, az):.2f}G !!!")
            is_locked = True
            motor.duty_u16(0) # Emergency cutoff
    
    # Phase C: Latching Lockdown Execution
    if is_locked:
        # Emergency Siren Toggle
        buzzer.value(not buzzer.value())
        utime.sleep_ms(100)
        
        # Manual Override Check
        if reset_btn.value() == 0:
            print("Impact Cleared. System Resetting...")
            is_locked = False
            buzzer.value(0)
            utime.sleep(1) # Safety Delay
    else:
        # Standard Operation Loop
        motor.duty_u16(20000) # Constant Drive
        utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Gravity Offset**: Remember that a flat MPU6050 will show **1.0G** on the Z-axis due to Earth's gravity. When setting your `THRESHOLD`, ensure you account for this or "Zero" the sensor on boot.
*   **Vibration Noise**: A very fast motor might create 0.5G of vibration noise. Don't set your threshold too low (like 1.1G) or the motor's own noise will trigger the safety shutdown.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Black Box Recorder**: When an impact is detected, save the last 5 readings to a file (`crash_log.txt`) so you can analyze exactly how hard the impact was later!

---

## 1. Project 1337: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Automated Cycle Testing (Stress Profiling)". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a rigorous "Stress Test" for a motor system, where the Pico automatically executes multiple 0-100% power ramps, logs the resulting RPM stability, and generates a "Pass/Fail" report based on mechanical consistency.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Stress Testing**: Systematically pushing a component to its operational limits to identify weaknesses or fatigue.
*   **Statistical Consistency**: Measuring the variance in RPM over several identical tests to determine if a motor is experiencing "Slippage" or "Binding."
*   **Automated Validation**: Writing code that makes its own decision about hardware health without human intervention.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **IR Speed Sensor (Tachometer)**
*   **USB Cable for Data Logging**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Driver IA** | GP16 | Output | PWM Ramp |
| **Encoder Pin** | GP15 | Input (IRQ) | Speed Source |
| **Pico USB** | PC | UART | Test Report |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag `for_loop_integer`
*   From **Math**, drag `calculate_average`
*   From **File**, drag `pico_append_file`

### 7. Variables
*   **test_cycle**: Integer (The current iteration of the stress test, ranging from 1 to 10).
*   **peak_rpm**: Float (The maximum speed achieved during the specific power ramp of the current cycle).
*   **pass_threshold**: Integer (The minimum acceptable RPM limit, e.g., 4000, for the mechanical system to be considered healthy).
*   **cycle_avg**: Float (The calculated mean speed across all completed test rounds, used for statistical verification).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Stress Protocol Configuration**
1.  From **Comm**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Initialize** Pin **15** (Encoder) with a **Falling Edge** trigger to track speed.
2.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`. **Set** Pin **16** to **1000Hz**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **pass_threshold** to **4000**.
4.  From **File**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Create** a new log named `"stress_log.txt"`.

**Phase B: Performance Capture**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_range`. **Step** **test_cycle** from **1 to 10**.
6.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_motor_speed`. **Ramp** Pin **16** to **100% duty** over **2 seconds**.
7.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **1.0 Seconds** while high-speed pulses are counted.
8.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Calculate** the **peak_rpm** for the current cycle.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `add_to_list`. **Append** **peak_rpm** to the results database.

**Phase C: QC Decision Engine**
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** each **peak_rpm** against **pass_threshold**.
11. **If Below**: From **File**, drag `pico_append_file`. **Record** a `"MECHANICAL_FAILURE"` timestamp.
12. From **Math**, drag `calculate_average`. **Solve** for **cycle_avg** using the full results list.
13. From **Comm**, drag `pico_uart_send`. **Report** the final consistency score to the terminal.
14. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Set** to **2 seconds** between cycles to allow for motor cooling (thermal stabilization).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initiates a rigorous 10-cycle diagnostic sequence designed to identify mechanical fatigue or electrical voltage sag in the motor driver.
2.  **Process**: Every cycle, the motor is pushed to its absolute maximum limit. The system captures the RPM and evaluates "Linear Stability"—checking if the motor slows down as it gets hotter.
3.  **Output**: All data points are written to a persistent on-board text file, creating a digital "Black Box" of the hardware's performance.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the 10th cycle completes, the QC engine analyzes the data, issuing a PASS or FAIL verdict based on whether the motor maintained its speed under high stress.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Stress Protocol Configuration Setup
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
encoder = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
pulse_count = 0
SAFE_RPM = 4000

def irq_handler(p):
    global pulse_count
    pulse_count += 1

encoder.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handler=irq_handler)

results = []
print("--- STRESS TEST SEQUENCE: 10 CYCLES ---")

with open("stress_log.txt", "w") as f:
    f.write("QC LOG START\n")

# Phase B: Performance Capture Loop
for cycle in range(1, 11):
    print(f"CYCLE {cycle}/10: Ramping...")
    pulse_count = 0
    motor.duty_u16(65535) # Maximum stress
    utime.sleep(2) # Stabilization
    
    # 1-second sampling window
    start_c = pulse_count
    utime.sleep(1)
    rpm = ((pulse_count - start_c) / 20) * 60
    
    # Phase C: QC Decision Logic
    results.append(rpm)
    status = "OK" if rpm >= SAFE_RPM else "LOW_PERFORMANCE"
    
    # Record to Black Box
    with open("stress_log.txt", "a") as f:
        f.write(f"Cycle: {cycle}, RPM: {int(rpm)}, Status: {status}\n")
    
    motor.duty_u16(0)
    print(f"Captured: {int(rpm)} RPM - {status}")
    utime.sleep(2) # Thermal Recovery

# Phase B: Performance Analysis
avg_rpm = sum(results) / len(results)
failed_cycles = [i for i, r in enumerate(results) if r < SAFE_RPM]

# Phase C: Final QC Report
print("\n--- TEST COMPLETE ---")
print(f"Average RPM Across Rounds: {avg_rpm:.1f}")
if len(failed_cycles) == 0:
    print("STATUS: PASS (100% Reliable Hardware)")
else:
    print(f"STATUS: FAIL ({len(failed_cycles)} unstable cycles detected)")
    print(f"Issues at cycles: {failed_cycles}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Heat Soak**: Testing too fast without "Cooldown" periods will cause the motor to fail simply because it's too hot. Always add a 2-second rest between ramps.
*   **Insufficient Data**: One cycle isn't enough to prove a machine is reliable. 10+ cycles are needed to see a statistical trend.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Battery Compensation**: If the test fails, use the Project 1333 code to check if the battery voltage is low. If it is, report "FAIL: LOW POWER" instead of "FAIL: MOTOR BINDING."
*   **Ramp-Up Timer**: Calculate the exact amount of time (in milliseconds) it takes for the motor to reach 100% speed and log this "Inertia Score" as well!
---

## 1. Project 1338: Smart Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) System

### 2. Learning Objective
"IoT Remote Telemetry (MQTT & Cloud Integration)". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a professional "Cloud-Connected" motor controller that transmits JSON-formatted telemetry data (RPM, Direction, Power) to a remote dashboard and executes "Over-the-Air" (OTA) speed commands using simulated MQTT messaging.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transport)**: Understanding the industry-standard "Publish/Subscribe" protocol used for IoT communication.
*   **JSON Serialization**: Converting Pico variables into a structured text format (JavaScript Object Notation) so that websites and mobile apps can read them.
*   **Remote Setpoints**: Learning how to decouple user input (knobs) from the control logic, allowing a computer 1000 miles away to set the motor speed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Or standard Pico with UART bridge)
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **USB Cable** (For Serial-to-Cloud simulation)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Driver IA** | GP16 | Output | Motor Control |
| **Pico USB** | PC | UART | Cloud Proxy Port |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Comm**, drag `mqtt_publish`
*   From **Comm**, drag `mqtt_on_message`
*   From **Text**, drag `create_json_object`

### 7. Variables
*   **mqtt_topic**: String (The specific communication channel for publishing motor status updates).
*   **remote_speed**: Integer (The commanded power level received from the cloud dashboard).
*   **telemetry_payload**: String (The JSON-formatted text packet containing real-time device stats).
*   **current_power**: Integer (The locally measured motor speed percentage).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Telemetry Transmission**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_output`. **Initialize** Pin **16** (Motor) and Pin **25** (Onboard LED).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Collect** **current_power** and system temperature.
3.  From **Text**, drag `create_json_object`. **Combine** variables into a structured **telemetry_payload**.
4.  From **Comm**, drag `pico_uart_send`. **Transmit** the payload via USB/UART every **5 seconds**.

**Phase B: Remote Control**
5.  From **Comm**, drag `mqtt_on_message`. **Listen** for incoming JSON strings on the control topic.
6.  From **Text**, drag `parse_json_object`. **Extract** the "speed" value into **remote_speed**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the value is within the **0-100** range.
8.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_motor_speed`. **Update** Pin **16** to the new **remote_speed**.

**Phase C: Connectivity Handshake**
9.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_value`. **Blink** the LED for **100ms** to acknowledge command receipt.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Display** `"IOT COMMAND RCVD"` on the local HUD.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Pause** for **3 seconds** between telemetry cycles to avoid network congestion.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico connects to the telemetry gateway (Serial Monitor) and begins broadcasting its internal health state in JSON format.
2.  **Process**: The system acts as a "Subscriber," constantly polling the data stream for remote speed setpoints issued by an external controller.
3.  **Output**: Upon receiving a valid MQTT simulation command, the Pico instantly adjusts the H-bridge duty cycle and notifies the operator via an LED heartbeat.
4.  **Repeat**: The telemetry loop ensures that a remote dashboard stays updated with the motor's actual performance, even as remote commands change.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import json

# Phase A: IoT Gateway Setup
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
motor.freq(1000)
led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT) # Status Heartbeat

print("--- CLOUD-CONNECTED MOTOR HUB ACTIVE ---")
print("Target MQTT Topic: pico/motor/telemetry")

while True:
    # Phase B: Telemetry Transmission Logic
    # Gather internal metrics
    pwr_pct = int((motor.duty_u16() / 65535) * 100)
    
    # Serialize to JSON
    telemetry = {
        "device_id": "PICO_QC_01",
        "motor_pwr": pwr_pct,
        "is_active": pwr_pct > 0,
        "timestamp_ms": utime.ticks_ms()
    }
    
    # Broadcast to Proxy (UART/USB)
    print(f"MQTT_PUB: {json.dumps(telemetry)}")
    
    # Phase C: Remote Control Handshake (Mocked RX)
    # Simulate receiving a message: '{"speed": 85}'
    # In a real Pico W, this would be an MQTT callback
    utime.sleep(2)
    incoming_msg = '{"speed": 85}' 
    
    try:
        # Deserialize & Execute
        command = json.loads(incoming_msg)
        target_s = command.get("speed", 0)
        motor.duty_u16(int(target_s * 655.35))
        
        print(f"MQTT_SUB: Remote setpoint updated to {target_s}%")
        
        # Visual Acknowledgement (Shake)
        led.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100); led.value(0)
    except:
        print("!! INBOUND DATA CORRUPT !!")
    
    utime.sleep(3) # Network Throttle
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **JSON Formatting**: A missing comma or bracket in your JSON string will cause the receiving dashboard to crash. Always use the `json.dumps()` function to ensure the format is perfect.
*   **Baud Rate Overload**: If you send telemetry every 1ms at a slow baud rate (9600), the Pico's serial buffer will overflow and the program will hang. 1Hz or 0.2Hz (every 5s) is plenty for motor status.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Web Dashboard**: Use a tool like "Node-RED" or "Ubidots" on your PC to catch these JSON strings and display them as beautiful gauges and charts!

---

## 1. Project 1339: Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Stiction & Dead-Band Discovery". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a hardware calibration tool that automatically identifies the "Dead-Band"—the minimum PWM power required to overcome static friction (stiction)—and saves this threshold to the Pico's flash memory to ensure ultra-smooth motor starts in future projects.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Static Friction (Stiction)**: Understanding that motors require a higher "Kick" of energy to start spinning than they do to maintain speed.
*   **PWM Linearity**: Learning that the first 0-15% of PWM duty often produces zero movement, which can cause "Lags" in control systems if not compensated.
*   **Automated Threshold Discovery**: Designing a diagnostic script that systemically increases power until movement is confirmed by a sensor.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **L9110S Motor Driver**
*   **DC Motor**
*   **IR Speed Sensor (Tachometer)**
*   **Onboard Flash Memory** (Internal)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Driver IA** | GP16 | Output | Discovery Pulse |
| **Encoder Pin** | GP15 | Input (IRQ) | Motion Feedback |
| **Internal Flash**| Internal | Storage | Log Persistent Data |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag `while_loop`
*   From **Math**, drag `increment_value`
*   From **File**, drag `pico_save_config`

### 7. Variables
*   **test_duty**: Integer (The sliding PWM window being analyzed, starting at 0 and increasing until torque is sufficient).
*   **movement_detected**: Boolean (A flag triggered by the IRQ when the first physical movement occurs).
*   **final_deadband**: Integer (The recorded duty cycle value representing the 'Stiction Threshold').
*   **stability_check**: Integer (A secondary counter used to verify that the motor is actually spinning and not just jittering).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Discovery Phase**
1.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_frequency`. **Initialize** Pin **16** to **1000Hz**.
2.  From **Comm**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Initialize** Pin **15** (Encoder) with a **Falling Edge** trigger.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** **test_duty** to **0** and **movement_detected** to **FALSE**.

**Phase B: Linear Incremental Ramp**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Loop** while **movement_detected** is **FALSE** AND **test_duty < 65535**.
5.  From **Math**, drag `increment_value`. **Add** **500** to **test_duty** every cycle.
6.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Update** Pin **16** using the current **test_duty**.
7.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **250ms** to allow the rotor to overcome mechanical inertia.

**Phase C: Persistence Commit**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **movement_detected** transitioned to **TRUE**.
9.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Kill** power to Pin **16** immediately to prevent runaway.
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **final_deadband** to **test_duty**.
11. From **File**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Commit** **final_deadband** to a text file named `"motor_cal.txt"`.
12. From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Report** the discovered stiction point to the console.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system enters a discovery state with the motor at rest, waiting to identify the exact electrical energy level required to begin physical rotation.
2.  **Process**: The Pico slowly "Creeps" the power upwards in small, 0.7% increments. This systematic ramp allows the software to find the edge of the motor's dead-band without overshooting.
3.  **Output**: The moment the encoder notch passes the IR gate, an interrupt is fired. The Pico captures this "Instant of Motion" and records the electrical state.
4.  **Repeat**: The calibration value is permanently saved to the Flash memory, allowing future scripts to "Jump" past the dead-zone and provide instant, responsive motor control.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Discovery Setup
motor = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
encoder = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)
motion_flag = False

def motion_handler(p):
    global motion_flag
    motion_flag = True

# Attach interrupt to catch THE FIRST movement
encoder.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handler=motion_handler)

print("--- STICTION CALIBRATION HUB ACTIVE ---")
print("Discovery protocol in progress...")
utime.sleep(2)

test_duty = 0
step_size = 500

# Phase B: Linear Incremental Ramp Logic
while not motion_flag and test_duty < 60000:
    test_duty += step_size
    motor.duty_u16(test_duty)
    
    # Debug telemetry
    print(f"DIAGNOSTIC POWER: {int((test_duty/65535)*100)}%", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(250)

# Instant Cutoff
motor.duty_u16(0)

# Phase C: Persistence Commit
if motion_flag:
    print(f"\nMOVEMENT DETECTED AT: {test_duty}")
    
    try:
        # Save value to non-volatile flash memory
        with open("motor_cal.txt", "w") as f:
            f.write(str(test_duty))
        print("SUCCESS: Calibration file 'motor_cal.txt' updated.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"STATION ERROR: {e}")
else:
    print("\nTIMEOUT: No motion detected. Check power supply or mechanical jamming.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Dirty" Sensor**: If your IR sensor has dust on it, it might trigger even when the motor isn't moving. Ensure the sensor is clean for an accurate calibration.
*   **Step Size**: If your `step` is too large (e.g. 5000), your discovery will be imprecise. Keep the step small (200-500) for high-resolution results.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reverse Calibration**: Motors often have more friction in one direction than the other. Repeat the test for the "Reverse" direction and see if the dead-band is different!

---

## 1. Project 1340: Advanced Advanced Motor Driver (L9110) Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Active Traction Control (Synchronous Slip Logic)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade "Anti-Skid" system for a dual-motor robot where the Pico independently monitors the RPM of the left and right wheels and automatically reduces power to any wheel that is "Slipping" (spinning too fast), ensuring maximum grip on slippery surfaces.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential RPM Analysis**: Comparing the rotational speeds of two independent axes to identify mechanical imbalances or loss of friction.
*   **Active Power Modulation**: Dynamically adjusting duty cycles in real-time to "Match" a target speed across multiple actuators.
*   **Slip Ratios**: Understanding the percentage of "Slip" allowed before a control system intervenes (e.g., a 10% difference is normal for turns, but 50% indicates a skid).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x L9110S Motor Drivers**
*   **2x DC Motors**
*   **2x IR Optical Encoders** (Left & Right)
*   **OLED Display** (For Traction HUD)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Encoder Left** | GP14 | Input (IRQ) | Pulse Counter L |
| **Encoder Right** | GP15 | Input (IRQ) | Pulse Counter R |
| **Motor Left IA** | GP16 | Output | Speed Control L |
| **Motor Right IA**| GP17 | Output | Speed Control R |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `absolute_difference`
*   From **Logic**, drag `if_differential_exceeds`
*   From **Variables**, drag `left_speed`, `right_speed`

### 7. Variables
*   **rpm_l**: Integer (The calculated revolutions per minute of the left drive wheel).
*   **rpm_r**: Integer (The calculated revolutions per minute of the right drive wheel).
*   **l_duty**: Integer (The current 16-bit PWM duty cycle assigned to the left motor).
*   **r_duty**: Integer (The current 16-bit PWM duty cycle assigned to the right motor).
*   **target_speed**: Integer (The baseline cruise speed required for the mission).
*   **is_tc_active**: Boolean (A status flag that becomes TRUE when the differential logic is actively overriding user power to stop a skid).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Dual-Speed Acquisition**
1.  From **Comm**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Initialize** Pin **14** (Left) and Pin **15** (Right) encoder inputs.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Snap** **100ms** to provide a high-speed sampling window.
3.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Calculate** **rpm_l** and **rpm_r** based on the pulse delta.

**Phase B: Slip Detection Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Compare** the ratio between **rpm_l** and **rpm_r**.
5.  **If Left > Right * 1.3**: From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_tc_active** to **TRUE** (Left wheel skidding).
6.  **If Right > Left * 1.3**: **Set** **is_tc_active** to **TRUE** (Right wheel skidding).
7.  **Else**: **Set** **is_tc_active** to **FALSE** (Traction maintained).

**Phase C: Recovery & Power Modulation**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_tc_active** is **TRUE**.
9.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **-**. **Subtract** **5000** from the slipping wheel's duty cycle.
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_tc_active** is **FALSE**.
11. From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Set** to **+**. **Ramp** both motors back toward **target_speed**.
12. From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Update** Pin **16** and Pin **17** with the corrected traction values.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The robot begins driving forward on a uniform surface, with both motors receiving equal power and maintaining synchronized RPMs.
2.  **Process**: The system independently monitors the rotational velocity of each wheel. If one wheel begins to spin significantly faster than the other, the Traction Control logic identifies a "Loss of Grip" event.
3.  **Output**: To regain control, the Pico instantly reduces the torque to the slipping wheel, allowing the mechanical tires to reconnect with the surface.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the skid is neutralized, the software gently ramps the power back up to full mission speed, ensuring the robot remains on its intended heading without spinning out.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Dual-Speed Acquisition Setup
l_count, r_count = 0, 0
def handle_l(p): global l_count; l_count += 1
def handle_r(p): global r_count; r_count += 1

# Connect high-speed IRQ to both wheels
machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN).irq(machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handle_l)
machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN).irq(machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handle_r)

# Motor Drivers
ml = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
mr = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17))
ml.freq(1000)
mr.freq(1000)

l_duty, r_duty = 35000, 35000
TARGET = 35000
SLIP_LIMIT = 1.3 # Trigger TC if one wheel is 30% faster

print("--- ACTIVE TRACTION CONTROL ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Slip Detection Data Sampling
    lc_start, rc_start = l_count, r_count
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
    # Calculate delta pulses per 100ms
    rpm_l = (l_count - lc_start) 
    rpm_r = (r_count - rc_start)
    
    # Phase C: Recovery & Power Modulation Logic
    is_tc_active = False
    
    # Detection: Left slip
    if rpm_l > (rpm_r * SLIP_LIMIT) and rpm_r > 5:
        l_duty -= 4000 # Aggressive reduction
        is_tc_active = True
        print(">> TC INTERVENTION: LEFT SLIP <<")
    
    # Detection: Right slip
    elif rpm_r > (rpm_l * SLIP_LIMIT) and rpm_l > 5:
        r_duty -= 4000
        is_tc_active = True
        print(">> TC INTERVENTION: RIGHT SLIP <<")
    
    else:
        # Smooth Recovery to Target Speed
        if l_duty < TARGET: l_duty += 500
        if r_duty < TARGET: r_duty += 500
        
    # Safety Clamp
    l_duty = max(0, min(65535, l_duty))
    r_duty = max(0, min(65535, r_duty))
    
    # Apply corrected power
    ml.duty_u16(int(l_duty))
    mr.duty_u16(int(r_duty))
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Cross-Talk**: If the IR encoders are too close together, the light from one might trigger the other. Use physical dividers (electrical tape) to isolate the sensors.
*   **Threshold Static**: If you set `SLIP_LIMIT` to `1.05` (5%), the system will constantly intervene due to tiny manufacturing differences in the tires. Use `1.2` to `1.3` for a stable drive.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cornering Logic**: Disable Traction Control when the robot is intentionally turning (e.g. `is_turning = True`), otherwise the system will try to "Fix" the turn because the outside wheel naturally spins faster!
---

## 1. Project 1341: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Adaptive Visibility & Contrast Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Smart Display" that automatically adjusts its contrast based on ambient light levels using an LDR (Light Dependent Resistor), ensuring the screen is readable in bright sunlight and comfortable in pitch-dark rooms.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **PWM for Analog Voltage**: Using a high-frequency PWM signal (GP16) smoothed into a DC voltage to control the LCD's "V0" (Contrast) pin, replacing the manual blue potentiometer.
*   **Inverse Scaling**: Realizing that as ambient light increases, we need to adjust the contrast voltage in the opposite direction to maintain readability.
*   **Contrast Response Curves**: Mapping 16-bit LDR readings (0-65535) to the useful range of the LCD's V0 pin.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD** (with V0 pin accessible)
*   **LDR (Light Dependent Resistor)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor** (Voltage Divider)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Setup** | GP26 | Analog Input | Light Sensor |
| **Contrast V0**| GP16 | PWM Output | Replaces Potentiometer |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`
*   From **Math**, drag `map_value`
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_duty`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`

### 7. Variables
*   **light_raw**: Integer (The direct ADC conversion from the LDR circuit, range 0-65535).
*   **contrast_duty**: Integer (The 16-bit PWM value calculated to set the LCD's liquid crystal alignment).
*   **lux_percentage**: Integer (The normalized brightness value displayed on the screen for the operator).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Ambient Light Sensing**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Initialize** Pin **26** to monitor the LDR voltage divider.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the sensor output into **light_raw**.
3.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Convert** **light_raw** into a human-readable **lux_percentage**.

**Phase B: Dynamic Contrast Mapping**
4.  From **Math**, drag `map_value`. **Input** the **light_raw** (0-65535).
5.  **Configure Range**: Set **From Low: 0, From High: 65535** to **To Low: 40000, To High: 65000**.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Assign** the mapped result to **contrast_duty**.
7.  **Logic Check**: Ensure the range never hits 0, as an LCD with 0 contrast is invisible to the user.

**Phase C: Display & Output Sync**
8.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `set_pwm_duty`. **Update** Pin **16** (V0 Contrast Pin) using **contrast_duty**.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Display** `"BRIGHT: "` + **lux_percentage** on the first line.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Display** `"PWM: "` + **contrast_duty** on the second line.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Pause** for **200ms** to provide a smooth, flicker-free transition.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the I2C bus and sets GP16 as a high-frequency PWM source acting as a digital-to-analog converter for the contrast pin.
2.  **Process**: The system constantly polls the LDR. When a flashlight is shone on the sensor, the input voltage rises, signaling that the environment is bright.
3.  **Output**: The mapping engine calculates a higher PWM duty cycle, which increases the liquid crystal opacity to maintain readability against the glare.
4.  **Repeat**: The loop ensures that whether the user moves into a dark basement or out into the sun, the display remains clear without manual adjustment.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
# Assume lcd is initialized

# Phase A: Sensing & Control Setup
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
contrast_pwm = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
contrast_pwm.freq(1000) # High freq to avoid flicker

# Standard LCD initialization (simplified)
# import I2C_LCD (Assuming library is present)
# lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

print("--- ADAPTIVE VISIBILITY HUB ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Light Acquisition logic
    light_raw = ldr.read_u16()
    lux_percentage = int((light_raw / 65535) * 100)
    
    # Phase C: Dynamic Mapping & Application
    # Maps ambient light to specific LCD liquid crystal bias voltage
    # Note: Values are inverted - brighter light needs 'stronger' PWM
    # From Low: 0, From High: 65535 to To Low: 40000, To High: 65000
    contrast_duty = int((light_raw / 65535) * (65000 - 40000) + 40000)
    
    # Apply to V0 (Replaces physical potentiometer)
    contrast_pwm.duty_u16(contrast_duty)
    
    # Visual Feedback
    # lcd.move_to(0,0); lcd.putstr(f"AMB: {lux_percentage}%")
    # lcd.move_to(0,1); lcd.putstr(f"PWM: {contrast_duty}")
    
    print(f"Sensing: {lux_percentage}% | Target PWM: {contrast_duty}", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Potentiometer Conflict**: You must disconnect the blue potentiometer from the V0 pin before connecting GP16, otherwise they will fight over the voltage.
*   **Voltage Limits**: The V0 pin usually expects <1V. A 3.3V PWM signal from the Pico might need a simple Resistor-Capacitor (RC) filter to become smooth DC.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Night Mode**: Add a button that forces the backlight OFF and the contrast to a "Reading Mode" preset for usage at night.

## 1. Project 1342: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Packet Decoding & Hex/ASCII Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a mini "Protocol Analyzer" that monitors incoming serial data and displays it in two separate formats simultaneously: raw Hexadecimal codes on the top row and their corresponding ASCII text characters on the bottom row.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Base Conversion (Hex)**: Converting raw binary data (0-255) into its 2-digit Hexadecimal representation (00-FF) for compact display.
*   **Character Mapping**: Using the `chr()` function to translate numerical bytes into human-readable letters (or dots `.` for non-printable codes).
*   **Buffer Slicing**: Managing a fixed 16-character "Window" to ensure the display doesn't overflow when receiving long data packets.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **USB Cable** (for Serial Feed)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |
| **VCC** | 5V | Power | For 1602 LCD |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Internet**, drag `pico_uart_read`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_to_hex`
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_to_ascii`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `lcd_print`

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_packet**: String (The raw incoming byte-stream captured from the serial interface).
*   **hex_view**: String (The processed text representing the machine-level base-16 numerical data).
*   **ascii_view**: String (The human-readable translation of the packet, filtered for printable characters).
*   **byte_count**: Integer (The total length of the received data used for display truncation).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Data Packet Ingestion**
1.  From **Comm**, drag `pico_uart_init`. **Initialize** the serial port to **9600 baud**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if `sys.stdin` contains pending data.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the incoming text into **raw_packet**.

**Phase B: Hexadecimal Conversion**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_for_each`. **Iterate** through the first **5 bytes** of the packet.
5.  From **Text**, drag `convert_to_hex`. **Transform** each numerical byte into a **2-digit uppercase hex** string.
6.  From **Text**, drag `string_join`. **Append** a space between hex codes to create **hex_view**.

**Phase C: Protocol HUD Visualization**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_clear`. **Wipe** the screen of old data before every refresh.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** **hex_view** to Row **1**.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare** byte values to the ASCII range (32-126).
10. From **Text**, drag `char_from_code`. **Action**: Populate **ascii_view** with characters or replacement dots (`.`).
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** **ascii_view** to Row **2**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters a passive listening state, monitoring the serial buffer for external data packets issued by a master computer.
2.  **Process**: Upon reception, the system performs a "Dual-Path" analysis, simultaneousy decoding the bits into machine-readable Hexadecimal and user-readable ASCII.
3.  **Output**: The LCD acts as a localized "Protocol HUD," mapping the complex binary relationships onto a split-screen interface.
4.  **Repeat**: By clearing the display and re-polling the buffer every 100ms, the system provides a real-time stream of data as it crosses the digital interface.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import sys

# Phase A: Data Ingestion Setup
# Standard LCD init logic (Mocked for reference)
# i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
# lcd = I2C_LCD(i2c)

print("--- PROTOCOL ANALYZER HUB ACTIVE ---")
print("Listening for 5-byte packets...")

while True:
    # Phase B: Hex/ASCII Conversion Engine
    # Check if data is waiting in the input buffer
    if sys.stdin in utime.select.select([sys.stdin], [], [], 0)[0]:
        raw_packet = sys.stdin.read(5)
        
        hex_view = ""
        ascii_view = ""
        
        for char in raw_packet:
            byte = ord(char)
            # Format to uppercase hex with padding
            hex_view += "{:02X} ".format(byte)
            
            # Phase C: Protocol HUD Filter
            # Standard printable ASCII range check
            if 32 <= byte <= 126:
                ascii_view += chr(byte) + "  "
            else:
                ascii_view += ".  "
        
        # Display update
        # lcd.clear()
        # lcd.move_to(0,0); lcd.putstr(f"HEX: {hex_view}")
        # lcd.move_to(0,1); lcd.putstr(f"ASC: {ascii_view}")
        print(f"H: {hex_view} | A: {ascii_view}")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Buffer Bloat**: If you send 100 characters, the `hex_view` string will be 300 characters long. The LCD will only show the first 16 and discard the rest. Always truncate your data variables.
*   **Non-Printables**: Sending "Enter" (byte 10 or 13) without checking for printable ranges will often cause the LCD to display a weird "custom character" or garbage. Always use the `.` placeholder for control codes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Scroll**: Implement a "Left-to-Right" shift so that as new bytes come in, the oldest hex codes slide off the screen.
