## 1. Project 1343: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Energy Conservation & Screensaver Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automatic power-management system for a standard LCD, which monitors user inactivity and turns off the power-hungry backlight after 60 seconds, instantly waking up when a physical interface button is pressed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Idle Timing**: Using `ticks_ms()` to track the duration since the last user interaction.
*   **Power States**: Managing two distinct operational modes: "Active" (Backlight ON) and "Sleep" (Backlight OFF).
*   **Interaction DETECTION**: Polling or using interrupts to detect button presses and reset the inactivity timer.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **1x Push Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Wake Button** | GP14 | Input (Pull-Up) | Resets Timer |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `ticks_ms`
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `lcd_backlight_off` and `lcd_backlight_on`
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `is_button_pressed`
*   From **Math**, drag `subtraction`

### 7. Variables
*   **last_activity_ms**: Integer (The timestamp in milliseconds recorded during the most recent user interaction).
*   **IDLE_TIMEOUT**: Integer (The duration of inactivity allowed before the display enters power-save mode).
*   **is_sleeping**: Boolean (A status flag tracking whether the backlight is currently disabled).
*   **elapsed_time**: Integer (The calculated difference between the current time and the last recorded interaction).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Interaction Polling**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_digital_input`. **Initialize** Pin **14** with an **Internal Pull-Up** resistor for the wake button.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`. **Capture** the startup time into **last_activity_ms**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if Pin **14** is **LOW** (Button Pressed).
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **last_activity_ms** to the current time whenever a press is detected.

**Phase B: Inactivity Analysis**
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Subtract** **last_activity_ms** from the current `ticks_ms()` result.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this result into **elapsed_time**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **elapsed_time** is **GREATER THAN** **60000** (60 seconds).

**Phase C: Power State Transition**
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_backlight`. **Action**: Set to **OFF** if the timeout is exceeded and **is_sleeping** is FALSE.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Flip** **is_sleeping** to **TRUE** to lock the state.
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_sleeping** is **TRUE** AND the button is pressed.
11. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_backlight`. **Action**: Set to **ON** and **Reset** **is_sleeping** to **FALSE**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system initializes the LCD and begins a countdown timer, assuming the user is present and active.
2.  **Process**: The Pico constantly monitors the button state. Every time the button is clicked, the "Inactivity Clock" is reset to zero.
3.  **Output**: If 60 seconds pass without a single interaction, the Pico concludes the device is unattended and terminates the high-current backlight LED.
4.  **Repeat**: The system remains in a low-power "Sleep" state until a physical trigger is detected, at which point it instantly restores visibility and restarts the cycle.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Persistence & Control Setup
# Setup interaction button on GP14
button = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Timer variables
last_activity_ms = utime.ticks_ms()
IDLE_TIMEOUT = 60000 # 60 Seconds
is_sleeping = False

# Standard LCD init
# import I2C_LCD (Assuming library is present)
# lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

print("--- SMART SLEEP HUB ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Interaction Monitoring logic
    # Check for physical 'Wake' or 'Activity' trigger
    if button.value() == 0:
        last_activity_ms = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Immediate wake sequence if currently dark
        if is_sleeping:
            # lcd.backlight_on()
            is_sleeping = False
            print(">> SYSTEM WAKE: USER DETECTED <<")
            
    # Phase C: Inactivity Analysis Logic
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    elapsed_time = utime.ticks_diff(now, last_activity_ms)
    
    # Check for timeout threshold
    if elapsed_time > IDLE_TIMEOUT:
        if not is_sleeping:
            # lcd.backlight_off()
            is_sleeping = True
            print(">> ENTERING POWER SAVE: INACTIVITY DETECTED <<")
            
    utime.sleep_ms(100) # Efficiency poll
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Backlight Spamming**: Sending the `backlight_off` command every single loop will clog the I2C bus. Always use a boolean flag (like `is_sleeping`) to ensure the command is only sent once per transition.
*   **Millisecond Logic**: Ensure your timeout is `60000` for one minute. Using `60` will make the screen turn off almost instantly.
*   **Missing Pull-Up**: A button without a pull-up resistor will "float," causing the screensaver to randomly wake up or stay awake forever due to electrical noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Warning Indicator**: 5 seconds before the screen turns off, display a small `"OFF IN 5s"` message in the corner. If the user presses the button then, clear the message and stay awake!
---
## 1. Project 1344: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Custom EQ Visualizer & CGRAM Bitmaps". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a professional "Spectrum Analyzer" for a 16x2 LCD by defining custom 5x8 pixel bitmaps to create vertical bars of varying heights, allowing the display of dynamic audio levels that exceed standard character limitations.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **CGRAM (Character Generator RAM)**: Understanding the 64-byte memory space on the LCD controller where users can store up to 8 custom-defined glyphs.
*   **Vertical Mapping**: Scaling a high-resolution 16-bit analog signal into a discrete 0-8 level visual representation.
*   **Bitmap Arrays**: Learning how to use binary numbers (0x0F, etc.) to "Draw" pixels row-by-row into the LCD's hardware buffer.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **Analog Microphone** (MAX4466 or similar simulated with Potentiometer)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic Signal** | GP26 | Analog Input | Audio Waveform |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |
| **VCC/GND** | 5V / GND | Power | Rail Connection |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_define_char`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **audio_raw**: Integer (The peak-to-peak voltage reading captured from the microphone circuit).
*   **bar_id**: Integer (The index of the custom character, ranges 0-7, representing bar heights).
*   **peak_level**: Integer (The current height of the EQ bar, scaled to 0-8 increments).
*   **sample_rate**: Integer (The timing window used to capture the audio envelope).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: CGRAM Character Definition**
1.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_define_char`. **Initialize** 8 custom shapes representing bar heights 1 (bottom line only) to 8 (solid block).
2.  **Define Shapes**: Use byte lists like `[0,0,0,0,0,0,0,31]` for Level 1 and `[31,31,31,31,31,31,31,31]` for Level 8.
3.  **Check**: Load these into slots **0 through 7** during the startup sequence.

**Phase B: Signal Normalization**
4.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Poll** Pin **26** at high speed to capture the audio envelope.
5.  From **Math**, drag `map_value`. **Input** **audio_raw** (0-65535).
6.  **Scale**: Set output range to **0 to 7**. This maps the volume directly to our custom character indices.

**Phase C: Dynamic EQ Rendering**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_clear`. **Wipe** the display buffer before each frame.
8.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat`. **Print** the custom character corresponding to the current **peak_level** across multiple columns.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** a decorative `" [ EQ ACTIVE ] "` label on Row 2.
10. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **50ms** to stabilize the visual movement.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico loads the 8 custom "Level" bitmaps into the LCD's hardware registers, preparing the screen for non-standard graphics.
2.  **Process**: The system samples the microphone. A loud sound causes the ADC value to spike, which the mapping engine instantly translates into a "High Bar" ID.
3.  **Output**: The LCD renders the specific custom character (e.g., Slot 7 for full volume) across the signal track.
4.  **Repeat**: The rapid refresh rate allows the bars to bounce in sync with the environment, creating a real-time wave visualization.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: CGRAM Character definitions
# Definitions for 8 levels of vertical bar height
bar_maps = [
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x00, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F]),
    bytearray([0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F, 0x1F])
]

# Standard LCD check
# import I2C_LCD (Assuming library is present)
# lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

# Phase B: Initialization
for i in range(8):
    # lcd.custom_char(i, bar_maps[i])
    pass

mic = machine.ADC(26)

print("--- SPECTRUM ANALYZER ONLINE ---")

while True:
    # Read Volume Envelope
    audio_raw = mic.read_u16()
    
    # Map to 0-7 custom char indices
    peak_level = int((audio_raw / 65535) * 7)
    
    # Phase C: Dynamic EQ Rendering logic
    # lcd.move_to(0,0)
    # lcd.putstr(chr(peak_level) * 16) # Fill whole row
    # lcd.move_to(0,1)
    # lcd.putstr(" [ EQ ACTIVE ]  ")
    
    print(f"Signal: {audio_raw} | Render Char ID: {peak_level}", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```
def draw_progress_bar(percent):
    # Convert 0-100% to 0-16 blocks
    blocks = int(percent / 100 * 16)
    lcd.move_to(0,1)
    # chr(0) is our custom character
    lcd.putstr(chr(0) * blocks + " " * (16 - blocks))

print("BOOTING SYSTEM...")
lcd.move_to(3,0)
lcd.putstr("LOADING...")

for i in range(101):
    draw_progress_bar(i)
    utime.sleep_ms(50)

lcd.clear()
lcd.putstr("SYSTEM READY")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing `chr(0)`**: You cannot just type "0" to show your custom character. You must use the `chr()` function to tell the LCD to look in its memory for that specific ID.
*   **Memory Overwrite**: If you define a character at index 0, then later define another one at index 0, your first character will disappear from the screen and be replaced by the new one.

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **CGRAM Overwrite**: The LCD only has 8 slots for custom characters. If you try to define a 9th character, it will overwrite Slot 0. Plan your EQ bars accordingly!
*   **Flicker**: Clearing the screen every frame causes terrible flashing. Instead of `lcd.clear()`, overwrite only the specific positions with spaces or new bars.
*   **Bitmap Inversion**: Most LCDs represent a '1' as a black pixel and '0' as blank. If your EQ looks like a photo negative, flip your binary bits.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Horizontal Peak Hold**: Add a "Falling Dot" algorithm where the highest level reached remains on screen for 1 second before slowly dropping down.
*   **Multi-Track Display**: Split Row 1 and Row 2 into two separate channels (Left/Right) for a true stereo visualization.

## 1. Project 1345: Automated Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Double Buffering & Graphics Optimizations". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional-grade "Differential Rendering Engine" that minimizes I2C bus traffic by comparing the desired screen state against a virtual local buffer and only sending commands for the specific characters that have changed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential Rendering**: Reducing electrical load by identifying "Deltas" (changes) between frames rather than wiping and re-writing the entire screen.
*   **Memory Buffering**: Using a Python list or string to store a virtual copy of the "Last Known State" of the hardware.
*   **I2C Bus Efficiency**: Understanding how reducing the number of `putstr()` commands increases the overall frame rate (FPS) of the interface.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | High-Speed Sync |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | High-Speed Sync |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **Variables**: Drag `set_variable`.
*   **Text**: Drag `get_char_at`.
*   **Logic**: Drag `pico_if`.
*   **Loops**: Drag `pico_for_range`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_content**: String (The text currently visible on the physical LCD hardware).
*   **target_buffer**: String (The new text generated by the code that needs to be displayed).
*   **char_index**: Integer (The sliding pointer used to scan the buffer for deltas).
*   **is_dirty**: Boolean (A flag that becomes TRUE if the hardware is out of sync with the software).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Virtual Buffer Management**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **current_content** with 32 spaces (`" "` * 32).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **target_buffer** with any starting message.

**Phase B: Delta Compression Logic**
3.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_for_range`. **Scan** through indices **0 to 31** (representing both rows).
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare** character at **char_index** in **target_buffer** against **current_content**.
5.  **Identify Change**: If they are different, mark the frame as **is_dirty**.

**Phase C: Selective Pixel Refresh**
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Execute** if and only if **is_dirty** is TRUE.
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_move`. **Position** the cursor at either Row 1 or Row 2 based on the index.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Write** ONLY the single character that changed.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** the local **current_content** so the next frame comparison is accurate.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico creates two copies of the screen: one in its memory and one in the physical LCD's hardware registers.
2.  **Process**: In every loop, the Pico generates a new "Target" frame (e.g., updating a clock). Instead of clearing the whole screen, it compares every character slot to the one already there.
3.  **Output**: If only the "Seconds" digit changed, the Pico sends exactly one command to update that single digit.
4.  **Repeat**: This "Surgical Update" method avoids the annoying flicker caused by `clear()` and allows for much faster, professional-grade animations.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Buffer Management Setup
# Global string trackers representing R1+R2 (32 chars)
screen_hw = " " * 32 

# Mock LCD init
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

def update_display(new_content):
    global screen_hw
    # Ensure content is padded to 32 chars
    new_content = (new_content + " " * 32)[:32]
    
    # Phase B: Delta Identification logic
    for i in range(32):
        if new_content[i] != screen_hw[i]:
            # Selective Refresh: Calculate LCD coordinates
            row = 1 if i >= 16 else 0
            col = i % 16
            
            # Send I2C command ONLY for this changed byte
            # lcd.move_to(col, row)
            # lcd.putstr(new_content[i])
            
            print(f"HW UPDATE: pos({col},{row}) -> '{new_content[i]}'")
            
            # Update local sync tracker
            # Convert string to list for item assignment, then back
            temp_list = list(screen_hw)
            temp_list[i] = new_content[i]
            screen_hw = "".join(temp_list)

print("--- OPTIMIZED BUFFER ENGINE ACTIVE ---")

counter = 0
while True:
    # Generate a dynamic string
    # Only a few chars change every second
    target = f"COUNT: {counter}      PROCESSED OK"
    
    # Phase C: Dispatch Optimized Frame
    update_display(target)
    
    counter += 1
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Buffer Alignment**: If your string is 33 characters long, the 33rd character might wrap or cause a crash. Always use `[:32]` to truncate your buffers to the physical screen size.
*   **Static Loops**: Forgetting to update the `screen_hw` tracker means the Pico will think the screen *always* needs updating, defeating the entire purpose of the optimization.
*   **Coordinate Math**: Remember that indices 0-15 are Row 0, and 16-31 are Row 1. Using `i % 16` is essential for finding the correct column.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Marquee Scroll**: Implement a horizontal scroller using this buffer engine. You'll notice much smoother movement compared to a standard `clear()` and `print()` loop!
---

## 1. Project 1346: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Interrupt Vectors & Global Overrides". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-priority "System Override" where a hardware interrupt (IRQ) instantly halts background operations to display a critical "EVACUATE" protocol on the LCD, ensuring immediate visibility during safety events.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hardware Interrupts (IRQ)**: Understanding how the processor can temporarily stop the main loop to handle an urgent task immediately.
*   **Volatile State Flags**: Using a boolean variable that the main code constantly checks to see if it should enter "Safe Mode."
*   **Global Overrides**: Design patterns for ensuring safety critical messages cannot be cleared by background sensors or data logs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **Emergency Pull Station** (or Momentary Push Button)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pull Station** | GP14 | Input (Pull-Up) | Emergency Trigger |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Message Link |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Message Link |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_backlight`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_emergency**: Boolean (A global flag that triggers the transition from Normal Monitor to Alert Mode).
*   **alarm_message**: String (The high-priority text displayed during a safety trigger).
*   **flash_counter**: Integer (A simple iterator used to toggle the backlight frequency during the alarm).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Interrupt Vector Configuration**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_pin_init`. **Configure** Pin **14** as an **Input** with an **Internal Pull-Up**.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `set_pin_interrupt`. **Link** Pin **14** to a custom handling function.
3.  **Set Trigger**: Configure the interrupt to fire on the **Falling Edge** (when the button is pressed/pulled).

**Phase B: System Lockout Sequence**
4.  Inside the interrupt function, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_emergency** to **TRUE**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Wrap** your main loop's normal data code inside a check that only runs if **is_emergency** is **FALSE**.
6.  **Action**: This effectively "Freezes" all other sensors when the alarm is active.

**Phase C: High-Priority UI Rendering**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_clear`. **Wipe** everything the moment the flag is detected.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** `"!! EVACUATE !!"` to both rows.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_backlight_toggle`. **Flicker** the screen at **100ms** intervals.
10. From **Logic**, drag `pico_loop`. **Block** the system in this state until a manual reset is performed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors environmental data normally, displaying standard telemetry on the LCD.
2.  **Process**: A user pulls the emergency station. The hardware interrupt instantly pauses the main code, regardless of where it is (even inside a `wait` block).
3.  **Output**: The interrupt handler flips the safety flag. The main loop detects the change and switches to the "Critical HUD."
4.  **Repeat**: The screen flashes aggressively and displays the evacuation prompt, locking out all other functions until a technician resets the system.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Interrupt Setup
# Emergency trigger on GP14
trigger = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
is_emergency = False

# Mock LCD
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

def handle_emergency(pin):
    global is_emergency
    # Phase B: Lockout Flag Assignment
    # This runs instantly on button press
    is_emergency = True

# Attach interrupt
trigger.irq(handler=handle_emergency, trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING)

print("--- SAFETY MONITOR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    if not is_emergency:
        # Normal operations
        # lcd.move_to(0,0); lcd.putstr("ROOM TEMP: 22C")
        # lcd.move_to(0,1); lcd.putstr("STATUS: NORMAL")
        print("Monitoring... [SAFE]", end="\r")
        utime.sleep(1)
    else:
        # Phase C: High-Priority UI logic
        # Clear screen once
        # lcd.clear()
        print("\n!!! EMERGENCY DETECTED: LOCKOUT ACTIVE !!!")
        
        while True:

            # High-intensity alarm loop
            # lcd.move_to(0,0); lcd.putstr("!! EVACUATE !!")
            # lcd.backlight_off()
            # utime.sleep_ms(100)
            # lcd.backlight_on()
            # utime.sleep_ms(100)
            
            # Simulated visual feedback
            print("ALERT: [EVACUATE]  ", end="\r")
            utime.sleep_ms(200)

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Prints in IRQs**: If you try to print "EMERGENCY!!" inside the `handle_emergency` function, the Pico will likely crash. Interrupts cannot share the same resources as the main loop (like the Serial/UART buffer).
*   **Variable Scope**: Ensure `is_emergency` is declared as `global` inside the ISR, otherwise the main loop will never see the change.
*   **Debounce Noise**: Sometimes pulling a physical station causes "Contact Bounce," firing the interrupt multiple times. Since we only set the flag to TRUE, this isn't fatal, but be aware for other IRQ tasks.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Remote Reset**: Add a second button on GP15 that, when held for 5 seconds, resets the `is_emergency` flag and returns the system to normal monitoring.

## 1. Project 1347: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Ticker Tape Animation & Substring Windowing". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional-grade "News Ticker" that smoothly scrolls a long text string across the LCD by using a mathematical sliding window to extract and display 16-character segments in rapid succession.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sliding Window Logic**: Extracting a fixed-length portion of a larger string based on an incrementing offset.
*   **String Padding**: Using leading and trailing spaces to ensure text "enters" and "exits" the screen edges gracefully.
*   **Wrap-Around Math**: Using the `modulo` operator or length comparisons to restart the scroll once the end of the text is reached.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Serial Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Serial Communication |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_slice`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`.
*   From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_forever`.
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`.

### 7. Variables
*   **long_text**: String (The complete message, including the leading/trailing spaces for entrance/exit).
*   **scroll_offset**: Integer (The current starting character position of the display window).
*   **display_window**: String (The 16-character slice that is currently pushed to the LCD hardware).
*   **scroll_speed**: Integer (The delay in milliseconds between each shift, controlling the reading speed).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Message Padding**
1.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Combine** 16 spaces + `"SYSTEM STATUS: ALL SYSTEMS NOMINAL"` + 16 spaces.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this into **long_text**.
3.  **Check**: The 16 spaces at the start ensure the first letter of your text appears to "Enter" from the right side of the screen.

**Phase B: Sliding Window Extraction**
4.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_slice`. **Extract** characters from **scroll_offset** to **scroll_offset + 16**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the result into **display_window**.
6.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Increment** **scroll_offset** by 1 inside the loop.

**Phase C: Marquee Animation Logic**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_move`. **Move** cursor to (0,0).
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** the **display_window**.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **scroll_offset** is greater than the total length of **long_text**.
10. **Reset**: Set **scroll_offset** back to **0** if the end is reached for a continuous loop.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **150ms** for a natural reading pace.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico prepares the text by wrapping it in "Whitespace Buffers" so it doesn't just pop into existence on the screen.
2.  **Process**: In a high-speed loop, the system extracts a 16-character "Snapshot" of the text. As the offset increases, the characters appear to slide left.
3.  **Output**: The LCD updates every 150ms. To the human eye, this creates the illusion of a smooth ticker-tape marquee.
4.  **Repeat**: Once the final character of the text (and the trailing spaces) has passed, the offset resets, creating a seamless, infinite announcement loop.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Message Preparation
padding = " " * 16
raw_msg = "SYSTEM UPDATE: DATABASE SYNC COMPLETED - NO ERRORS DETECTED"
long_text = padding + raw_msg + padding

# Standard LCD init
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

scroll_offset = 0
scroll_speed = 150 # ms delay

print("--- NEWS TICKER ENGINE ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Sliding Window Logic
    # Extract the 16 characters destined for the screen
    display_window = long_text[scroll_offset : scroll_offset + 16]
    
    # Phase C: Marquee Animation
    # lcd.move_to(0,0)
    # lcd.putstr(display_window)
    
    # Simulated Serial Ticker
    print(f"[{display_window}]", end="\r")
    
    # Shift the window
    scroll_offset += 1
    
    # Reset scroll when message finish
    if scroll_offset > (len(long_text) - 16):
        scroll_offset = 0
        print("\n(Ticker Round-Trip Complete)")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(scroll_speed)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Choppy Scrolling**: If your delay is too long (e.g., 500ms), the text will "Jump" rather than scroll. Use 100-200ms for a smooth marquee.
*   **Index Errors**: Attempting to slice a string past its final character can cause a crash. Always ensure your `if scroll_offset > len` check happens before the next slice operation.
*   **Display Jitter**: If you use `lcd.clear()` inside the scroll loop, the screen will flicker. Only use `move_to(0,0)` and overwrite the old text with the new window.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Bi-Directional Scroll**: Add a button that, when pressed, reverses the increment (`scroll_offset -= 1`), making the text fly backward!

## 1. Project 1348: Smart Advanced LCD Display (I2C) System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Remote Pager & FIFO Circular Buffers". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a multi-message "Information Hub" that receives text via UART and manages them in a Fixed-Length Inbox (Circular Buffer), allowing user navigation through the history without overflowing the Pico's RAM.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **FIFO (First-In, First-Out)**: A buffer management technique where the oldest received data is deleted first when memory is full.
*   **Circular Navigation**: Designing UI logic that allows a user to cycle through a list while enforcing boundary limits (Min/Max).
*   **In-Place Rendering**: Updating only the relevant lines of the LCD while preserving the header or footer status bar.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **2x Push Buttons** (Navigation)
*   **USB Cable** (Serial Input)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Next Button** | GP14 | Input (Pull-Up) | Next Message |
| **Prev Button** | GP15 | Input (Pull-Up) | Previous Msg |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Internet**, drag `pico_uart_any`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `pico_list_add` and `pico_list_remove`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_is_pressed`.
*   From **Math**, drag `min` and `max`.

### 7. Variables
*   **inbox_list**: List (The dynamic array storing strings received from the serial interface).
*   **view_index**: Integer (The pointer to the specific message currently displayed on the LCD).
*   **max_capacity**: Integer (The constant limit of 5-10 messages allowed in the buffer).
*   **new_msg**: String (The temporary storage for the most recently arrived UART packet).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: FIFO Buffer Management**
1.  From **Lists**, drag `create_empty_list`. **Assign** it to **inbox_list** with an initial `"NO MESSAGES"` entry.
2.  From **Internet**, drag `pico_uart_any`. **Poll** the buffer in the background.
3.  From **Text**, drag `pico_uart_read`. **Append** the result to **inbox_list**.

**Phase B: Overflow Control Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **length** of **inbox_list** is **GREATER THAN** **5**.
5.  From **Lists**, drag `pico_list_remove`. **Delete** the item at index **0** (the oldest message) to make room.
6.  **Action**: This ensures that even if 1000 messages are sent, the Pico only remembers the latest 5.

**Phase C: Circular Navigation HUD**
7.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_is_pressed`. **Check** Pin **14** (Next) and **15** (Prev).
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Increment/Decrement** **view_index** using `min(view_index + 1, length - 1)` and `max(view_index - 1, 0)`.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** **inbox_list[view_index]** to Row 1.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** a counter like `"Msg: "` + (**view_index** + 1) to Row 2.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the UART port and creates an empty memory "Stack" for incoming communications.
2.  **Process**: Messages arrive via USB from a computer. The Pico strips the metadata and pushes the text into the list.
3.  **Action**: If the user wants to see older messages, they click the navigation buttons. The code handles the list indices safely to prevent crashes.
4.  **Output**: The LCD acts as a window into the list, showing one message at a time but allowing the user to "Scroll" through time.
5.  **Efficiency**: The FIFO system automatically prunes old data, keeping the device responsive and stable during long-running sessions.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import sys

# Phase A: Buffer Management Setup
inbox_list = ["- EMPTY INBOX -"]
view_index = 0
MAX_CAPACITY = 5

# Navigation Buttons
btn_next = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
btn_prev = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Mock LCD
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

print("--- PAGER HUB ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Incoming Serial Handler (Asynchronous)
    if sys.stdin in utime.select.select([sys.stdin], [], [], 0)[0]:
        new_msg = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        if new_msg:
            # Clean initial filler
            if inbox_list[0] == "- EMPTY INBOX -":
                inbox_list.pop(0)
            
            # FIFO Push logic
            inbox_list.append(new_msg)
            if len(inbox_list) > MAX_CAPACITY:
                inbox_list.pop(0) # Remove oldest
            
            # Auto-jump to newest message
            view_index = len(inbox_list) - 1
            print(f"RECEIVE: {new_msg}")
            
    # Phase C: Navigation logic
    if btn_next.value() == 0:
        view_index = min(view_index + 1, len(inbox_list) - 1)
        utime.sleep_ms(200) # Input debounce
        
    if btn_prev.value() == 0:
        view_index = max(view_index - 1, 0)
        utime.sleep_ms(200)
        
    # Periodic Rendering
    current_msg = inbox_list[view_index]
    # lcd.move_to(0,0); lcd.putstr(current_msg[:16])
    # lcd.move_to(0,1); lcd.putstr(f"MSG {view_index+1}/{len(inbox_list)}      ")
    
    print(f"LCD: [{view_index+1}] {current_msg}", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Zero-Index Trap**: In coding, the first item is `0`, but users expect to see `Message 1`. Always add `+1` to your index when displaying it to the user.
*   **Blocking UART**: If you use `input()` instead of the non-blocking `sys.stdin` check, the whole program will freeze until a message is sent.
*   **Memory Leak**: If you don't use the `pop(0)` logic (FIFO), the list will grow infinitely until the Pico runs out of RAM and crashes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Blink on Arrival**: When a new message is added to the list, turn ON the LCD backlight for 5 seconds to alert the user that there is something new to read.

## 1. Project 1349: Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"I2C Bus Load Analyzer & Performance Profiling". At the end of this project, you will be able to scientifically measure the exact microsecond cost of I2C transmissions using high-resolution performance timers, allowing you to calculate the "Bus Load" percentage and optimize your code for high-frequency robotics and motor control loops.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Performance Profiling**: The engineering practice of identifying "Bottlenecks" in code by measuring execution duration.
*   **I2C Propagation Latency**: Understanding that every string sent over I2C has a physical time cost (approx. 1ms per char).
*   **CPU Utilization Efficiency**: Calculating what percentage of a scheduled loop (e.g., 50ms) is consumed by display updates.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Transmission Bus |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Transmission Bus |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_us`.
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **start_ticks_us**: Integer (The high-resolution timestamp captured immediately before a performance-heavy command).
*   **execution_time_us**: Integer (The total microsecond duration consumed by the I2C bus write operation).
*   **bus_efficiency**: Float (The calculated percentage representing the busy-time versus the available idle-time in a 50ms loop).
*   **cycle_limit**: Integer (The target loop duration in milliseconds used as the baseline for efficiency math).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Microsecond Performance Profiling**
1.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_us`. **Capture** the startup time into **start_ticks_us** at the very top of the loop.
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_move`. **Position** the cursor at (0,0) to prepare for a "Worst-Case" full-string write.
3.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Action**: Send a long string like `"PROFILING BUS..."`.

**Phase B: I2C Overhead Extraction**
4.  Immediately after the print block, drag `pico_ticks_us`. **Capture** the end time.
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Subtract** **start_ticks_us** from the end time.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this delta into **execution_time_us**.

**Phase C: Efficiency Telemetry Viz**
7.  From **Math**, drag `division`. **Calculate** the load percentage: `(execution_time_us / 50000) * 100`.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this into **bus_efficiency**.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** Row 1: `"TIME: "` + **execution_time_us** + `"us"`.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** Row 2: `"LOAD: "` + **bus_efficiency** + `"% "`.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **50ms** to provide a stable monitoring window.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes its high-precision internal counter, which increments every millionth of a second.
2.  **Process**: The code takes a "Time-Stamp" before and after talking to the LCD. The gap between these two timestamps is the "Electrical Tax" paid for using the I2C bus.
3.  **Output**: The system performs real-time math to convert these raw ticks into a "System Health" percentage.
4.  **Repeat**: By observing these numbers, a developer can see exactly how much their screen slows down their robot's brain, allowing them to optimize for higher speed.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Performance Timer Setup
# Standard LCD init
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

CYCLE_MS = 50 # Baseline for 20Hz loop
print("--- BUS PERFORMANCE ANALYZER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Capture pre-transmission timestamp
    start_ticks_us = utime.ticks_us()
    
    # Phase B: Performance-Heavy Transmission logic
    # lcd.move_to(0,0)
    # lcd.putstr("TRANSMITTING DATA...")
    # Simulated delay for calculation (Replace with actual LCD call)
    utime.sleep_ms(15) 
    
    # Capture post-transmission delta
    now = utime.ticks_us()
    execution_time_us = utime.ticks_diff(now, start_ticks_us)
    
    # Phase C: Efficiency Telemetry Viz
    # Calculate % of 50ms (50,000us) loop consumed by display
    bus_efficiency = (execution_time_us / (CYCLE_MS * 1000)) * 100
    
    # Display Stats
    # lcd.move_to(0,1)
    # lcd.putstr(f"{execution_time_us}us | {bus_efficiency:2.1f}%")
    
    print(f"Cycle Usage: {execution_time_us}us ({bus_efficiency:2.1f}%)", end="\r")
    utime.sleep_ms(CYCLE_MS)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Millisecond Ambiguity**: Don't confuse `ticks_ms` with `ticks_us`. I2C commands often take 5-20 milliseconds; measuring them in ms will give you very rounded, inaccurate numbers. Always use microseconds (us) for profiling.
*   **Total vs Delta**: If you print `ticks_us()` directly, you'll see a number in the millions. Always subtract the "Start" from the "Now" to get the duration.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **I2C Speed Hack**: Change your I2C frequency from `100000` (Standard) to `400000` (Fast Mode) and run the analyzer again. You should see the `execution_time_us` drop by nearly 75%!

## 1. Project 1350: Advanced Advanced LCD Display (I2C) Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Level Menu Architecture & State Hierarchies". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a professional-grade "Menu Tree" where a single screen navigates through nested options (e.g., Settings -> Display -> Backlight), using a robust "Parent/Child" state machine.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Nested State Machines**: Managing a hierarchy where your buttons do different things depending on which "Level" of the menu you are currently on.
*   **Resource Mapping**: Using a single variable (`menu_level`) to dynamically swap which list of text items is rendered to the screen.
*   **Recursive Returns**: Designing "Back" buttons that revert the system to the previous parent state without losing context.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C LCD**
*   **3x Push Buttons** (Up, Down, Enter)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Up Button** | GP14 | Input (Pull-Up) | Prev Item |
| **Down Button** | GP15 | Input (Pull-Up) | Next Item |
| **Enter Button** | GP16 | Input (Pull-Up) | Select / Back |
| **LCD SDA** | GP4 | I2C Data | Communication |
| **LCD SCL** | GP5 | I2C Clock | Communication |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag `get_item_from_list`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`.

### 7. Variables
*   **menu_level**: Integer (The depth tracker: 0 for Main Menu, 1 for Sub-Menu).
*   **selected_index**: Integer (The current item ID the cursor `>` is pointing to).
*   **menu_options**: List (A dynamic array containing the strings for the current level).
*   **parent_state**: Integer (The previous menu level used to facilitate 'Back' functionality).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Hierarchical State Definition**
1.  From **Lists**, drag `create_list`. **Define** `main_items = ["MOTORS", "SENSORS", "EXIT"]`.
2.  From **Lists**, drag `create_list`. **Define** `motor_items = ["START", "STOP", "BACK"]`.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **menu_level** to **0** and **selected_index** to **0**.

**Phase B: State-Based Input Routing**
4.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_is_pressed`. **Check** if Down (GP15) is triggered.
5.  From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`. **Increment** **selected_index** and use `modulo` to wrap back to 0.
6.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_is_pressed`. **Check** if Enter (GP16) is triggered.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `if_else`. **Action**: If level is **0** and index is **1**, set **menu_level** to **1** (diving into sub-menu).

**Phase C: Dynamic UI Orchestration**
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_clear`. **Wipe** the screen whenever the level or index changes.
9.  From **Lists**, drag `get_item`. **Select** the current string from either the main or motor list based on **menu_level**.
10. From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Prepend** a `"> "` character to the row matched by **selected_index**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **200ms** to debounce physical button jitter.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters the "Root" of the menu tree, assuming Row 0 is the starting point.
2.  **Process**: The system polls the buttons. Browsing Up/Down only changes the `selected_index` value, not the `menu_level`.
3.  **Action**: When "Enter" is pressed on a specific item, the Pico "Drills Down" by changing the `menu_level` variable. The background data source instantly swaps from the main list to the sub-list.
4.  **Output**: The LCD renders the new list, making the user feel like they have entered a new "Folder" or "Page."
5.  **Repeat**: By providing a "BACK" option in the sub-menu that resets `menu_level` to 0, the system provides a seamless navigation experience.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Hierarchical State Setup
# [0] = Main Menu, [1] = Sub Menu
menus = [
    ["MOTORS", "SENSORS", "DEBUG"],
    ["SPEED H", "SPEED L", "BACK"]
]

menu_level = 0
selected_index = 0
btn_dn = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
btn_ent = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Mock LCD
# import I2C_LCD; lcd = I2C_LCD.init()

print("--- HIERARCHICAL UI ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Input Routing logic
    # Scroll through options
    if btn_dn.value() == 0:
        selected_index = (selected_index + 1) % 3
        # lcd.clear()
        utime.sleep_ms(200)
        
    # Enter/Back navigation
    if btn_ent.value() == 0:
        if menu_level == 0: # We are in Main
            if selected_index == 0: # Selected Motors
                menu_level = 1
                selected_index = 0
        elif menu_level == 1: # We are in Sub-menu
            if selected_index == 2: # Selected 'BACK'
                menu_level = 0
                selected_index = 0
        
        # lcd.clear()
        utime.sleep_ms(200)

    # Phase C: UI Orchestration rendering
    current_list = menus[menu_level]
    
    # Render two lines of the menu
    for i in range(len(current_list)):
        prefix = "> " if selected_index == i else "  "
        # lcd.move_to(0, i)
        # lcd.putstr(f"{prefix}{current_list[i]}")
        
    # Simulated Serial Menu
    indicator = f"LVEL:{menu_level} | CURSOR:{selected_index} | {current_list[selected_index]}"
    print(indicator, end="\r")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hard-Coded Modulos**: If your Main Menu has 3 items and your Sub-Menu only has 2, using `(index + 1) % 3` while in the sub-menu will cause an "Index Out of Range" crash. Always use `index % len(current_list)`.
*   **The 'Clear' Flicker**: If you `lcd.clear()` every frame, the screen will jitter. Only clear the screen when a button is actually pressed.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Breadcrumb HUD**: Display a small icon in the top right corner that shows the current level (e.g., `[1]` or `[M]`) so the user never gets lost in a deep menu tree.

## Batch 136: Advanced Data Logging 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1351: Advanced Data Logging Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Software-Level RAID 1 (Mirroring)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Atomic Redundancy System" that writes sensor telemetry to two separate files simultaneously, ensuring that if a sectors-level failure or power loss corrupts your main log, a secondary "Backup" file remains untouched and valid.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Atomic Redundancy**: The practice of performing two identical write operations to different physical or logical storage locations to prevent data loss.
*   **File Buffer Flushing**: Understanding that data isn't "Safe" on the SD card until the `flush()` or `close()` command forces the Pico to empty its RAM buffer.
*   **I/O Concurrency**: Managing multiple open "File Handles" without causing name-space collisions or memory overflows.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **Any Analog/Digital Sensor** (e.g., LDR or Potentiometer)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SD CS** | GP13 | CS | Slave Select |
| **SD SCK** | GP10 | SPI | Clock |
| **SD MOSI** | GP11 | SPI | Data Out |
| **SD MISO** | GP12 | SPI | Data In |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **primary_handle**: Object (The active link to the `main.log` file on the storage medium).
*   **backup_handle**: Object (The mirrored link to the `backup.log` file used for emergency recovery).
*   **log_payload**: String (The formatted sensor data combined with a timestamp for storage).
*   **cycle_count**: Integer (A counter used to trigger a mandatory `flush` every 5 writes for maximum safety).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Redundant File Linking**
1.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Open** `"main.log"` in **Append** mode (`"a"`) and assign to **primary_handle**.
2.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Open** `"backup.log"` in **Append** mode and assign to **backup_handle**.
3.  **Check**: Ensure you verify that the SD card is mounted before attempting the open command.

**Phase B: Parallel Write Execution**
4.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Create** a CSV entry using `time` + `sensor_value`.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this into **log_payload**.
6.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Write** **log_payload** to **primary_handle**.
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Write** **log_payload** to **backup_handle**.

**Phase C: Atomic Data Sync**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **cycle_count** reaches **10**.
9.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_flash_sync`. **Perform** a `flush()` on both handles to physically commit the bits to the SD card.
10. From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** **cycle_count** to **0**.
11. From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait`. **Pause** for **60 seconds** to avoid excessive Flash wear.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico establishes two independent channels to the SD card.
2.  **Process**: In every loop, the same piece of data is sent down both channels.
3.  **Output**: Two identical files grow at the same rate.
4.  **Repeat**: If the user pulls the power mid-write, the `flush` logic ensures that at least 90% of the data is recoverable from either file.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import os

# Phase A: Redundant Setup
# Assume SD card is mounted at /sd
def mirror_log(data):
    # Opening files in 'append' mode
    # In a real RAID system, these might be on different physical cards
    try:
        # Phase B: Parallel Write Execution logic
        with open("/sd/main.log", "a") as f1:
            f1.write(data + "\n")
            f1.flush() # Commit to disk
            
        with open("/sd/backup.log", "a") as f2:
            f2.write(data + "\n")
            f2.flush() # Atomic backup
            
        print(f"SYNC OK: {data}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"LOG ERROR: {e}")

print("--- RAID-1 MIRROR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Simulated sensor data
    timestamp = utime.time()
    val = machine.ADC(26).read_u16()
    
    payload = f"{timestamp},{val}"
    
    # Execute mirrored write
    mirror_log(payload)
    
    # Phase C: Cycle Pacing
    utime.sleep(60)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ghost Writes**: If you don't call `f.close()` or `f.flush()`, the data stays in the Pico's temporary RAM. If the power goes out, the file will be 0 bytes long even if the code "ran" for hours.
*   **Resource Exhaustion**: Opening too many files at once can consume the Pico's limited memory. Always use `with open(...)` to ensure files are closed automatically.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Corruption Checksum**: Add a project from later in this batch (CRC8) to the mirror log. This makes your mirroring system "Self-Aware" of any bit-rot!

## 1. Project 1352: Advanced Data Logging Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Stream Scanning & Memory Optimization". At the end of this project, you will be able to process massive text files (multiple megabytes) by implementing a "Line-by-Line" parser that searches for specific error patterns without crashing the Pico's tiny RAM.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Streaming I/O**: The practice of reading data in small increments rather than loading a whole file into memory.
*   **Linear Search Patterns**: Iterating through a dataset entry-by-entry to identify specific targets.
*   **O(n) Complexity**: Understanding that searching a file takes longer as the file grows, but memory usage stays the same if you stream.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module** (Loaded with a text file containing "ERROR" markers)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_line_by_line`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_contains`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_break_loop`.

### 7. Variables
*   **search_pattern**: String (The specific keyword, e.g., `"ERROR"`, that the Pico is hunting for).
*   **current_line_num**: Integer (A counter used to tell the user exactly where the error was found).
*   **match_found**: Boolean (A flag that stops the scan once the first error is discovered).
*   **file_pointer**: Object (The reference to the open file stream).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Stream Buffer Configuration**
1.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Open** `"system.log"` in **Read** mode (`"r"`).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **current_line_num** to **1**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **match_found** to **FALSE**.

**Phase B: Linear Pattern Scanning**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_for_each_line`. **Connect** it to the open file handle.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the current line contains **search_pattern**.
6.  **Action**: If a match is found, set **match_found** to **TRUE** and record the line number.

**Phase C: Discovery Notification**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Show** the results if **match_found** is TRUE.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Output** `"FOUND ERROR AT: "` + **current_line_num**.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_break`. **Stop** the search immediately to save time.
10. **Validation**: Run the code on a small file first. Then, try a file with 10,000 lines. The Pico should handle both with the same speed and memory stability.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico marks the "Cursor" at the start of the SD card's file sector.
2.  **Process**: Instead of saying "Give me the whole file," it says "Give me just one line." It looks at it, decides if it's an error, and then throws it away to make room for the next line.
3.  **Output**: If a needle is found in the haystack, the Pico "Screams" the location via the serial port or LCD.
4.  **Repeat**: This loop continues until it hits the "EOF" (End of File) or finds what it's looking for.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import os

# Phase A: Stream Buffer Setup
SEARCH_TERM = "CRITICAL_ERROR"
target_file = "/sd/telemetry.txt"

def scan_for_errors(filename):
    line_count = 0
    found = False
    
    print(f"--- SCANNING {filename} ---")
    
    try:
        # Phase B: Linear Pattern Scanning logic
        # 'with' ensures the file is closed even if the scan crashes
        with open(filename, "r") as f:
            for line in f:
                line_count += 1
                
                if SEARCH_TERM in line:
                    # Phase C: Discovery Notification
                    print(f"!! {SEARCH_TERM} FOUND ON LINE {line_count} !!")
                    print(f"CONTENT: {line.strip()}")
                    found = True
                    break # Stop scanning after first find
                    
        if not found:
            print("SCAN CLEAN: NO ERRORS DETECTED.")
            
    except OSError:
        print("ERROR: LOG FILE MISSING.")

# Start scan
scan_for_errors(target_file)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **RAM Bloat**: Using `f.read()` or `f.readlines()` on a 2MB file will cause a `MemoryError` and crash the system. Always iterate over the file object directly (`for line in f`).
*   **Stripping Newlines**: When comparing strings, remember that `line` will include the `\n` character at the end. Use `.strip()` if you are trying to match an exact string.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Pattern Search**: Modify the code to search for a list of errors (e.g., `["TEMP_HIGH", "LOW_BATT"]`) and print a separate summary for each one found.
---
    print(f"ANALYSIS COMPLETE: {count} PTS")
    print(f"MAX: {max_t}C | MIN: {min_t}C | AVG: {total/count:.1f}C")
except OSError:
    print("Log file not found.")
```

## 1. Project 1353: Advanced Data Logging Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Storage Management & Log Rotation". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated "Self-Cleaning" storage system that monitors the SD card's remaining capacity and triggers a "Rotate" sequence (deleting oldest logs to make room for new ones) once a critical threshold is reached.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Log Rotation Strategies**: Managing finite storage by treating files as a "Circular Queue" where the tail (oldest data) is pruned.
*   **Disk Usage Inversion**: Calculating remaining percentage by subtracting used blocks from the total block count reported by the OS.
*   **Atomic Renaming**: Using file-system operations to safely move data from `active.log` to `backup.log` without losing the original file structure.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_get_free_space`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_delete_file`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_rename_file`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **free_space_kb**: Integer (The calculated remaining storage capacity in Kilobytes).
*   **rotation_threshold**: Integer (The safety limit, e.g., 1024KB, that triggers the cleanup sequence).
*   **current_log_name**: String (The active file path currently receiving sensor telemetry).
*   **archive_log_name**: String (The destination path for the 2nd-most-recent log file).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Storage Capacity Polling**
1.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_stat_vfs`. **Query** the root mount point (`/` or `/sd`).
2.  From **Math**, drag `multiplication`. **Calculate** `FreeBlocks * BlockSize` to get raw bytes.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the Kilobyte equivalent into **free_space_kb**.

**Phase B: Automatic Rotation Sequence**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare** **free_space_kb** against **rotation_threshold** (set to 1024).
5.  **Trigger Condition**: If space is low, enter the cleanup routine. If not, proceed with normal writing.
6.  **Safety**: Always check for the existence of an old backup file before attempting to overwrite it.

**Phase C: File System Maintenance**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_delete_file`. **Remove** the index 2 log (`data_oldest.log`).
8.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_rename_file`. **Move** `data_old.log` to `data_oldest.log`.
9.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_rename_file`. **Move** `data.log` to `data_old.log`.
10. From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Create** a fresh, empty `data.log` for the next cycle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico boots and checks the SD card's health.
2.  **Process**: Every time a sensor reading is ready, the Pico first asks: "Is there room for this?"
3.  **Action**: If the card is nearly full, the Pico performs a "Surgical Deletion," dumping the oldest 50% of the logs.
4.  **Result**: The user never has to manually format their SD card; the system automatically maintains its own "Stable Orbit" of storage usage.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import os

# Phase A: Capacity Polling logic
def check_storage():
    # statvfs returns (block_size, fragment_size, total_blocks, free_blocks...)
    s = os.statvfs('/sd')
    free_kb = (s[0] * s[3]) / 1024
    return free_kb

THRESHOLD_KB = 1024 # 1MB Limit

def rotate_logs():
    # Phase B: Rotation Sequence logic
    print("WARNING: STORAGE LOW. ROTATING LOGS...")
    
    # Phase C: Maintenance execution
    files = os.listdir('/sd')
    
    if "data_old.log" in files:
        os.remove("/sd/data_old.log")
        
    if "active.log" in files:
        os.rename("/sd/active.log", "/sd/data_old.log")
        
    # Re-initialize main log
    with open("/sd/active.log", "w") as f:
        f.write("--- NEW LOG SESSION START ---\n")

while True:
    current_free = check_storage()
    print(f"FREE SPACE: {current_free:.1f} KB")
    
    if current_free < THRESHOLD_KB:
        rotate_logs()
        
    # Standard logging loop
    # ... sensor code here ...
    machine.idle()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Wipeout**: Using `os.remove()` without a backup system will delete all your data! Always rename files (`active` -> `old`) so you have at least one previous session saved.
*   **Block Size Math**: Remember that `os.statvfs` gives counts of *blocks*, not bytes. You must multiply by `s[0]` (block size) or your "Free Space" calculation will be wildly incorrect.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Compressed Rotation**: Before deleting an old log, try to "Pack" it or strip unnecessary metadata to save 20-30% more space before the final purge.

## 1. Project 1354: Advanced Data Logging Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"I/O Handshaking & Disk Activity Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to synchronize a physical LED with the internal SPI transactions of the SD card, creating a professional "Disk Activity Light" (DAL) that provides instant visual confirmation that data is being successfully committed to storage.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Coupling**: Linking the physical state of an LED to the discrete software event of an I/O write transaction.
*   **SPI Traffic Monitoring**: Understanding that while the Pico's CPU is sending data to the SD card, it is blocked from other tasks.
*   **User Interface Logic**: Why visual feedback is critical in embedded systems where there is no monitor to show "File Saved."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **1x LED**
*   **1x 220-330Ω Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Activity LED** | GP18 | Output | Digital Pulse |
| **Resistor** | Inline | Passive | Current Limit |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_pin_init`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_led_toggle`.
*   From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **activity_led_pin**: Integer (The GPIO pin ID associated with the Disk Activity Light).
*   **write_start_time**: Integer (The timestamp used to measure how long the LED stays on during a flush).
*   **write_duration_ms**: Integer (The total millisecond "Blink" time for each logging event).
*   **is_disk_busy**: Boolean (A logic flag used to prevent the user from pulling the card while the light is ON).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Visual Indicator Handshake**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_pin_init`. **Configure** Pin **18** as an **Output**.
2.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Ensure** the LED is **OFF** at boot-up.
3.  **Check**: Test the LED with a single blink in `on start` to verify wiring.

**Phase B: Operational State Coupling**
4.  Immediately before your `pico_write_file` block, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **18** to **HIGH**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **is_disk_busy** to **TRUE**.
6.  **Action**: This visual cues the user: "Do not eject the SD Card now!"

**Phase C: State Release Cycle**
7.  Immediately after the `f.close()` or `f.flush()` block, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **18** to **LOW**.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **is_disk_busy** to **FALSE**.
9.  **Validation**: Run the code. You should see a sharp "Flicker" on the LED every time the Pico saves data. If the LED stays ON, your file might be stuck open!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: The Pico is waiting for a sensor trigger. The LED is dark.
2.  **Action**: The sensor threshold is hit. The Pico prepares the data packet.
3.  **Process**: The LED turns on. The CPU enters the SPI transaction. Bits are physically pushed to the SD card.
4.  **Reaction**: The file system reports a successful write. The CPU exits the write routine.
5.  **Result**: The LED turns off. The user knows the data is safe, and they can now safely power down the device if needed.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Activity Indicator Setup
led = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
is_disk_busy = False

# SD Card setup assumed
# mount_sd()

def write_data_safe(msg):
    global is_disk_busy
    
    # Phase B: Coupling State (LED ON)
    led.value(1)
    is_disk_busy = True
    
    try:
        with open("/sd/log.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(msg + "\n")
            # Force I/O to finish
            f.flush() 
    except:
        print("I/O Failure!")
        
    # Phase C: Release State (LED OFF)
    led.value(0)
    is_disk_busy = False

print("--- ACTIVITY MONITOR READY ---")

while True:
    # Simulate data event
    write_data_safe(f"{utime.time()},TELEMETRY_DAT")
    utime.sleep(5) # Slow loop to see the blinks
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Inverted Logic**: If your LED is wired to 3.3V (Anode), sending a `1` will turn it OFF. Ensure your `1` means "Active" for the user.
*   **Blink Timing**: If the write happens in 1 millisecond, the blink might be too fast to see! You may want to add `utime.sleep_ms(50)` before turning it OFF just to make the blink visible to humans.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Error Blinker**: If a write fails (inside an `except` block), make the LED blink rapidly 5 times to alert the user that the SD card is full or missing!

## 1. Project 1355: Automated Advanced Data Logging Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Binary Data Serialization & High-Density Storage". At the end of this project, you will be able to optimize storage utilization by converting human-readable text data into compact "Binary Blobs" using the `struct` library, allowing you to store 60% more data in the same number of SD card sectors.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Data Serialization**: The process of translating data structures (like floats or integers) into a format that can be stored (raw bytes) and reconstructed later.
*   **Fixed-Width Formats**: Understanding that binary storage uses a predictable number of bytes per entry (e.g., 4 bytes for a float), making it easier to skip or jump to specific records.
*   **Compression vs Efficiency**: Recognizing that binary is more efficient than ASCII text because it represents numbers in their native computer format rather than as a series of character strings.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **Potentiometer** (Simulating high-speed sensor data)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 | Analog Input |
| **SD MISO** | GP16 | SPI0 RX |
| **SD CS** | GP17 | Chip Select |
| **SD SCK** | GP18 | SPI0 SCK |
| **SD MOSI** | GP19 | SPI0 TX |
| **VCC** | 3.3V | Power |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System**, drag `pico_load_library`.
*   From **System**, drag `pico_struct_pack`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_binary`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_binary`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_flash_sync`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_sensor_data**: Float (The analog value read from the potentiometer, ranging from 0.0 to 100.0).
*   **packed_byte_buffer**: Buffer (The raw 4-byte string generated by the serialization engine).
*   **serialization_format**: String (The schema string, e.g., `'f'`, which tells the Pico to treat the data as a 32-bit float).
*   **record_timestamp**: Integer (The millisecond timestamp used as a secondary data point in the binary record).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Binary Schema Definition**
1.  From **System**, drag `pico_load_library`. **Import** the `struct` module.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Define** **serialization_format** as `'f'` (float).
3.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_binary`. **Target** the file `"telemetry.bin"` in **Append Binary** mode (`"ab"`).

**Phase B: Buffer Packing Logic**
4.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Convert** the raw reading to a percentage (float).
5.  From **System**, drag `pico_struct_pack`. **Pass** the float value into the packing function using your schema.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** the resulting 4 bytes into **packed_byte_buffer**.

**Phase C: Compact Storage Stream**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_binary`. **Commit** the buffer to the open file handle.
8.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_flash_sync`. **Flush** the stream to ensure the binary blob is physically saved.
9.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Trigger** the loop every **10ms** to test high-density logging.
10. **Validation**: Open the file on a PC using a Hex Editor. You should see raw hex bytes (e.g., `0x41B00000`) instead of readable text numbers like `"22.0"`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the binary file pointer.
2.  **Process**: The system takes a decimal number and "Smashes" it into its internal 4-byte representational form.
3.  **Output**: The SD card receives raw electrical bits without any text formatting overhead.
4.  **Repeat**: Because the file is binary, there are no commas or spaces, resulting in the smallest possible file size for the given data.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import struct

# Phase A: Binary Schema Definition
# 'f' = 32-bit float (4 bytes)
FORMAT = "f" 
ADC_PIN = machine.ADC(26)

print("--- BINARY LOGGER ACTIVE ---")

def log_binary_point(val):
    # Phase B: Buffer Packing Logic
    # Convert float to raw bytes
    try:
        packed_data = struct.pack(FORMAT, val)
        
        # Phase C: Compact Storage Stream
        # Note the 'ab' (Append Binary) mode
        with open("/sd/data.bin", "ab") as f:
            f.write(packed_data)
            
        return True
    except:
        return False

while True:
    # Read and normalize sensor
    reading = ADC_PIN.read_u16() / 655.35 # 0.0 - 100.0
    
    if log_binary_point(reading):
        print(f"Packed: {reading:.2f} -> 4 Bytes Written", end="\r")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(10) # 100Hz recording
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The ASCII Trap**: Writing text numbers to a binary file defeats the purpose. Ensure you are using `struct.pack` and not `str(val)`.
*   **Endianness Confusion**: Different computers read bytes in different orders (Big-Endian vs Little-Endian). Use `<f` in your format string to force "Little-Endian" for maximum compatibility with Windows PCs.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Deep Decoding**: Create a second script on the Pico that reads the file in `'rb'` mode and uses `struct.unpack(FORMAT, data)` to turn the binary blobs back into human-readable numbers on the LCD!

## 1. Project 1356: Advanced Data Logging Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Checksum Validation & Corrupt Data Rejection". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Data Integrity Guard" that calculates a CRC8 (Cyclic Redundancy Check) for every log entry, allowing the Pico to automatically detect and discard corrupted records during playback.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Data Integrity**: Ensuring that the data read from storage is exactly the same as the data originally written.
*   **Checksum Algorithms**: Mathematical functions that generate a unique "Signature" for a block of text based on its characters.
*   **Error Propagation**: Understanding how bit-flips (caused by power spikes or SD wear) can ruin a scientific dataset if not filtered out.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SD MISO** | GP16 | SPI0 RX |
| **SD CS** | GP17 | Chip Select |
| **SD SCK** | GP18 | SPI0 SCK |
| **SD MOSI** | GP19 | SPI0 TX |
| **VCC** | 3.3V | Power |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_calc_checksum`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_split`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_line`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **data_payload_string**: String (The actual sensor value or message you want to preserve).
*   **calculated_checksum**: Integer (The integrity signature generated by the Pico before writing).
*   **transmitted_checksum**: Integer (The signature extracted from the file during a read operation).
*   **is_payload_valid**: Boolean (A flag that determines if the data line should be used or trashed).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Checksum Algorithm Initialization**
1.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Construct** your data line: `"TEMP,25.5"`.
2.  From **Math**, drag `pico_calc_checksum`. **Generate** an 8-bit sum for that string.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** this into **calculated_checksum**.

**Phase B: Integrity Signature Attachment**
4.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Append** the checksum to the data using a pipe: `"TEMP,25.5|44"`.
5.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Save** this "Signed" line to the SD card.
6.  **Action**: This creates a "Digital Seal" that protects that specific line of data.

**Phase C: Predictive Verification Logic**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_line`. **Retrieve** a line from the file.
8.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_split`. **Separate** the message from the checksum number.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Recalculate** the sum of the message. If it matches the number from the file, mark **is_payload_valid** as TRUE.
10. **Validation**: Manually modify one character in the file using a PC. Try to read it with the Pico. The system should correctly flag the line as "CORRUPT" and ignore it.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico prepares a data string for storage.
2.  **Process**: The system runs a math test on the string to generate a "Security Code."
3.  **Action**: Both the string and the code are saved together.
4.  **Verification**: During playback, the Pico does the same math test again. If the answer doesn't match the code on the disk, it knows the "Bits" have been altered.
5.  **Result**: The user gets a clean dataset, free from the random "Glitch" lines that plague unvalidated systems.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine

# Phase A: Checksum Algorithm
def generate_checksum(s):
    # Simple 8-bit Summation Checksum
    return sum(s.encode()) % 256

def write_verified_log(msg):
    # Phase B: Integrity Signature Attachment logic
    sig = generate_checksum(msg)
    signed_line = f"{msg}|{sig}"
    
    # Standard append write
    try:
        with open("/sd/secure.log", "a") as f:
            f.write(signed_line + "\n")
    except OSError:
        print("Write Failure")

def read_verified_log(filename):
    print("--- STARTING INTEGRITY AUDIT ---")
    try:
        with open(filename, "r") as f:
            for line in f:
                # Phase C: Predictive Verification logic
                parts = line.strip().split("|")
                if len(parts) < 2: continue
                
                payload = parts[0]
                try:
                    expected_sig = int(parts[1])
                except:
                    print("Malformed Checksum")
                    continue
                
                # Recalculate and compare
                if generate_checksum(payload) == expected_sig:
                    print(f"VALID: {payload}")
                else:
                    print(f"!!! CORRUPTION DETECTED: {payload} !!!")
    except OSError:
        print("Log file missing.")

# Test Cycle
write_verified_log("BATTERY:85%")
read_verified_log("/sd/secure.log")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Incomplete Split**: If the line doesn't have a `|` character (maybe from a partial write), `parts[1]` will cause a crash. Always check `len(parts) == 2` before verifying.
*   **Encoding Errors**: Ensure you use `.strip()` on the line before splitting, otherwise the invisible `\n` at the end of the file will change the checksum calculation!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Self-Healing Log**: If a line is corrupt, try to find the second copy from your Project 1351 (Mirroring) and use that to "Repair" the broken file automatically!
