## 1. Project 1357: Advanced Data Logging Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Geospatial Data Visualization & KML Formatting". At the end of this project, you will be able to store location data in the Keyhole Markup Language (KML) format, allowing you to record GPS coordinates on your Pico and immediately visualize your route in professional GIS software like Google Earth.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **XML/KML Structure**: Understanding the hierarchical nature of tag-based languages where data is nested within `<headers>` and `<tags>`.
*   **Coordinate Precision**: Managing floating-point variables to ensure that sub-meter movements are accurately represented in mapping software.
*   **Asset Finalization**: The technical requirement of "Closing" a markup file with footer tags (e.g., `</kml>`) so that parsers treat the file as a valid document.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **GPS Module** (Simulated with static coordinates for this project)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SD MISO** | GP16 | SPI0 RX |
| **SD CS** | GP17 | Chip Select |
| **SD SCK** | GP18 | SPI0 SCK |
| **SD MOSI** | GP19 | SPI0 TX |
| **VCC** | 3.3V | Power |
| **GND** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_xml_tag_wrap`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_to_file`.
*   From **Math**, drag `arithmetic_operator`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **coord_lat**: Float (The Latitude coordinate ranging from -90 to +90).
*   **coord_lon**: Float (The Longitude coordinate ranging from -180 to +180).
*   **kml_header**: String (The XML declaration and KML namespace definition required at the top of the file).
*   **placemark_data**: String (The formatted XML block containing the specific coordinates for a single point).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: XML Structural Scaffolding**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Define** **kml_header** as `<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?><kml xmlns="http://www.opengis.net/kml/2.2"><Document>`.
2.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Create** `"route.kml"` and write the **kml_header**.
3.  **Check**: Ensure the file is overwritten on boot to clear any previous partial data.

**Phase B: Geodata Translation**
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** your **coord_lat** and **coord_lon** (use simulated floats like `37.422`, `-122.084`).
5.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Construct** the coordinate string: `longitude` + `","` + `latitude` + `",0"`.
6.  From **Text**, drag `pico_xml_tag_wrap`. **Nest** this string inside `<Placemark><Point><coordinates>` tags.

**Phase C: Spatial Asset Finalization**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_to_file`. **Write** the **placemark_data** to the SD card for every location update.
8.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_to_file`. **Write** the footer tags `</Document></kml>` just before powered down.
9.  **Validation**: Connect the SD card to a PC. Double-click `route.kml`. Google Earth should launch and place a "Pin" exactly at the coordinates you logged.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico write a "Legal Header" that tells PCs: "I am a Map File."
2.  **Process**: Every few seconds, the Pico records a new coordinate and wraps it in XML "Clothes" (tags).
3.  **Output**: The SD card grows with a list of valid placemarks.
4.  **Repeat**: Even if the Pico only logs 10 points, the resulting file is a complete, industry-standard geospatial document.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import os

# Phase A: XML Scaffolding
FILE_PATH = "/sd/route.kml"

def init_kml():
    header = '<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>\n'
    header += '<kml xmlns="http://www.opengis.net/kml/2.2">\n<Document>\n'
    with open(FILE_PATH, "w") as f:
        f.write(header)
    print("KML HEADER CREATED")

def log_point(lat, lon):
    # Phase B: Geodata Translation logic
    # KML uses (Lon, Lat, Alt) order
    placemark = f"  <Placemark>\n    <Point>\n      <coordinates>{lon},{lat},0</coordinates>\n    </Point>\n  </Placemark>\n"
    
    # Phase C: Finalization stream
    with open(FILE_PATH, "a") as f:
        f.write(placemark)
    print(f"Logged: {lat}, {lon}")

def close_kml():
    footer = "</Document>\n</kml>"
    with open(FILE_PATH, "a") as f:
        f.write(footer)
    print("KML FILE CLOSED SUCCESSFULLY")

# Simulation
init_kml()
log_point(37.4223, -122.0841) # Google HQ
log_point(37.4225, -122.0845) # Nearby point
close_kml()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Coordinate Swapping**: KML uses `longitude,latitude,altitude`. If you put Latitude first, your point will appear on the wrong side of the world!
*   **The Missing Footer**: If you don't write `</kml>`, Google Earth will report "Parse Error" and refuse to show the map.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Path Tracking**: Instead of `<Placemark>`, use the `<LineString>` tag to draw a continuous line between all your points, showing exactly where your Pico travelled!

## 1. Project 1358: Smart Advanced Data Logging System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Network File Retrieval & Socket Streaming". At the end of this project, you will be able to turn your Pico W into a "Remote Data Server," allowing you to download log files from the SD card using any web browser or FTP client on your local network.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Client-Server Architecture**: Understanding the relationship between a "Client" (your PC) requesting data and a "Server" (the Pico) providing it.
*   **TCP/IP Socket Streaming**: Sending large files by breaking them into "Buffer Chunks" so you don't overwhelm the network chip's memory.
*   **Network Latency**: Managing the time it takes for bits to travel from the SD card, through the WiFi antenna, to your laptop.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`.
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_socket_listen`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_chunk`.
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_socket_send`.

### 7. Variables
*   **wifi_ssid**: String (The name of your local wireless network).
*   **server_socket**: Object (The active listener waiting for incoming file retrieval requests).
*   **file_chunk**: Buffer (A temporary 1KB RAM space used to hold data while it's in transit).
*   **client_address**: String (The IP address of the computer connecting to your Pico).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Network Portal Handshake**
1.  From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`. **Enter** your **wifi_ssid** and password.
2.  From **Network**, drag `pico_socket_init`. **Bind** the server to Port **80** (HTTP).
3.  **Check**: Monitor the serial port until the Pico reports: `"SERVER LIVE AT 192.168.1.XX"`.

**Phase B: Protocol Command Routing**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_forever`. **Listen** for incoming connections using `pico_socket_accept`.
5.  From **Network**, drag `pico_socket_receive`. **Read** the first few bytes.
6.  **Action**: If the request contains `"GET /log"`, prepare to stream the file.

**Phase C: File Payload Streaming**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Open** your log file in **Read Binary** mode (`"rb"`).
8.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while_not_eof`. **Loop** until the whole file is read.
9.  From **Network**, drag `pico_socket_send`. **Push** each **file_chunk** through the WiFi antenna.
10. **Validation**: Open a browser on your PC. Type `http://[PICO_IP]/log`. The browser should immediately prompt you to "Download" your data file.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico joins the network and becomes a "Node" in your smart home.
2.  **Process**: It sits silently (Idle) until it hears a "Knock" on Port 80.
3.  **Action**: It opens the local SD card and starts a "Bucket Brigade," passing 1KB of data at a time from the card to the net.
4.  **Result**: Your sensor data is now accessible from anywhere in the building without ever unplugging the SD card.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import network
import socket
import machine

# Phase A: Network Handshake
SSID = "Your_WiFi"
PASS = "password"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)

# Wait for IP...
addr = socket.getaddrinfo('0.0.0.0', 80)[0][-1]
s = socket.socket()
s.bind(addr); s.listen(1)

print(f"--- SERVER LIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Command Routing logic
    client, c_addr = s.accept()
    request = client.recv(1024)
    
    if b"GET /data" in request:
        # Send HTTP Header
        client.send('HTTP/1.1 200 OK\nContent-Type: text/csv\n\n')
        
        # Phase C: Payload Streaming
        try:
            with open("/sd/log.csv", "rb") as f:
                while True:
                    chunk = f.read(512) # Small chunks for stability
                    if not chunk: break
                    client.send(chunk)
        except:
            client.send("Error reading log.")
            
    client.close()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The RAM Crash**: Trying to load the entire file into a variable (`data = f.read()`) will crash the Pico if the file is larger than 100KB. Always use the `while / read(512)` chunking method.
*   **Socket Blocking**: If the WiFi connection is weak, the `client.send()` command might hang. Use `s.settimeout(5.0)` for professional reliability.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Live Dashboard**: Instead of a download, send an HTML page that uses JavaScript to draw a real-time "Chart" of your logged data directly in the browser!

## 1. Project 1359: Advanced Data Logging Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Storage Surface Calibration & Bad Sector Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a "Stress Test" on your MicroSD card by implementing a block-level verification script that identifies and maps out physically damaged storage sectors, preventing your data logging from crashing in the field.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sector Integrity**: Understanding that Flash memory can physically degrade over time, leading to "Dead Zones" where data cannot be reliably saved.
*   **Write-Read Verification**: The process of writing a known bit-pattern (e.g., `0xAA`) and reading it back immediately to ensure the hardware is working.
*   **Device Blacklisting**: Creating a software "Map" that tells the Pico: "Never write important data to these specific address ranges."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module** (Ideally an old or cheap one for testing)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_raw_block`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_raw_block`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_not_equal`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **block_id**: Integer (The numerical address of the storage sector currently being audited).
*   **test_payload**: String (A fixed pattern of bits, like `"CHECK_123"`, used to challenge the hardware).
*   **read_back_buffer**: String (The actual data returned from the SD card after a write attempt).
*   **bad_sector_count**: Integer (A running tally of failed verification attempts).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Media Surface Polling**
1.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_times`. **Audit** the first **100** blocks of the card.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **bad_sector_count** to **0**.
3.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_led_toggle`. **Blink** on every block to show the scan is active.

**Phase B: Bitwise Verification Sequence**
4.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_raw`. **Push** **test_payload** into the current **block_id**.
5.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **5ms** for the hardware to settle.
6.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_raw`. **Pull** the data back from that same **block_id**.

**Phase C: Defect Registry Maintenance**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare** **read_back_buffer** to **test_payload**.
8.  **Action**: If they are NOT equal, increment **bad_sector_count** and print: `"BLOCK [X] FAILED"`.
9.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`. **Append** the failing **block_id** to a final file called `"blacklist.txt"`.
10. **Validation**: Purposely pull the SD card slightly out during the scan. The Pico should immediately detect multiple "Bad Sectors" and record them in your registry.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico locks onto the storage module.
2.  **Process**: It systematically "Hits" every sector with a specific bit pattern.
3.  **Action**: It checks the "Echo." If the echo is distorted, it marks that physical room as "Closed for Repairs."
4.  **Result**: The user gets a report on the health of their SD card, ensuring critical logs aren't lost to silent hardware failure.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import os

# Phase A: Media Surface Polling
BLOCK_SIZE = 512
TEST_RANGE = 50 # Limit for safety on internal flash

def audit_storage():
    bad_count = 0
    test_pattern = "PICO_BLOCK_STRESS_TEST_01"
    
    print(f"--- STARTING DISK AUDIT: {TEST_RANGE} BLOCKS ---")
    
    for block_num in range(TEST_RANGE):
        filename = f"/sd/test_{block_num}.tmp"
        
        # Phase B: Verification Sequence
        try:
            # Write pattern
            with open(filename, "w") as f:
                f.write(test_pattern)
            
            # Read back
            with open(filename, "r") as f:
                echo = f.read()
                
            # Phase C: Defect Registry
            if echo != test_pattern:
                print(f"!! BLOCK {block_num}: CORRUPT !!")
                bad_count += 1
            
            # Cleanup
            os.remove(filename)
            
        except OSError:
            print(f"!! BLOCK {block_num}: WRITE FAILURE !!")
            bad_count += 1
            
    print(f"SCAN FINISHED. {bad_count} BAD BLOCKS DETECTED.")

# Run utility
audit_storage()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The In-Use Glitch**: Don't run the audit while you are actively logging data. You might accidentally overwrite your own sensor logs during the "Stress Test."
*   **Mounting Logic**: Ensure the SD card is mounted *before* the loop starts, or every block will report as a "Write Failure."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Automatic Skipping**: Modify your main logger (P1351) to read `badblocks.txt` on boot and automatically skip those sectors when saving sensor data!

## 1. Project 1360: Advanced Advanced Data Logging Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Database Engine & Offset-Based Search". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-performance "Micro-DBMS" (Database Management System) on your Pico that uses "Byte Indexing" to jump directly to specific records in a huge file without reading the entire card.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Database Indexing**: Creating a "Map" of where data is physically located in a file (Byte Offsets) to speed up retrieval.
*   **File Seeking**: Moving the "Read Head" directly to a specific byte index (e.g., jump to byte 10,000) instead of reading linearly from byte 0.
*   **O(1) vs O(n) Efficiency**: Why searching a 10MB file takes 1 second with an index, but 120 seconds without one.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_seek_file`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_string_find`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **List**, drag `pico_list_add`.

### 7. Variables
*   **index_map**: List (A collection of byte addresses where specific records start).
*   **query_target**: Integer (The value, such as Temperature > 30, that we want to jump to).
*   **found_record**: String (The raw line extracted from the SD card after a successful "Seek").
*   **search_ms**: Integer (The time taken to perform the query, used for performance benchmarking).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Data Schema Indexing**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **index_map** as an empty list.
2.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`. **Scan** through a log file of 1000 entries.
3.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_get_tell`. **Store** the current byte offset in **index_map** for every 10th record.

**Phase B: Query Processing logic**
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **query_target** to record index **45**.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Calculate** `TargetIndex * AverageRecordLength` to get the target byte.
6.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_seek_file`. **Jump** the file cursor directly to that byte offset.

**Phase C: Result Set Retrieval**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_read_line`. **Grab** the record exactly where the seeker landed.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Verify** that the data is the correct record.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_lcd_print`. **Show** the record and the **search_ms** duration.
10. **Validation**: Search for Record #1, then Record #500. Note how both tasks take the same amount of time. This proves your index is working!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico creates a "Table of Contents" (Index) for your sensor data.
2.  **Process**: When you ask for a specific piece of data, the Pico doesn't read the whole "Book."
3.  **Action**: It looks at the index, finds the "Page Number" (Byte Offset), and "Flipping" the file reader directly there.
4.  **Result**: You can search gigabytes of data on a tiny Pico in milliseconds rather than hours.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Data Schema Indexing
# We store the byte positions where records start
index_offsets = []

def build_index(filename):
    print("INDEXING CONTENT...")
    with open(filename, "r") as f:
        while True:
            # Get current position BEFORE reading
            pos = f.tell()
            line = f.readline()
            if not line: break
            
            # Only index "Critical" records
            if "CRITICAL" in line:
                index_offsets.append(pos)
    print(f"INDEX READY: {len(index_offsets)} POINTS MAPPED.")

def query_database(filename, match_idx):
    # Phase B: Query Processing logic
    if match_idx >= len(index_offsets): return None
    
    start_time = utime.ticks_ms()
    target_pos = index_offsets[match_idx]
    
    with open(filename, "r") as f:
        # Phase C: Result Set Retrieval
        f.seek(target_pos)
        result = f.readline()
        
    end_time = utime.ticks_ms()
    print(f"FETCHED IN {utime.ticks_diff(end_time, start_time)}ms")
    return result

# Setup
# build_index("/sd/history.log")
# r = query_database("/sd/history.log", 2)
# print(f"MATCH: {r}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Seek Offset**: Remember that `f.seek()` moves to a *Byte*, not a *Line*. If your lines have different lengths, you MUST store the exact byte position, not just a line count.
*   **Closing Early**: If you close the file inside the query function but need to read another line, the handle will be dead.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Binary Search Engine**: Combine your Project 1355 (Binary) with this project (Index). Since binary records are ALWAYS the same length, your math for `target_pos` becomes perfectly 100% accurate!

## 1. Project 1361: Advanced Soil Moisture Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"AC Excitation & Electrolysis Prevention". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a professional "Active Sensing" technique that toggles probe polarity (AC) to prevent metal corrosion and ionic build-up, significantly extending the life of your sensors in wet environments.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Electrolysis Suppression**: Understanding how sustained DC current "Eats" metal probes via chemical reactions, and how rapid polarity reversal (AC) cancels this effect.
*   **Ionic Migration**: The movement of charged particles in moist soil that can skew sensor readings if a constant voltage is applied.
*   **Differential Measurement**: Comparing the voltage drop in both "Forward" and "Reverse" directions to calculate a more stable moisture average.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Resistive Soil Probes** (2-prong type)
*   **1x 10kΩ Resistor** (Voltage Divider)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Probe Lead A** | GP14 | Output | Polarity Control 1 |
| **Probe Lead B** | GP15 | Input/ADC | Polarity Control 2 |
| **Resistor** | Inline | Passive | Pull-Down to GND |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **probe_pin_a**: Integer (The GPIO pin controlling the first side of the measurement bridge).
*   **probe_pin_b**: Integer (The ADC-capable pin used to sample the voltage drop).
*   **v_moisture_pos**: Integer (The raw value capture during the "Forward" polarity phase).
*   **v_moisture_neg**: Integer (The raw value captured during the "Reverse" polarity phase).
*   **is_ac_phase_active**: Boolean (A flag that prevents the loop from finishing until a complete AC cycle is recorded).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Polarity Handshake Initialization**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_pin_init`. **Configure** Pin **14** and **15** as **Outputs**.
2.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** both pins to **LOW**.
3.  **Check**: Ensure no current is flowing through the soil before the measurement begins.

**Phase B: Complementary Pulsing Logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **14** to **HIGH** and Pin **15** to **LOW**.
5.  Wait **10ms**, then use `pico_read_adc` to capture **v_moisture_pos**.
6.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Swap** the pins: Pin **14** to **LOW** and Pin **15** to **HIGH**.
7.  Wait **10ms**, then capture the reading for **v_moisture_neg**.

**Phase C: Electrolysis Neutralization**
8.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Force** both pins back to **LOW**.
9.  **Action**: This immediately stops the chemical reaction in the soil, preventing probe "Blackening."
10. **Validation**: Run the code for 24 hours. Pull the probes out and inspect the metal. They should look shiny and new, whereas DC-only sensing would have left them covered in oxidation.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico holds the probes in a "dormant" (Zero Voltage) state.
2.  **Process**: For a fraction of a second, it pushes current "Left-to-Right" and takes a snapshot of the soil resistance.
3.  **Action**: It immediately slams the current into reverse ("Right-to-Left") and takes a second snapshot.
4.  **Reaction**: The two values are averaged to eliminate environmental noise.
5.  **Result**: The current is cut. The probes are protected. The user gets a stable, high-reliability moisture reading.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Polarity Handshake Setup
p_a = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
p_b_ctrl = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
adc = machine.ADC(27) # Shared pin for reading

def get_ac_moisture():
    # Phase B: Complementary Pulsing logic
    # Step 1: Forward Direction
    p_a.value(1)
    p_b_ctrl.value(0)
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
    read_pos = adc.read_u16()
    
    # Step 2: Reverse Direction
    p_a.value(0)
    p_b_ctrl.value(1)
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
    read_neg = adc.read_u16()
    
    # Phase C: Neutralization logic
    p_a.value(0)
    p_b_ctrl.value(0)
    
    # Average the two readings for stability
    return (read_pos + read_neg) / 2

while True:
    moisture = get_ac_moisture()
    # Normalize to percentage
    pct = 100 - (moisture / 655.35)
    print(f"STABLE MOISTURE: {pct:.1f}%")
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The DC Hang**: Forgetting to set pins to LOW at the end of the script. This keeps the battery draining and the probes corroding even while "resting."
*   **High Impedance**: For best results, re-configure the pins as `machine.Pin.IN` during the idle phase to completely isolate them from the power rail.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ionic Compensator**: Plot both `pos` and `neg` readings on a graph. If they start to drift apart, it means your soil has high salt content—you've just built a basic "Salinity Meter!"

## 1. Project 1362: Advanced Soil Moisture Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Trend Analysis & Derivative Indication". At the end of this project, you will be able to program your Pico to identify not just the moisture level, but its "Rate of Change," enabling the display of "Trend Arrows" (Is the soil drying out or getting wetter?).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Rate of Change (Derivative)**: Understanding that `NewValue - OldValue` represents the speed and direction of environmental change.
*   **Hysteresis Filtering**: Preventing trend arrows from "Flickering" by requiring a minimum threshold of change before updating the indicator.
*   **Temporal State Memory**: Why the Pico must "Remember" what happened in the past to interpret what is happening in the present.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C OLED Display**
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I2C OLED** | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) | I2C | UI Output |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 (ADC) | Analog | Soil Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_arrow`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_moisture**: Float (The moisture percentage recorded in the current loop).
*   **previous_moisture**: Float (The value saved from 10 seconds ago to allow for comparison).
*   **moisture_delta**: Float (The mathematical difference used to determine the trend).
*   **trend_threshold**: Float (The minimum change, e.g., 0.5%, required to trigger an arrow update).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Temporal State Capture**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the result of your soil sensor into **current_moisture**.
2.  From **Math**, drag `subtraction`. **Calculate** `current_moisture - previous_moisture`.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **moisture_delta** with the result.

**Phase B: Derivative Comparative Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **moisture_delta** is greater than **trend_threshold**.
5.  **Action**: If TRUE, the soil is "WETTING." Prepare to draw an UP arrow.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_else_if`. **Check** if **moisture_delta** is less than **negative** **trend_threshold**.
7.  **Action**: If TRUE, the soil is "DRYING." Prepare to draw a DOWN arrow.

**Phase C: Dynamic UI Visualization**
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Output** the raw percentage.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_arrow`. **Render** the trend based on the results of Phase B.
10. **Validation**: Pour a small amount of water near the sensor. You should see the percentage rise AND the "UP" arrow appear. Wait 5 minutes—as the water settles, the arrow should disappear.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico takes a baseline reading and waits.
2.  **Process**: After a set interval, it takes a second reading.
3.  **Action**: It performs a "Subtraction Test" to see if the soil is gaining or losing water.
4.  **Reaction**: If the change is significant, it updates the visual dashboard.
5.  **Result**: The user can see at a glance if their plant's soil is actively drying out, providing more useful info than a single static number.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# OLED and Sensor setup
adc = machine.ADC(26)
prev_val = 0.0
THRESHOLD = 0.5 # 0.5% change needed to trigger arrow

def get_pct():
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    return 100 - (raw / 655.35)

print("--- TREND MONITOR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Temporal State Capture
    curr_val = get_pct()
    delta = curr_val - prev_val
    
    # Phase B: Derivative Comparative logic
    trend = "STABLE"
    if delta > THRESHOLD:
        trend = "WETTING ↑"
    elif delta < -THRESHOLD:
        trend = "DRYING ↓"
        
    # Phase C: Dynamic UI Visualization
    print(f"MOISTURE: {curr_val:.1f}% [{trend}]")
    
    # Update state for next cycle
    prev_val = curr_val
    utime.sleep(10) # 10s trend window
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Noise Sensitivity**: Using a `trend_threshold` of `0` will make the arrow flip up/down every second due to tiny electrical noise. Always include a small "Deadzone."
*   **The Initialization Gap**: On the very first loop, `prev_val` is 0, so the Pico will always report a massive "WETTING" trend. Set `prev_val = get_pct()` once before the `while True` loop begins.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Long-Term Predictor**: Store your readings in a List (P1367) over 1 hour. Use the slope of the line to predict *exactly* what time your plant will be completely dry!

## 1. Project 1363: Advanced Soil Moisture Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Water Runaway Detection & Safety Feedback Loops". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Intelligence-Based Safety" system that monitors the effectiveness of an irrigation pump. If the moisture levels do not rise after 30 seconds of pumping, the Pico will automatically shut down the system to prevent flooding caused by a disconnected hose or empty reservoir.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Closed-Loop Verification**: The practice of checking if an "Output Action" (pumping water) is creating the "Expected Input Change" (rising moisture).
*   **Runaway Protection**: Preventing catastrophic damage (floods) in automated systems when a physical component (a pipe or tank) fails.
*   **Safety Lockouts**: Implementing a permanent "System Stop" state that requires a human reset after a critical failure is detected.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Relay Module** (Representing a 5V/12V Pump)
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer** (Emergency Alarm)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay (Pump)** | GP16 | Output | Control Signal |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 | ADC | High-Res Feedback |
| **Buzzer** | GP18 | Output | Alarm Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **pump_active_flag**: Boolean (A status bit used to track if the irrigation cycle is currently running).
*   **starting_moisture**: Float (the moisture level recorded the exact microsecond the pump was triggered).
*   **safety_timer_start**: Integer (The timestamp used to measure the 30-second verification window).
*   **is_hose_disconnected**: Boolean (A permanent error flag that stops the code if a runaway is detected).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Starting Baseline Snapshot**
1.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the soil is DRY (< 30%).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the current moisture into **starting_moisture**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Record** the current `pico_ticks_ms` into **safety_timer_start**.
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **16** to **HIGH** (Start Pump).

**Phase B: Dynamic Impact Monitoring**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Monitor** the system while pump is running.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if `current_moisture - starting_moisture` is >= **3%**.
7.  **Success**: If moisture is rising, the pump is working! Reset the safety timers.

**Phase C: Emergency Cut-off Execution**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if `pico_ticks_ms - safety_timer_start` is >= **30,000** (30 seconds).
9.  **Action**: If time is up and moisture hasn't risen, set **is_hose_disconnected** to TRUE.
10. **Validation**: Run the code and pull the soil sensor out of the soil while the pump is running. After 30 seconds, the Pico should "Scream" with the buzzer and shut down the pump permanently.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the soil. If it's thirsty, it starts the "Irrigation Protocol."
2.  **Process**: The Pico takes a "Mental Note" (Snapshot) of the moisture and starts a stopwatch.
3.  **Action**: It pumps. It expects to see the soil get wetter almost immediately.
4.  **Reaction**: If the soil stays dry (Runaway), the Pico concludes the water is hitting the floor instead of the plant.
5.  **Result**: The pump is killed. The alarm sounds. The user saves their floor from water damage.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

pump = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
buzzer = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
adc = machine.ADC(26)

# Global Safety States
is_locked = False

def get_moisture():
    return 100 - (adc.read_u16() / 655.35)

while True:
    if is_locked:
        # Emergency Alarm Loop
        buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100)
        buzzer.value(0); utime.sleep_ms(100)
        continue

    val = get_moisture()
    
    if val < 30.0:
        # Phase A: Baseline Snapshot
        start_m = val
        start_t = utime.ticks_ms()
        pump.value(1)
        print("PUMP START: MONITORING FEEDBACK...")
        
        while get_moisture() < 60.0:
            # Phase B: Impact Monitoring
            curr_t = utime.ticks_ms()
            elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(curr_t, start_t)
            
            # Check for Runaway (No change in 10s for test)
            if elapsed > 10000 and (get_moisture() - start_m < 2.0):
                # Phase C: Emergency Cut-off
                print("!! SAFETY ALERT: WATER RUNAWAY !!")
                pump.value(0)
                is_locked = True
                break
                
            utime.sleep_ms(500)
        
        pump.value(0)
        print("WATERING COMPLETE")

    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Tiny Deadbands**: If you expect moisture to rise by 0.1%, sensor noise might trick the safety system into thinking it's working. Always use a clear 2-3% "Proof of Water" threshold.
*   **Blocking While**: A `while` loop that never checks the time will never trigger the safety cut-off. Ensure `utime.ticks_diff` is checked inside every iteration.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Reset**: After 1 hour of "Lockout," have the Pico attempt a tiny 2-second "Pulse Test" to see if a human has refilled the water tank.

## 1. Project 1364: Advanced Soil Moisture Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Gamified UI & Plant Emotion Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to convert abstract sensor data into a "Digital Pet" interface where the Pico displays an expressive character that reacts emotionally to the health of your plant.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **User Interface (UI) Personification**: The design principle of using human-like traits (faces/emotions) to make complex data more understandable.
*   **Conditional Graphic Rendering**: Switching between different memory-stored bitmaps based on multi-sensor logic.
*   **Comfort-Index Algorithms**: Combining light, temperature, and moisture into a single "Happiness Score."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C OLED Display (128x64)**
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)** (For sunlight monitoring)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED Display** | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) | I2C | Faces + Values |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 | ADC | Thirst Logic |
| **Photoresistor** | GP27 | ADC | Sun Logic |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_bitmap`.
*   From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **soil_comfort_index**: Integer (A calculated score from 0-100 indicating overall plant health).
*   **face_character_id**: Integer (A number representing the current emotion: 0=Happy, 1=Sad, 2=Cool).
*   **is_thirsty**: Boolean (A logic trigger that overrides normal faces with a "Gasping" expression).
*   **light_level**: Integer (The raw brightness from the photoresistor).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Environmental Comfort Scoring**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** the soil sensor and convert to percentage (**current_moisture**).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** the light sensor (**light_level**).
3.  From **Math**, drag `multiplication`. **Calculate** a score where moisture counts for 80% and light counts for 20%.

**Phase B: Emotion State Branching**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if moisture is < 15%. **Set** **face_character_id** to **1** (Gasping).
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_else_if`. **Check** if light_level is > 50,000. **Set** **face_character_id** to **2** (Cool/Sunglasses).
6.  **Default**: If everything is in the "Goldilocks Zone," set **face_character_id** to **0** (Happy).

**Phase C: Pixels-to-Personification Mapping**
7.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_clear`. **Refresh** the frame for the next animation.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_bitmap`. **Search** for the bitmap matching your **face_character_id**.
9.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Push** the "Emotion" to the glass.
10. **Validation**: Use a flashlight to shine on the LDR. The plant should suddenly put on "Sunglasses." Pour water on the soil. The "Gasping" face should transform back into a "Smile."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico polls the environment (Current State).
2.  **Process**: The system evaluates the data against the "Plant's Preferences."
3.  **Action**: The Pico decides which "Mask" (Emotion) to wear today.
4.  **Reaction**: The OLED is updated with the personality-driven graphic.
5.  **Result**: The user feels a stronger connection to their plant, making them more likely to remember to water it!

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# OLED setup assumed via standard library
# display = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

adc_soil = machine.ADC(26)
adc_light = machine.ADC(27)

def draw_happy():
    # Placeholder for OLED drawing commands (Eyes: ^^ Mouth: \_/)
    print("UI STATE: HAPPY :)")

def draw_thirsty():
    # Eyes: O O Mouth: [ ]
    print("UI STATE: THIRSTY !!")

def draw_cool():
    # Eyes: -- -- (Sunglasses)
    print("UI STATE: COOL (Sunglasses)")

while True:
    moisture = 100 - (adc_soil.read_u16() / 655.35)
    light = adc_light.read_u16()
    
    # Logic Branching
    if moisture < 20:
        draw_thirsty()
    elif light > 40000:
        draw_cool()
    else:
        draw_happy()
        
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flicker Face**: Updating the OLED every 10ms. This makes the screen look glitchy. Only update when the `face_character_id` actually *changes*.
*   **Contrast Clashing**: Drawing text on top of the "Eyes." Always leave a 20-pixel margin around your face sprites for labels like "TEMP" or "HUMIDITY."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Idle Animation**: Add a `random` generator so that once every 30 seconds, the plant "Blinks" its eyes, making it feel truly alive!

## 1. Project 1365: Automated Advanced Soil Moisture Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Hybrid Actuator Orchestration & Dual-Loop Control". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Climate-Aware" greenhouse controller that manages two independent environmental states: atmospheric humidity (Mist) and root-zone moisture (Drip), using prioritized logic to ensure plants receive balanced hydration.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Decoupled Control Loops**: Understanding how to manage two variables (Humidity vs. Soil) that interact with each other but require separate responses.
*   **Resource Prioritization**: Designing logic that prevents two high-current motors (pumps) from starting at the exact same microsecond, protecting the power supply from brownouts.
*   **Transpiration Control**: The science of using mist to lower plant stress (cooling) while using drip to provide actual hydration.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT22 Sensor** (Atmospheric Humidity)
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor** (Resistive/Capacitive)
*   **2x Relay Modules** (Mist Pump & Drip Pump)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT22** | GP14 | Single-Wire | Humidity/Temp |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 | ADC | Soil Moisture |
| **Mist Relay** | GP16 | Output | Overhead Misting |
| **Drip Relay** | GP17 | Output | Root Irrigation |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_dht`.
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **humidity_pct**: Float (The current relative humidity of the greenhouse air from the DHT22).
*   **soil_moisture_pct**: Float (The moisture level extracted from the root-zone sensor).
*   **mist_pump_status**: Boolean (A flag representing the 'On/Off' state of the atmospheric cooling system).
*   **drip_pump_status**: Boolean (A flag representing the 'On/Off' state of the hydration system).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Hybrid Atmosphere Sampling**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_dht`. **Capture** the **humidity_pct** value.
2.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Convert** the raw voltage to **soil_moisture_pct**.
3.  **Action**: Scan both sensors every 2 seconds to maintain a fresh "Bio-Model" of the greenhouse.

**Phase B: Multi-Phase Hysteresis Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **humidity_pct** is less than **60%**.
5.  **Action**: If TRUE, set **mist_pump_status** to ON (Spray overhead cooling mist).
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **soil_moisture_pct** is less than **30%**.
7.  **Action**: If TRUE, set **drip_pump_status** to ON (Water the roots).

**Phase C: Resource Priority Management**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if both pump states are TRUE.
9.  **Action**: Force a 500ms delay between the first and second pump activation to prevent a massive "Inrush Current" spike.
10. **Validation**: Blow on the DHT22 (raising humidity) and dry the soil probes. You should see the Mist pump stop while the Drip pump continues until the soil is saturated.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the "Air" and the "Earth" simultaneously.
2.  **Process**: It compares current levels against the "Ideal Greenhouse Rules."
3.  **Action**: It fires the relays for misting or dripping as needed.
4.  **Reaction**: If both systems are needed, it staggers their start-up to keep the electronics stable.
5.  **Result**: The plants stay cool and hydrated through an automated expert system.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

# Hardware Prep
dht_sensor = dht.DHT22(machine.Pin(14))
soil_adc = machine.ADC(26)
mist_relay = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
drip_relay = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

def run_greenhouse():
    # Phase A: Hybrid Atmosphere Sampling
    try:
        dht_sensor.measure()
        air_hum = dht_sensor.humidity()
    except OSError:
        air_hum = 100 # Default to safe if sensor fails
        
    soil_raw = soil_adc.read_u16()
    soil_hum = 100 - (soil_raw / 655.35)
    
    # Phase B: Multi-Phase Hysteresis logic
    mist_needed = air_hum < 60
    drip_needed = soil_hum < 30
    
    # Phase C: Resource Priority Management
    if mist_needed:
        mist_relay.value(1)
        print("MISTING ACTIVE")
        if drip_needed: utime.sleep_ms(500) # Staggered Inrush
        
    if drip_needed:
        drip_relay.value(1)
        print("DRIPPING ACTIVE")
        
    # Stop if satisfied
    if not mist_needed: mist_relay.value(0)
    if not drip_needed: drip_relay.value(0)

while True:
    run_greenhouse()
    utime.sleep(5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Cross-Contamination**: Hanging the DHT22 too close to the Mist nozzles. If the DHT gets wet, it will read 99.9% humidity forever, and the misting will never turn back on!
*   **Sensor Saturation**: Ensure your `sleep` duration is long enough for the water to actually soak the soil, or the drip pump will turn on/off too rapidly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **VPD Calculator**: Research "Vapor Pressure Deficit" (VPD) and modify the logic to start misting based on the Temp/Humidity relationship rather than just humidity alone!

## 1. Project 1366: Advanced Soil Moisture Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Sensor Health Verification & Open/Short Failsafe". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Integrity Guard" that distinguishes between a thirsty plant and a broken wire, preventing the Pico from accidentally flooding your room when a sensor is disconnected.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Plausibility Checking**: The practice of verifying that sensor data falls within "Real-World" physical limits (e.g., soil moisture shouldn't be 0% or 100% in a normal pot).
*   **Fail-Silent State**: A design philosophy where any hardware doubt causes the system to stop all actuators (pumps) immediately to minimize risk.
*   **Floating Pins**: Understanding how an open circuit (broken wire) creates "Random" ADC noise that must be filtered out by software logic.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**
*   **Red LED** (Fault Indicator)
*   **Push Button** (Safety Reset)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 | ADC | Signal Input |
| **Fault LED** | GP15 | Output | Status Indicator |
| **Reset Button** | GP14 | Input | Manual Recovery |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_map_range`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_adc_reading**: Integer (The 0-65535 value pulled directly from the sensor).
*   **is_sensor_offline**: Boolean (A latching flag that represents a detected hardware failure).
*   **lockout_retry_count**: Integer (A counter used to attempt a soft-reset before locking the system).
*   **safety_status_msg**: String (A descriptive text shown on the serial monitor like "OK", "OPEN", or "SHORT").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Raw Signal Sanitation**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Capture** the **raw_adc_reading**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **raw_adc_reading** is > **64,000** (Indicates an Open Circuit/Broken Wire).
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_else_if`. **Check** if **raw_adc_reading** is < **1,000** (Indicates a Short Circuit).

**Phase B: Error State Persistence**
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_sensor_offline** to **TRUE** if either condition in Phase A is met.
5.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **15** (RED LED) to **HIGH**.
6.  **Action**: This "Latches" the error so the pump stays off even if the wire touches for a split second later.

**Phase C: Fail-Silent Actuator Disarming**
7.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Force** the Pump Relay (GP16) to **LOW**.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the Reset Button is pressed.
9.  **Action**: If pressed AND the signal is fixed, clear the **is_sensor_offline** flag to resume.
10. **Validation**: Unplug the ground wire from your soil sensor. The "Fault LED" should immediately turn on, and the pump code should stop responding until the wire is fixed and the button is pressed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the electrical "Presence" of the sensor.
2.  **Process**: It checks: "Is this value physically possible for a soil probe?"
3.  **Action**: If the wire breaks (Value hits 65535), the Pico assumes a disaster is about to happen.
4.  **Reaction**: It kills the power to the pump before the logic tries to "Water" a sensor that isn't there.
5.  **Result**: The user's home stays dry, even if the plant sensors are damaged.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Safety Config
adc = machine.ADC(26)
fault_led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
pump = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

error_latch = False

def safety_audit():
    global error_latch
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    
    # Phase A: Raw Signal Sanitation
    # 65535 = Floating/Open, 0 = Short
    if raw > 64000 or raw < 1000:
        # Phase B: Error Persistence
        error_latch = True
        fault_led.value(1)
        # Phase C: Fail-Silent Disarming
        pump.value(0)
        print("!! CRITICAL SENSOR FAULT DETECTED !!")
    
    # Reset Logic
    if reset_btn.value() == 0:
        error_latch = False
        fault_led.value(0)
        print("Safety System Reset.")

while True:
    safety_audit()
    
    if not error_latch:
        # Normal irrigation logic here
        pass
    
    utime.sleep_ms(500)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Soft Error Trap**: Forgetting to use a "Latch." If you don't latch the error, a loose wire will make the pump "Stutter" (On/Off/On) which can burn out the relay.
*   **Floating Voltages**: On a Pico, an unconnected ADC pin doesn't always stay at 0 or 65525. It can "Float" in the middle. Use a high-value (1MΩ) resistor between ADC and VCC to force an open-circuit to read 65535.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Voter System**: Use *two* soil sensors in the same pot. If they don't agree with each other within 10%, trigger a "Sensor Conflict" warning!

## 1. Project 1367: Advanced Soil Moisture Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Scientific Analytics & Drying Curve Logging". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Event-Driven" logging system that identifies the exact moment a plant is watered and automatically records the "Dry-Down" slope over 24 hours to calculate the soil's hydraulic retention properties.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Series Analysis**: The practice of recording a variable over a fixed timeline to identify patterns like "Daily Water Usage."
*   **Event Triggers**: Designing software that only starts high-frequency logging when a specific condition (a sudden 20% spike in moisture) is met.
*   **Storage Buffering**: Managing how data is saved to Flash/SD so that the system doesn't lose data during a power failure.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_write_file`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_moisture**: Float (The moisture percentage recorded in the current loop).
*   **log_timer_start**: Integer (The timestamp used to manage the 10-minute logging intervals).
*   **is_log_session_active**: Boolean (A flag that tells the Pico: "We just watered, start recording the curve!").
*   **last_reading**: Float (The previous moisture value used to detect the 'Watering Spike').

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Event-Driven Log Trigger**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Capture** the **current_moisture**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if `current_moisture - last_reading` is > **20%**.
3.  **Action**: If a spike is detected, set **is_log_session_active** to TRUE. This marks the "Watering Event."

**Phase B: Sequential Time-Series Recording**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_log_session_active** is TRUE.
5.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`. **Check** if **10 minutes** have passed since the last entry.
6.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_join`. **Construct** a line: `Timestamp + "," + Moisture`.

**Phase C: Persistence Layer Finalization**
7.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_to_file`. **Write** the line to `"dry_curve.csv"`.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Stop** the session after 144 entries (24 hours of data).
9.  **Validation**: Water your plant. Check the SD card. You should see a high-density log of the moisture dropping from 80% down to 30%, which you can now graph in Excel to see your plant's "Thirst Profile."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the soil at low frequency (Idle Mode).
2.  **Process**: It waits for a "Spike" (The user watering the plant).
3.  **Action**: Once watered, the Pico "Wakes Up" its high-density logging engine.
4.  **Reaction**: Every 10 minutes, it takes a scientific snapshot of how the water is evaporating/draining.
5.  **Result**: The user gets a precise mathematical "Drying Curve" for that specific plant and soil type.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware Setup
adc = machine.ADC(26)
last_m = 100.0
is_logging = False
entries = 0

def get_m():
    return 100 - (adc.read_u16() / 655.35)

print("--- SCIENTIFIC LOGGER ARMED ---")

while True:
    curr_m = get_m()
    
    # Phase A: Event-Driven Trigger
    if not is_logging and (curr_m - last_m > 20.0):
        print("WATERING DETECTED. STARTING DRY-CURVE LOG.")
        is_logging = True
        entries = 0
        
    # Phase B: Time-Series Recording logic
    if is_logging:
        # Phase C: Persistence Layer
        try:
            with open("/sd/drying.csv", "a") as f:
                f.write(f"{utime.time()},{curr_m:.2f}\n")
            entries += 1
            print(f"Entry {entries}/144 Saved.")
        except:
            print("Disk Error")
            
        # Stop after 24 hours (if logging every 10 mins)
        if entries >= 144:
            is_logging = False
            print("SESSION COMPLETE. DATA ARCHIVED.")

    last_m = curr_m
    utime.sleep(600) # 10 Minute Interval
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Delta Trap**: If you check for a spike too slowly (e.g., once an hour), you might miss the "Fast Soak" of the watering event. Check for the trigger every 1-2 seconds, even if you only log every 10 minutes.
*   **File Bloat**: If you don't use `is_logging` and just log every 10 minutes forever, your SD card will fill up with useless "Static" data where the plant is already dry.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-File Mirroring**: Use your logic from Project 1351 to mirror the drying curve to a backup file so you never lose your scientific observations!

## 1. Project 1368: Smart Advanced Soil Moisture System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Predictive Optimization & Weather-Aware Irrigation". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Thinking" water controller that connects to a weather forecast API and chooses to *skip* its watering schedule if rain is predicted in the next 4 hours, significantly reducing water waste.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Predictive Logic**: Moving from "Reaction" (watering because soil is dry) to "Prediction" (not watering because rain is coming).
*   **API Polling**: Connecting to a network endpoint and "Parsing" a JSON response to find specific weather keywords.
*   **Resource Stewardship**: Using software to optimize natural resources (water/electricity) based on external environmental data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Wireless version required)
*   **Relay Module** (Pump)
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay** | GP16 | Output | Irrigation Control |
| **Soil Sensor** | GP26 | ADC | Local Feedback |
| **WiFi** | Internal | CYW43 | Cloud Forecaster |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`.
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_get_json`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.

### 7. Variables
*   **forecast_api_result**: String (The raw text returned from the weather station simulator).
*   **imminent_rain_flag**: Boolean (A logic state that is TRUE if the keyword 'Rain' or 'Storm' is found in the forecast).
*   **local_soil_status**: Float (The moisture percentage currently in the dirt).
*   **wifi_connection_obj**: Object (The active link to the local internet router).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Network Portal Handshake**
1.  From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`. **Enter** your home SSID and Password.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** until the Pico confirms it has an IP address.
3.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Show** `"STATUS: CONNECTED"`.

**Phase B: Predictive Suppression Logic**
4.  From **Network**, drag `pico_get_json`. **Target** the URL `http://weather.local/forecast`.
5.  From **Text**, drag `pico_string_find`. **Search** for the keywords `"RAIN"` or `"SHOWER"`.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **imminent_rain_flag** based on the search result.

**Phase C: Intelligent Resource Allocation**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: `soil_is_dry` AND `imminent_rain_flag` is FALSE.
8.  **Action**: Turn on the Pump Relay (GP16).
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_else_if`. **Condition**: `soil_is_dry` AND `imminent_rain_flag` is TRUE.
10. **Validation**: Set your "Local Weather Simulator" (a simple PC script) to report "SUNNY." The Pico should water the plant. Change it to "RAIN." The Pico should display "SKIPPING - NATURE WILL PROVIDE" and stay idle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico connects to the global internet.
2.  **Process**: It checks the local soil (Internal data) and then the local clouds (External data).
3.  **Action**: It weighs the two: "It's dry, but free water is coming from the sky soon."
4.  **Reaction**: It chooses to stay OFF to save water.
5.  **Result**: An intelligent, eco-friendly garden that uses exactly 0.00mL more water than necessary.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import network
import urequests
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Network Portal Handshake
SSID = "WiFi_Home"
PASS = "password123"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)

# Mocked Weather API (Simulation)
API_URL = "http://myweatherhub.com/api/get_forecast"

def check_weather():
    # Phase B: Predictive Suppression logic
    try:
        response = urequests.get(API_URL)
        data = response.json()
        response.close()
        
        # If forecast says rain in < 4 hours
        if data['rain_prob'] > 70:
            return True # Rain is coming
    except:
        return False # Default to 'no rain' if API is down
    return False

def manage_irrigation():
    # Phase C: Resource Allocation
    is_raining_soon = check_weather()
    soil_m = 100 - (machine.ADC(26).read_u16() / 655.35)
    
    if soil_m < 30.0:
        if is_raining_soon:
            print("SOIL DRY but RAIN FORECASTED. Skipping irrigation.")
            machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT).value(0)
        else:
            print("SOIL DRY and CLEAR SKIES. Starting Pump.")
            machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT).value(1)
    else:
        print("Soil moisture optimal.")

while True:
    if wlan.isconnected():
        manage_irrigation()
    utime.sleep(3600) # Check every hour
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **API Timeouts**: Networking on a Pico can fail easily. Always wrap your `urequests.get()` in a `try/except` block or your entire garden controller will crash if the WiFi blips!
*   **The Forecast Delay**: Internet forecasts aren't 100% accurate. If the forecast is wrong, your plant might dry out. Always include an "Emergency Override" where if soil hits < 5%, it waters regardless of the weather report.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Solar Tracking**: Use your Network API to also check "Cloud Cover." If it's very cloudy, reduce the misting (Project 1365) to save even more power!

## 1. Project 1369: Advanced Soil Moisture Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Non-Linear Mapping & Multi-Point Calibration". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Piecewise Interpolation" for your soil sensor, mapping raw ADC values to accurate percentages using three reference points (Air, Damp Sponge, and Submerged Water), ensuring the sensor responds accurately across the entire moisture spectrum.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Piecewise Interpolation**: Dividing a non-linear sensor range into multiple linear segments (e.g., mapping 0-50% differently than 50-100%) for higher precision.
*   **Reference Benchmarking**: The process of using known physical states (like a wet sponge) to create "Anchor Points" for a software curve.
*   **Coefficient of Fit**: Understanding how different soil densities affect the slope of the moisture-to-voltage relationship.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Soil Moisture Sensor**
*   **A "Damp" Reference** (Soil at field capacity or a wet sponge)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Math, drag `pico_map_range`.**
*   **From Logic, drag `pico_if_else`.**
*   **From Variables, drag `set_variable`.**
*   **From Text, drag `pico_print_labeled`.**

### 7. Variables
*   **calibration_points_list**: List (A set of three raw ADC values recorded during the calibration phase).
*   **raw_reading**: Integer (The current live ADC output from the sensor).
*   **mapped_percentage**: Float (The final moisture value calculated using the multi-point piecewise model).
*   **fit_coefficient**: Float (A scaling factor used to fine-tune the dampness slope for sandy vs. clay soil).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Reference Data Collection**
1.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Record** the value when probes are in AIR (**CAL_AIR**).
2.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Record** the value when probes are in a damp sponge (**CAL_DAMP**).
3.  From **Smart Sensors**, drag `pico_read_adc`. **Record** the value when probes are submerged in WATER (**CAL_WATER**).

**Phase B: Piecewise Interpolation Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is **raw_reading** between **CAL_AIR** and **CAL_DAMP**?
5.  **Action**: Use `pico_map_range` to scale the input from `[AIR to DAMP]` onto the output `[0% to 50%]`.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_else`. **Action**: Use `pico_map_range` to scale from `[DAMP to WATER]` onto `[50% to 100%]`.

**Phase C: Curve Fitting Verification**
7.  From **Variables**, drag `change_variable`. **Apply** a **fit_coefficient** of **1.1** to dampen noise in the mid-range.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_show`. **Graph** the resulting percentage.
9.  **Validation**: Move the probes from air to damp soil. You should see the percentage hit exactly 50% when you reach your reference point, regardless of the sensor's internal non-linearity.
10. **Result**: Your DIY sensor now matches the accuracy of professional $100+ agricultural probes.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico loads the "Reference Map" for the current sensor.
2.  **Process**: It checks where the current reading lands on the 3-point map.
3.  **Action**: It chooses the "Top Half" or "Bottom Half" math formula to calculate the percentage.
4.  **Reaction**: The non-linear curves of the resistive probe are "Flattened" by the piecewise logic.
5.  **Result**: The user gets a medical-grade accurate moisture percentage for their plant.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

adc = machine.ADC(26)

# Phase A: Reference Data Collection (Hardcoded from test)
CAL_AIR = 62000
CAL_DAMP = 45000
CAL_WATER = 20000

def get_calibrated_moisture():
    # Phase B: Piecewise Interpolation logic
    raw = adc.read_u16()
    
    if raw > CAL_DAMP:
        # Segment 1: Dry to Damp (0% - 50%)
        # Math: Linear map between AIR and DAMP
        pct = (raw - CAL_AIR) / (CAL_DAMP - CAL_AIR) * 50
    else:
        # Segment 2: Damp to Submerged (50% - 100%)
        # Math: Linear map between DAMP and WATER
        pct = 50 + (raw - CAL_DAMP) / (CAL_WATER - CAL_DAMP) * 50
        
    return max(0, min(100, pct))

while True:
    # Phase C: Curve Fitting Verification
    val = get_calibrated_moisture()
    print(f"PIECEWISE MOISTURE: {val:.1f}%")
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Inversion Gap**: On resistive sensors, higher ADC usually means DRYER soil. If your math is backwards (100% when air-dry), swap your `AIR` and `WATER` constants in the map function.
*   **Segment Jumps**: If the reading "Jumps" from 48% to 52% instantly, your `CAL_DAMP` point was moved. Re-calibrate in the damp sponge.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **3-Point Storage**: Save your `CAL_` values to a file (P1367) so you can calibrate the sensor once and have the Pico remember it even after a power cut!

## 1. Project 1370: Advanced Advanced Soil Moisture Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"3D Soil Profiling & Vertical Root-Zone Control". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Deep-Watering" system that uses three sensors at different soil depths (Surface, Middle, Deep) to ensure that water actually reaches the roots rather than just wetting the surface dust.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Vertical Saturation Gradient**: Understanding how water travels through soil over time, often leaving deep roots dry even if the surface is muddy.
*   **Time-of-Flight (Saturation)**: The delay between water hitting the surface and it being detected by the "Deep" sensor.
*   **Dynamic Percolation Analysis**: Using multiple data points to map the 3D moisture profile of a planter.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x Soil Moisture Sensors**
*   **Submersible Pump + Relay**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor (Surface)** | GP26 | ADC0 | 2cm Deep |
| **Sensor (Middle)** | GP27 | ADC1 | 10cm Deep |
| **Sensor (Deep)** | GP28 | ADC2 | 25cm Deep |
| **Pump Relay** | GP16 | Output | Irrigation |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Smart Sensors, drag `pico_read_adc`.**
*   **From Logic, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.**
*   **From Wait, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.**
*   **From Variables, drag `set_variable`.**

### 7. Variables
*   **surface_pct**: Float (The moisture level at the top of the soil).
*   **middle_pct**: Float (The moisture level at the root-ball level).
*   **deep_pct**: Float (The moisture level at the bottom of the pot).
*   **is_deep_saturation_achieved**: Boolean (A flag that triggers the pump to STOP only when the bottom sensor sees water).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Multi-Depth Hardware Handshake**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** all three ADC channels (**GP26, GP27, GP28**).
2.  From **Math**, drag `pico_map_range`. **Normalize** all three sensors to **0-100%**.
3.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Show** all three levels as a "Vertical Column" of numbers.

**Phase B: Depth-Prioritized Irrigation Logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **surface_pct** is < **15%** (Soil is bone-dry).
5.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **16** to **HIGH** (Start Pump).
6.  **Action**: Continue pumping even if surface_pct hits 100%. Watch the **deep_pct** sensor.

**Phase C: Saturation Profile Analysis**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Stop** the pump ONLY when **deep_pct** rises by at least **5%**.
8.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`. **Calculate** the "Percolation Time": `DeepDetectionTime - SurfaceTriggerTime`.
9.  **Validation**: Water the pot. Note how the "Surface" sensor reacts instantly, while the "Deep" sensor might take 30-60 seconds to "See" the water. This interval is your soil's drainage speed.
10. **Result**: Your plant now receives a "Deep Soak" that promotes strong root growth into the lower soil layers.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the hydration "Slice" of the soil.
2.  **Process**: It detects the surface is dry and starts the pump.
3.  **Action**: It ignores the surface sensor for the "Stop" command, looking only at the deep sensor.
4.  **Reaction**: Once the deep roots are confirmed to be wet, it kills the pump.
5.  **Result**: No more "Surface Wetting" errors; the entire root system is properly hydrated every time.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Sensors at different depths
s_surface = machine.ADC(26)
s_middle = machine.ADC(27)
s_deep = machine.ADC(28)
pump = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

def get_profile():
    # Phase A: Multi-Depth Handshake
    surf = 100 - (s_surface.read_u16() / 655.35)
    mid = 100 - (s_middle.read_u16() / 655.35)
    deep = 100 - (s_deep.read_u16() / 655.35)
    return surf, mid, deep

print("--- VERTICAL PROFILER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    surf, mid, deep = get_profile()
    print(f"PROFILE: S:{surf:.0f}% M:{mid:.0f}% D:{deep:.0f}%")
    
    # Phase B: Depth-Prioritized Logic
    if surf < 20.0:
        print("SURFACE DRY. STARTING DEEP WATERING.")
        pump.value(1)
        
        # Phase C: Saturation Analysis
        start_deep = deep
        while True:
            # Poll deep sensor
            _, _, current_deep = get_profile()
            if (current_deep - start_deep) > 5.0:
                print("DEEP SATURATION REACHED. STOPPING.")
                pump.value(0)
                break
            utime.sleep(1)
            
    utime.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Flood Trap**: If your "Deep" sensor is broken or disconnected, the pump will run forever! Always include a `MAX_PUMP_TIME` (e.g., 2 minutes) as an emergency override.
*   **Sensor Cross-Talk**: If you bury sensors too close to each other, water will "Wick" between them, giving a false 3D reading. Space them vertically at least 10cm apart.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Drainage Calculator**: Plot the time it takes for the "Deep" sensor to return to its dry state. Fast drainage means you need to water more often; slow drainage might indicate a muddy "Root Rot" risk!

## 1. Project 1371: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Logical Polarity Abstraction & XOR Gate Integration". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a universal software layer that allows the Pico to work with both Normally Open (NO) and Normally Closed (NC) reed switches. By using bitwise XOR logic, you can swap your hardware sensors without ever changing your main alarm code.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hardware Abstraction**: Creating code that doesn't care about the specific physical details of the sensor (e.g., whether it pulls HIGH or LOW when triggered).
*   **Bitwise XOR Logic**: Using the `^` operator to flip a bit value. If `Pin == 1` and `Mask == 1`, XOR results in `0`. This allows for "Software Inversion."
*   **Switch Topology**: Understanding the difference between NO (Circuit closes when magnet is near) and NC (Circuit opens when magnet is near).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch** (Either NO or NC type)
*   **Magnet**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Input | Pull-Up Enabled |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Output | Status Alert |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_bitwise_xor`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **reed_pin_state**: Integer (The raw 0 or 1 value coming from Pin 14).
*   **config_xor_mask**: Integer (Set this to 1 for NC switches and 0 for NO switches).
*   **alarm_is_triggered**: Boolean (The final, normalized logic state of the alarm).
*   **system_config_bits**: Integer (A global setting used to define the hardware environment).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Hardware Polarity Handshake**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **config_xor_mask** to **1** if your switch is "Normally Closed" (NC).
2.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** Pin **14** and store in **reed_pin_state**.
3.  **Action**: Mount the magnet to your door. The Pico needs to know what "Safe" looks like.

**Phase B: Logical Inversion Logic**
4.  From **Math**, drag `pico_bitwise_xor`. **Calculate** `reed_pin_state ^ config_xor_mask`.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the result in **alarm_is_triggered**.
6.  **Action**: This XOR operation ensures that regardless of the switch type, a "1" in **alarm_is_triggered** always means "Door Open."

**Phase C: Safety Configuration Validation**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **alarm_is_triggered** is TRUE.
8.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **15** to **HIGH** (Sound Alarm).
9.  **Validation**: Remove the magnet. The buzzer should sound. Swap the **config_xor_mask** to 0 and re-test. This demonstrates "Hot Swapping" sensor logic in software.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico reads the physical voltage on the pin.
2.  **Process**: It applies the "XOR Mask" to translate raw voltage into a meaningful "Trigger" state.
3.  **Action**: The system ignores the physical type (NO/NC) and looks only at the normalized trigger.
4.  **Reaction**: If the door opens, the buzzer sounds immediately.
5.  **Result**: A professional, flexible security system that can use any hardware component in the world.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Hardware Setup
reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Phase A: Hardware Polarity Handshake
# Set MASK to 1 for NC, 0 for NO
CONFIG_XOR_MASK = 1 

while True:
    # Phase B: Logical Inversion logic
    raw_val = reed.value()
    # Normalize the signal
    is_triggered = raw_val ^ CONFIG_XOR_MASK
    
    # Phase C: Safety Configuration Validation
    if is_triggered:
        print("!! ALARM DETECTED !!")
        buzzer.value(1)
    else:
        buzzer.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Mask Flip**: Using a mask of `1` on a `NO` switch will make the alarm sound when the door is *closed*. Always double-check your switch type.
*   **Internal Pull-Ups**: If you forget to enable `PULL_UP` in code, the pin will "Float," and the XOR logic will trigger the alarm randomly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Configurator**: Write code that polls the switch at start-up (when the door is known to be closed) and *automatically* sets the **config_xor_mask**!
