## 1. Project 1372: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Signal Stability & Software Debouncing". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Flutter Filter" that prevents false alarms caused by mechanical vibrations or weak magnets, ensuring the Pico only triggers the alarm when a door is truly opened for more than 50 milliseconds.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Contact Bounce**: The physical phenomenon where metal switch contacts "bounce" (on/off) multiple times in microseconds before settling.
*   **Temporal Validation**: Requiring a signal to remain stable for a specific "Time Window" before accepting it as valid.
*   **Noise Rejection**: The engineering practice of ignoring high-frequency "Glitches" to improve system reliability.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Input | Pulse Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_stable_state**: Integer (The last confirmed 0 or 1 value that passed the debounce check).
*   **current_raw_signal**: Integer (The unfiltered, live reading from the sensor).
*   **debounce_timestamp**: Integer (The clock time when the switch last changed its state).
*   **is_signal_validated**: Boolean (A flag that is only TRUE when the signal is rock-solid).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: High-Frequency Pin Polling**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Sample** Pin **14** into **current_raw_signal**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **current_raw_signal** is different from **last_stable_state**.
3.  **Action**: If it changed, reset the **debounce_timestamp** using `pico_ticks_ms`.

**Phase B: Temporal Stability Verification**
4.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** the time since the last change.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if this time is greater than **50ms**.
6.  **Action**: If the signal has been identical for 50ms, the "Bounce" has finished.

**Phase C: Clean Signal Output**
7.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **last_stable_state** with the confirmed value.
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **is_signal_validated** to **TRUE**.
9.  **Validation**: Smack the table near the reed switch. A "Glitch" might happen, but the code should ignore it. Gently pull the magnet away—the alarm should trigger smoothly after a 50ms delay.
10. **Result**: Your alarm system is now immune to electromagnetic interference and mechanical rattle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico polls the reed switch at a very high speed (1000 times per second).
2.  **Process**: It watches for any "Flicker" (state change).
3.  **Action**: It starts a stopwatch every time it sees a change.
4.  **Reaction**: It only believes the signal "Means something" if the stopwatch hits 50ms without another flicker.
5.  **Result**: The user never gets a "False Positive" alarm.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
stable_val = reed.value()
last_change_t = utime.ticks_ms()

print("--- DEBOUNCE MONITOR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: High-Frequency Polling
    raw_val = reed.value()
    
    if raw_val != stable_val:
        # State changed, start the clock
        current_t = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Phase B: Temporal Stability Verification
        if utime.ticks_diff(current_t, last_change_t) > 50:
            # Phase C: Clean Signal Output
            stable_val = raw_val
            print(f"SIGNAL VALIDATED: {'OPEN' if stable_val == 1 else 'CLOSED'}")
        
    else:
        last_change_t = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    utime.sleep_ms(1)
```

---

## 1. Project 1373: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"State-Change Latency Guard & Door-Ajar Alarms". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Smart Watchman" logic that allows a door to be open for short durations (unloading groceries) but triggers a persistent alarm if the door remains "Ajar" for more than 10 seconds.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Beyond-Limit Logic**: Calculating the duration a state has remained active and comparing it to a safe "Grace Period."
*   **Latency Thresholds**: Understanding why immediate alarms are sometimes less useful than "Delayed Persistence" alarms.
*   **Escalation States**: Designing code that moves from "Warning" (soft beep) to "Critical" (full siren) based on time.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Passive Buzzer** (To allow for frequency changes)
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Input | Pulse Feedback |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | PWM | Variable Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_door_ajar**: Boolean (Set to TRUE as long as the magnet is away from the switch).
*   **open_duration_ms**: Integer (The total time in milliseconds the door has been in the 'Open' state).
*   **latency_limit_s**: Integer (The 10-second security window before the alarm fires).
*   **alert_cooldown_timer**: Integer (A delay used to prevent the buzzer from sounding too frequently).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Duration-Based Monitoring**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the door is OPEN.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Start** a stopwatch using `pico_ticks_ms` if it's the first moment the door opened.
4.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** the **open_duration_ms**.

**Phase B: Breach Threshold Logic**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **open_duration_ms** is > **10,000** (10 seconds).
6.  **Critical State**: If the door has been open too long, the "Latency Threshold" has been breached.

**Phase C: Continuous Alert Escalation**
7.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_pwm`. **Start** the buzzer at a low frequency (440Hz).
8.  From **Math**, drag `pico_addition`. **Increase** the frequency by 100Hz every second the door remains open.
9.  **Validation**: Open the door. Wait 5 seconds and close it—no alarm should sound. Now open it and wait 11 seconds. The buzzer should start "Whining" and get louder/higher as time passes.
10. **Result**: A humane alarm system that ignores normal usage but catches the "Forgotten Door" or intruder.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the door.
2.  **Process**: If the door opens, the Pico starts a "Patience Counter."
3.  **Action**: It checks: "Is the user just walking through, or is the door actually left open?"
4.  **Reaction**: Once 10 seconds pass, the Pico loses patience and activates the "Escalation Siren."
5.  **Result**: Energy is saved (if this was a fridge) and security is maintained (if this was a safe).

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
buzzer.duty_u16(0)

door_open_start = 0
LIMIT = 10000 # 10 Seconds

while True:
    is_open = reed.value() == 1
    
    if is_open:
        # Phase A: Duration-Based Monitoring
        if door_open_start == 0:
            door_open_start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), door_open_start)
        
        # Phase B: Breach Threshold logic
        if elapsed > LIMIT:
            # Phase C: Continuous Alert Escalation
            # Frequency proportional to time over limit
            freq = 440 + (elapsed - LIMIT) // 10
            buzzer.freq(min(freq, 2000))
            buzzer.duty_u16(32768)
            print(f"!! ALERT: DOOR AJAR FOR {elapsed/1000:.1f}s !!")
    else:
        # Door is closed, reset system
        door_open_start = 0
        buzzer.duty_u16(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Overflow Mistake**: Forgetting that `ticks_ms` can roll over. Always use `ticks_diff` to calculate duration safely.
*   **Speaker Burnout**: Running a PWM buzzer at 100% duty for an hour. Use a duty of `32768` (50%) for maximum volume with minimum heat.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Pre-Warning Beep**: At the 5-second mark, give a single short "Chirp" to warn the user they have 5 seconds left to close the door before the loud alarm starts.

## 1. Project 1374: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Zoned Security Dashboard & House-Map Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to draw a top-down graphical layout of your home on an OLED screen and overlay dynamic icons that blink or change shape based on which room (Zone) has triggered a security breach.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Coordinate-Based Mapping**: Translating physical room locations into X and Y pixels on a 128x64 display.
*   **Zoning**: Dividing a system into logical sectors (e.g., "Kitchen," "Garage") so the user can locate a problem instantly.
*   **Sprite Invalidation**: The technique of only updating small 8x8 "Status Icons" on a screen rather than redrawing the whole background.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **I2C OLED Display** (SSD1306)
*   **2x Reed Switches** (Zone A and Zone B)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | GP4 (SDA), GP5 (SCL) | I2C | System HUD |
| **Zone A (Door)** | GP14 | Input | Front Entry |
| **Zone B (Wndw)** | GP15 | Input | Master Bedroom |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **From Smart Display, drag `pico_oled_draw_rect`.**
*   **From Smart Display, drag `pico_oled_draw_text`.**
*   **From Logic, drag `pico_if_else`.**
*   **From Variables, drag `set_variable`.**

### 7. Variables
*   **zone_alpha_status**: Boolean (TRUE if the front door magnet is missing).
*   **zone_beta_status**: Boolean (TRUE if the bedroom window magnet is missing).
*   **map_refresh_rate**: Integer (The frequency at which the dashboard updates).
*   **active_fault_overlay**: Integer (A state bit used to toggle the blinking 'X' icon).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Multi-Zone Identity Mapping**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** both pins (**14** and **15**).
2.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_rect`. **Draw** a large rectangle (The House) and a line (The internal wall).
3.  **Action**: Define Zone A as (X:10, Y:30) and Zone B as (X:80, Y:30).

**Phase B: Graphical UI Invalidation**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if either zone is breached.
5.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_draw_text`. **Print** the labels "DOOR" and "WNDW" at their map coordinates.
6.  **Action**: Do not clear the whole screen; only clear the small 16x8 area where the text appears.

**Phase C: Interactive Fault Visualization**
7.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Create** a blinking effect.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **zone_alpha_status** is TRUE.
9.  **Action**: Draw a filled box at the door location to indicate "OPEN." Blink it every 500ms.
10. **Validation**: Open the front door. You should see a blinking block appear on the left side of your "LCD Map." Close it and open the window—the block should shift to the right side.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico paints a static "Blue-Print" (Map) on the OLED.
2.  **Process**: It continuously checks two separate security zones.
3.  **Action**: If a zone is breached, it targets that exact spot on the map.
4.  **Reaction**: It punches a visual "Warning Icon" over the top of the map layout.
5.  **Result**: The user knows exactly *which* door is open without reading a single word of text.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# OLED Setup (Assumed standard init)
# display = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

zone_a = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
zone_b = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

def draw_house_map():
    # Phase A: Identity Mapping
    # Static Walls (drawn once or every refresh)
    # oled.rect(10, 10, 100, 44, 1)
    # oled.line(60, 10, 60, 54, 1)
    
    # Phase B: Graphical UI Invalidation logic
    status_a = "OPEN" if zone_a.value() == 1 else "SAFE"
    status_b = "OPEN" if zone_b.value() == 1 else "SAFE"
    
    # Phase C: Interactive Fault Visualization
    # Zone A (Coordinates 15, 25)
    print(f"Z_A:{status_a}  Z_B:{status_b}") # Serial mirroring
    # oled.text(status_a, 15, 25)
    # oled.text(status_b, 70, 25)
    
    # Visual Alert Block
    if zone_a.value() == 1:
        # oled.fill_rect(12, 12, 6, 6, 1) # Blink this!
        pass
    
    # oled.show()

while True:
    draw_house_map()
    utime.sleep_ms(500)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Coordinates Confusion**: Getting X and Y Mixed up. In OLEDs, X is left-to-right (0-127) and Y is top-to-bottom (0-63).
*   **OLED Burn-in**: Leaving the "House Map" on at full brightness for a month. Use a screensaver or turn the screen off if no doors are open for 10 minutes (Project 1343).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Bitmap Sprites**: Instead of a "Filled Box," use an actual icon of an open door when the zone is breached!

## 1. Project 1375: Automated Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Safety-Interlock Airlock System & Concurrent Access Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a high-security "Airlock" controller where two doors (Outer and Inner) are physically prevented from being open simultaneously. This is the foundation of clean-room security and high-value vault management.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Interlock Protocols**: A safety design where the state of one device (Door A) physically or logically "Locks out" the operation of a second device (Door B).
*   **Race Condition Prevention**: Ensuring that even if two buttons are pressed at the exact same time, the software priority system only allows one door to cycle.
*   **Solenoid State Feedback**: Using reed switches not just for alarms, but for "State Confirmation" before energizing high-power locks.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Reed Switches** (Outer Door, Inner Door)
*   **2x Magnets**
*   **2x Relay Modules** (Simulating 12V Solenoid Locks)
*   **2x Push Buttons** (Request to Enter A, Request to Enter B)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed A (Outer)** | GP14 | Input | Loop A |
| **Reed B (Inner)** | GP15 | Input | Loop B |
| **Relay A (Lock A)** | GP16 | Output | Solenoid Lock A |
| **Relay B (Lock B)** | GP17 | Output | Solenoid Lock B |
| **Button A** | GP18 | Input | Access Request |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_and`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **outer_door_state**: Boolean (Current open/closed status of the exterior airlock door).
*   **inner_door_state**: Boolean (Current open/closed status of the interior clean-room door).
*   **is_airlock_violation**: Boolean (A flag that triggers an alarm if both doors are ever detected as open).
*   **solenoid_lock_status**: String (A descriptive state for the LCD dashboard: "LOCKED" or "UNLOCKED").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Interlock Dependency Mapping**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** both reed switches.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is **inner_door_state** CLOSED (0)?
3.  **Action**: Only if inner is closed, allow the "Outer Door" solenoid to be eligible for activation.
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Enable** the relay for Lock A only when the safety condition is MET.

**Phase B: Concurrent Access Logic**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_and`. **Check** if **Button A** is pressed AND **inner_door_state** is CLOSED.
6.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Pulse** the relay for 5 seconds to allow entry.
7.  **Action**: If Button B is pressed while A is open, the Pico must ignore the request and sound a "Denial" tone on the buzzer.

**Phase C: Fail-Safe Locking Execution**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_and`. **Monitor** if **outer_door_state** == OPEN and **inner_door_state** == OPEN.
9.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Engage** BOTH locks instantly and sound a "Security Breach" alarm.
10. **Validation**: Open Door A. While it's open, try to open Door B. The system should physically (relay click) and logically (serial output) prevent B from moving.
11. **Result**: Your airlock is now secured against unauthorized simultaneous passage.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the "Seal" of the airlock.
2.  **Process**: It checks if any user is requesting entry via buttons.
3.  **Action**: Before unlocking a door, it asks: "Is the other door safely sealed?"
4.  **Reaction**: If yes, it releases the lock. If no, it enforces the "Airlock Rule" and keeps both locked.
5.  **Result**: Controlled, one-at-a-time passage through a high-security zone.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Sensors (Reed Switches)
outer_s = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
inner_s = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Actuators (Solenoid Relays)
outer_lock = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
inner_lock = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

# Request Buttons (Pins simulated for logic)
btn_a = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
btn_b = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    # Phase A: Interlock Dependency Mapping
    is_outer_open = outer_s.value() == 1
    is_inner_open = inner_s.value() == 1
    
    # Phase B: Concurrent Access logic
    # Button A requests Outer door. Only allow if Inner is closed.
    if btn_a.value() == 0:
        if not is_inner_open:
            print("AIRLOCK CLEAR: Opening Outer.")
            outer_lock.value(1); utime.sleep(5); outer_lock.value(0)
        else:
            print("DENIED: Inner door is currently open!")
            
    # Button B requests Inner door. Only allow if Outer is closed.
    if btn_b.value() == 0:
        if not is_outer_open:
            print("AIRLOCK CLEAR: Opening Inner.")
            inner_lock.value(1); utime.sleep(5); inner_lock.value(0)
        else:
            print("DENIED: Outer door is currently open!")

    # Phase C: Fail-Safe Locking Execution
    if is_outer_open and is_inner_open:
        print("!! SECURITY BREACH: BOTH DOORS UNSEALED !!")
        # Trigger Alarm Logic here
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Fire Trap**: Forgetting that an interlock can trap people in a fire. Always include an "Emergency Release" physical button that cuts power to the relays directly.
*   **Relay Bounce**: If the relay handles too much current, it can spark. Use a flyback diode on your solenoid to prevent the inductive spike from resetting your Pico.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **LCD Status HUD**: Connect an I2C OLED (Project 1341) to show "AIRLOCK SEALED" or "DOOR A ACTIVE" so users know why their button press was ignored.

## 1. Project 1376: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Software Debounce Efficacy Audit & Sampling Diagnostics". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional diagnostic tool that measures the "Quality" of your reed switch contacts. You will compare three different sampling rates (1ms, 10ms, 100ms) to see how many "Glitches" your software filter is successfully catching vs. missing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multi-Rate Sampling**: The technique of reading the same sensor at different speeds to identify high-frequency noise.
*   **Statistical Divergence**: Identifying the gap between "Raw Reality" (1ms sampling) and "Filtered Perception" (100ms sampling).
*   **Filter Confidence Score**: A mathematical ratio used to judge if a debounce algorithm is too aggressive or too weak.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch** (Preferably an older or cheaper one with more 'bounce')
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1372)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_addition`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **sample_rate_ms**: Integer (The time delay between diagnostics snapshots).
*   **glitch_detected_count**: Integer (Counter for how many times the 1ms loop saw a change that the 100ms loop missed).
*   **filter_confidence_score**: Float (A percentage representing the reliability of the current sensor).
*   **raw_signal_history**: List (A buffer of the last 10 readings to analyze noise patterns).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Multi-Rate Signal Sampling**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** Pin **14** every **1ms**. Store in `fast_read`.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Pause** for **100ms**.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** Pin **14** again. Store in `slow_read`.

**Phase B: Statistical Divergence Analysis**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Compare**: Did `fast_read` see a "1" while `slow_read` saw a "0"?
5.  **Action**: If yes, a "Glitch" or "Bounce" was detected. Increment the **glitch_detected_count**.
6.  From **Math**, drag `pico_division`. **Calculate** the ratio of valid triggers to total bounces.

**Phase C: Filter Optimization Output**
7.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`. **Show** the "Current Efficacy: 92%" on the serial monitor.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Suggest** to the user: "INCREASE DEBOUNCE TIME" if the score drops below 80%.
9.  **Validation**: Shake the magnet near the switch without pulling it away. You should see the "Glitch Count" skyrocket while the "Validated State" stays at 0.
10. **Result**: You now have empirical data to prove your code is working, rather than just guessing.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins a "Scientific Observation" session of the hardware.
2.  **Process**: It captures data at two speeds: "Zoomed In" (1ms) and "Zoomed Out" (100ms).
3.  **Action**: It identifies discrepancies (Flickers) between the two views.
4.  **Reaction**: It calculates how "Dirty" the electrical signal is.
5.  **Result**: The user can "Tune" their debounce timer (Project 1372) to the exact number of milliseconds needed for their specific switch.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
glitches = 0
valid_triggers = 0

print("--- DEBOUNCE EFFICACY AUDIT STARTING ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Multi-Rate Signal Sampling
    # Capturing high-speed pulse
    fast_history = []
    for _ in range(100):
        fast_history.append(reed.value())
        utime.sleep_ms(1)
    
    # Snapshot at the end (the 'Slow' view)
    slow_view = reed.value()
    
    # Phase B: Statistical Divergence Analysis
    # If the fast history has any '1's but the slow view is '0', 
    # it was a transient glitch.
    if 1 in fast_history and slow_view == 0:
        glitches += 1
        
    if 1 in fast_history and slow_view == 1:
        valid_triggers += 1

    # Phase C: Filter Optimization Output
    if (glitches + valid_triggers) > 0:
        cleanliness = (valid_triggers / (glitches + valid_triggers)) * 100
        print(f"Signal Quality: {cleanliness:.1f}% | Glitches: {glitches}")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

## 1. Project 1377: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Energy-Audit Fridge Monitor & Event Archiving". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a persistence-based logger that records every time a fridge door is opened, how long it stayed open, and calculates the "KWh Wasted" based on the internal temperature rise, saving all data to a CSV file on an SD card for later analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Non-Volatile Persistence**: Storing data that survives a power loss by writing to an SD Card or the Pico's internal Flash.
*   **Time-Stamp Delays**: Learning to record the *start* and *end* of an event to calculate its duration.
*   **Energy Metrics Integration**: Translating physical durations (seconds) into thermal loss estimates (Energy Waste).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **SD Card Module** (SPI)
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
- (Same as P1351 for SD Card)

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Internet**, drag `pico_sd_write`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **sd_card_mounted**: Boolean (A flag that confirms the SPI storage is ready for writes).
*   **log_entry_string**: String (The CSV-formatted line containing the duration and energy math).
*   **open_time_stamp**: Integer (The clock value when the fridge magnet first left the sensor).
*   **energy_waste_estimate**: Float (A calculation of thermal loss based on door-open duration).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Persistence Layer Handshake**
1.  From **Smart IO**, drag `pico_sd_mount`. **Connect** Pin **13** (CS).
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **sd_card_mounted** is TRUE.
3.  From **Internet**, drag `pico_sd_write`. **Initialize** the file `fridge_log.csv` with a header row.

**Phase B: Sequential Time-Series Recording**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the door is OPEN.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** the **open_time_stamp**.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the door is CLOSED.
7.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** the total duration.

**Phase C: Energy Calculation Audit**
8.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Multiply** `duration_seconds` by **0.05** (A simplified constant for energy loss per second).
9.  From **Internet**, drag `pico_sd_write`. **Append** the **log_entry_string** to the SD card.
10. **Validation**: Open the door for 10 seconds and close it. Remove the SD card and check it on your PC. You should see a new line: `10, 0.50` (10 seconds, 0.5 Watts wasted).
11. **Result**: You now have a scientific tool to prove to your family that leaving the fridge open is expensive!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes its local hard drive (SD Card).
2.  **Process**: It waits patiently for the door to move.
3.  **Action**: It acts as a stopwatch, timing the exact second the seal is broken.
4.  **Reaction**: Once the door closes, it does the math and "Commits" the data to memory.
5.  **Result**: A permanent, tamper-proof record of energy usage patterns.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import os

# SD Card Setup assumed (pins 10-13)
# sd = machine.SDCard(slot=2, ...)
# os.mount(sd, "/sd")

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
door_active = False
start_ms = 0

print("--- FRIDGE LOGGER ONLINE ---")

while True:
    is_open = reed.value() == 1
    
    # Phase B: Sequential Time-Series Recording
    if is_open and not door_active:
        # Door just opened
        start_ms = utime.ticks_ms()
        door_active = True
        print("Door Opened...")
        
    elif not is_open and door_active:
        # Door just closed
        end_ms = utime.ticks_ms()
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(end_ms, start_ms) / 1000
        
        # Phase C: Energy Calculation Audit
        waste = duration * 0.05 # Simulation: 0.05W loss per second
        
        log_line = f"{utime.time()},{duration:.1f},{waste:.2f}\n"
        
        # Write to SD
        try:
            with open("/sd/fridge_log.csv", "a") as f:
                f.write(log_line)
            print(f"Logged: {duration}s")
        except:
            print("SD Card Error!")
            
        door_active = False
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Unmounted SD**: Trying to write before the card is plugged in. Always use a `try/except` block or the Pico will crash when you pull the card to check it on your PC!
*   **File Bloat**: Recording data every 10ms. This will fill your SD card in an hour. Only write to the card when an event *finishes* (the door closes).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cumulative Counter**: Add a variable that tracks the *Total* energy wasted since the Pico was turned on, and show it on an OLED (Project 1374).

## 1. Project 1378: Smart Advanced Reed Switch Alarm System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Wireless Remote Sentential & MQTT Telemetry". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a network-connected security node that sends a "BREACH" alert to your smartphone via an MQTT broker the moment a door or window magnet is separated.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transport)**: Use of a lightweight messaging protocol designed for low-power IoT devices.
*   **Pub/Sub Model**: Understanding how the Pico "Publishes" an alarm and your phone "Subscribes" to it.
*   **Keep-Alive Handshakes**: Ensuring your Pico stays connected to the cloud even if no alarms happen for hours.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Wireless required)
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Input | Pulse Feedback |
| **WiFi** | Internal | CYW43 | Cloud Link |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`.
*   From **Network**, drag `pico_mqtt_publish`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **wifi_signal_strength**: Integer (The current RSSI value of your home router connection).
*   **mqtt_payload_msg**: String (The text message sent to your phone, e.g., "ZONE_FRONT_DOOR_OPEN").
*   **is_transmission_confirmed**: Boolean (A flag set to TRUE once the MQTT server acknowledges the message).
*   **retry_backoff_ms**: Integer (A delay used to wait before trying to reconnect if the WiFi drops).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Network Portal Handshake**
1.  From **Network**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`. **Enter** your WiFi credentials.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Wait** until the Pico shows a green LED (Connection established).
3.  From **Network**, drag `pico_mqtt_connect`. **Point** it to a public broker like `broker.hivemq.com`.

**Phase B: Real-Time Event Transmission**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is the reed switch magnet missing?
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Set** **mqtt_payload_msg** to `"ALARM"`.
6.  From **Network**, drag `pico_mqtt_publish`. **Send** the message to the topic `"myhome/security/door"`.

**Phase C: Remote Acknowledgement Loop**
7.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Wait** for a "SUCCESS" response from the network.
8.  From **Smart Display**, drag `pico_oled_print`. **Show** `"STATUS: SENT"`.
9.  **Validation**: Connect your laptop to the same MQTT topic using a web client. Move the magnet. You should see the word "ALARM" appear on your laptop screen within 100ms.
10. **Result**: Your door is now being monitored from anywhere in the world with an internet connection.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico joins the local WiFi mesh.
2.  **Process**: It logs into the "Communication Broker" (Waitress) and waits.
3.  **Action**: The moment the door is touched, it hands a "Note" (MQTT Message) to the broker.
4.  **Reaction**: The broker instantly delivers that note to every phone/computer listening.
5.  **Result**: Invisible, global security coverage.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import network
import machine
import utime
from simple import MQTTClient # Standard MicroPython MQTT lib

# Phase A: Network Portal Handshake
WIFI_SSID = "MyHomeWiFi"
WIFI_PASS = "Pass123"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(WIFI_SSID, WIFI_PASS)

while not wlan.isconnected():
    print("Waiting for WiFi...")
    utime.sleep(1)

client = MQTTClient("Pico_Security", "broker.hivemq.com")
client.connect()

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
prev_state = reed.value()

print("--- WIRELESS SENTINEL ARMED ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Real-Time Event Transmission
    curr_state = reed.value()
    
    if curr_state != prev_state:
        if curr_state == 1:
            print("BREACH! Publishing MQTT...")
            client.publish("pico/security/alert", "DOOR_OPEN")
        else:
            client.publish("pico/security/alert", "DOOR_CLOSED")
            
        prev_state = curr_state
        
    # Phase C: Remote Acknowledgement (Keep-Alive)
    client.check_msg() # Process any incoming network packets
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Public Broker Privacy**: Using a simple topic name like `"door"`. Every Pico W in the world uses that topic! Always use a unique prefix like `"JohnDoe_1234/home/door"`.
*   **WiFi Sleep**: The Pico W sometimes puts its WiFi chip to sleep to save power. If your alarm triggers during a sleep cycle, it might lag. Always use `wlan.config(pm=0xa11140)` to keep the radio awake for security apps.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Two-Way Talk**: Make your Pico SUBSCRIBE to a topic. If you send "LOCK" from your phone, have the Pico engage the solenoid from Project 1375!

## 1. Project 1379: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Magnet distance Mapping & Sensitivity Tuning". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a scientific calibration tool that maps the "Trigger Zone" of a reed switch. You will learn to identify the exact distance (in mm) where a magnet reliably closes the switch and, more importantly, the "Release Gap" (Hysteresis) where it opens, to ensure 100% reliable security triggers.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Magnetic Hysteresis**: The difference between the "Pull-in" force (to close the switch) and the "Drop-out" force (to open it). It takes more energy to open a switch than to close it.
*   **Signal Reliability Index**: A percentage score calculated based on how many times a magnet triggers vs. misses at a specific physical distance.
*   **Field Mapping**: Visualizing the invisible magnetic field by recording binary states across a physical axis.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**
*   **Metric Ruler**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Input | Pulse Trigger |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_addition`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **trigger_distance_mm**: Integer (The user-input distance recorded during the test).
*   **release_distance_mm**: Integer (The recorded distance where the switch opens).
*   **calibration_state_index**: Integer (Tracks if we are test 'Closing' or 'Opening').
*   **hysteresis_gap**: Integer (The calculated difference between Trigger and Release).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Magnetic Field Profiling**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Place** the magnet far away (10cm).
2.  From **User Input**, drag `pico_observe_serial_output`. **Action**: Slowly move the magnet closer to the reed switch until you see the trigger alert.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Record** the distance from the ruler into **trigger_distance_mm**.

**Phase B: Hysteresis Window Mapping**
4.  From **User Input**, drag `pico_observe_physical_action`. **Action**: Slowly pull the magnet *away* from the switch until it opens again.
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Record** this second distance into **release_distance_mm**.
6.  From **Math**, drag `pico_subtraction`. **Find** the "Hysteresis Gap" (**release_distance_mm** - **trigger_distance_mm**).

**Phase C: Reliability Scoring Execution**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is the gap larger than 5mm?
8.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`. **Show** the score: "RELIABILITY: EXCELLENT" or "WARNING: TIGHT TOLERANCE".
9.  **Validation**: Perform the test 5 times. If the trigger point moves more than 2mm, the mounting is unstable.
10. **Result**: You now know exactly where to mount your sensors for zero false-alarms.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters "Lab Mode" and waits for a magnetic event.
2.  **Process**: It captures the "Make" and "Break" points of the electrical circuit.
3.  **Action**: It calculates the mechanical tolerance of the installation.
4.  **Reaction**: It grades the installation based on magnetic stability.
5.  **Result**: A certified, verified security installation.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Magnetic Field Profiling
reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

trigger_mm = 0
release_mm = 0
state = 0 # 0: Wait for Close, 1: Wait for Open

print("--- REED SENSITIVITY CALIBRATOR ---")

while True:
    # Wait for sensor to close
    if state == 0 and reed.value() == 0:
        print("\n>> TRIGGER DETECTED! <<")
        # Elite Standard: Map the physical distance to the electrical state
        trigger_mm = int(input("Enter distance from ruler (mm): "))
        state = 1
        print("Now SLOWLY pull the magnet away...")
        
    # Phase B: Hysteresis Window Mapping
    elif state == 1 and reed.value() == 1:
        print(">> RELEASE DETECTED! <<")
        release_mm = int(input("Enter distance from ruler (mm): "))
        
        # Phase C: Reliability Scoring Execution
        gap = abs(release_mm - trigger_mm)
        print(f"Hysteresis Gap: {gap} mm")
        
        if gap > 5:
            print("RELIABILITY SCORE: 100% (STABLE)")
        else:
            print("RELIABILITY SCORE: 60% (UNSTABLE - INCREASE GAP)")
            
        state = 0
        utime.sleep(2)
        print("\nReady for next test cycle...")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Metal Obstacle**: Running this test on a steel door. Steel absorbs magnetism! Always calibrate on the actual surface.
*   **Magnet Polarity**: Rotating the magnet can change trigger distance by 200%. Keep the magnet orientation fixed.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sensitivity Map**: Record the trigger point from the Side (X-axis) vs Head-on (Y-axis) to find the "Sweet Spot."

## 1. Project 1380: Advanced Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"3-Phase BLDC Commutation & Hall-Effect Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a primitive Brushless DC (BLDC) motor controller. You will use three reed switches (functioning as Hall sensors) to detect a rotor's position and use that feedback to pulse three separate electromagnets in the perfect sequence to create continuous rotation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Electronic Commutation**: Replacing physical brushes in a motor with software logic that switches power to coils based on magnetic position.
*   **3-Phase Sequencing**: The "Six-Step" commutation pattern (U, V, W) used to pull and push a rotor smoothly.
*   **Closed-Loop Feedback**: A system where the output (Motor speed) is used as an input (Sensor timing) to adjust the power.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x Reed Switches** (Arranged in a 120-degree circle)
*   **3x High-Power MOSFETs** or Relay Modules
*   **3x Homemade Electromagnets**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Hall A (Reed)** | GP14 | Input | Phase A Detect |
| **Hall B (Reed)** | GP15 | Input | Phase B Detect |
| **Hall C (Reed)** | GP16 | Input | Phase C Detect |
| **Coil U** | GP18 | Output| Phase U Drive |
| **Coil V** | GP19 | Output| Phase V Drive |
| **Coil W** | GP20 | Output| Phase W Drive |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_binary_or`.

### 7. Variables
*   **hall_phase_vector**: Integer (A 3-bit binary number 0bABC representing sensor states).
*   **last_vector**: Integer (Stores previous state to identify rotation changes).
*   **commutation_delay_us**: Integer (Timing between steps).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: 3-Phase Hall Sequencing**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** all three reed switches (**14, 15, 16**).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Combine** them into a Phase Vector: `(A << 2) | (B << 1) | C`.
3.  **Action**: This creates a unique number (1-6) identifying the rotor's exact location.

**Phase B: Torque Vectoring Logic logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Check** the **hall_phase_vector**.
5.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Fire** the coil that is *in front* of the current magnet to pull it forward.
6.  **Action**: Turn OFF all other coils to prevent "Braking" or overheating.

**Phase C: Speed Control Execution**
7.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_us`. **Measure** the time between state changes.
8.  From **User Input**, drag `pico_observe_physical_action`. **Validation**: Give the rotor a manual "Kickstart" spin. The Pico will pick up the speed and begin pulsing coils automatically to maintain rotation.
9.  **Result**: A fully functional software-controlled motor driver.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico scans the magnetic "Footprint" of the rotor.
2.  **Process**: It calculates which electromagnet needs power to maintain momentum.
3.  **Action**: It switches high-current power to the correct phase.
4.  **Reaction**: The rotor moves, triggering the *next* reed switch automatically.
5.  **Result**: High-speed, brushless continuous rotation.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: 3-Phase Hall Sequencing
h_a = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
h_b = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
h_c = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

c_u = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT)
c_v = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.OUT)
c_w = machine.Pin(20, machine.Pin.OUT)

def drive_motor(u, v, w):
    c_u.value(u); c_v.value(v); c_w.value(w)

print("--- BLDC DRIVE ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Read Phase Vector (Inverted logic for Reed grounds)
    v = (not h_a.value() << 2) | (not h_b.value() << 1) | (not h_c.value())
    
    # Phase B: Torque Vectoring Logic logic
    if v == 0b100: # Magnet at A
        drive_motor(0, 1, 0) # Pull toward B
    elif v == 0b010: # Magnet at B
        drive_motor(0, 0, 1) # Pull toward C
    elif v == 0b001: # Magnet at C
        drive_motor(1, 0, 0) # Pull toward A
    else:
        drive_motor(0, 0, 0) # Neutral
        
    utime.sleep_us(500) # Resolution of control
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Phase Misalignment**: If the motor "Sings" or shakes but won't spin, your coils are wired backwards. Swap Coil V and Coil W.
*   **Reed Sparking**: Electromagnets create high-voltage "Kickback". If you don't use Flyback Diodes, the Reed switches will melt shut in seconds.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **OLED Tachometer**: Use `ticks_diff` to calculate the actual RPM and display it on the screen.

## Batch 139: Advanced Tilt Sensor 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1381: Advanced Tilt Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dual-Switch Complementary Orientation & Signal Fusion". At the end of this project, you will be able to use two discrete tilt sensors mounted at 90-degree offsets to create a "Truth Table" logic engine. This allows the Pico to differentiate between four distinct physical orientations (Flat, Left, Right, Inverted) instead of just a binary "Tilted" state.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Truth Table Logic**: Mapping binary combinations (00, 01, 10, 11) to higher-level logical states to increase system intelligence.
*   **Sensor Fusion (Foundations)**: The technique of combining data from multiple low-cost sensors to infer a complex physical state that a single sensor cannot measure.
*   **Polarity Abstraction**: Engineering a software layer that standardizes "Tilted" as 1 and "Upright" as 0, regardless of physical wiring.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Tilt Switches** (SW-520D Ball Type)
*   **Magnet or Mounting Tape**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt A (Vert)** | GP14 | Input | PULL_UP |
| **Tilt B (Horiz)** | GP15 | Input | PULL_UP |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **y_axis_state**: Integer (The binary reading from the vertically mounted sensor).
*   **x_axis_state**: Integer (The binary reading from the horizontally mounted sensor).
*   **calculated_pose**: String (The final human-readable orientation: "FLAT", "LEFT", "RIGHT", or "UPSIDE_DOWN").
*   **fusion_bitmask**: Integer (A combined value used to index the truth table).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: 2-Axis Identity Mapping**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Sample** Pin **14** into **y_axis_state**.
2.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Sample** Pin **15** into **x_axis_state**.
3.  **Action**: Mount the sensors so that Tilt A is vertical and Tilt B is perfectly horizontal on the breadboard.

**Phase B: Truth-Table Logic Engine**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Check** if **y_axis_state** is 1 and **x_axis_state** is 1.
5.  **Action**: If both are open (not tilted), define the state as "HORIZONTAL".
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **y_axis_state** is 0 (Tilted) and **x_axis_state** is 1 (Flat).
7.  **Action**: Define this state as "LEFT_LEAN".

**Phase C: State Change Validation**
8.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`. **Show** the **calculated_pose** on the serial monitor.
9.  **Validation**: Rotate the breadboard 90 degrees clockwise. You should see "RIGHT_LEAN". Flip it completely over—the table should output "UPSIDE_DOWN".
10. **Result**: Your Pico now has a rudimentary understanding of its own position in 3D space using only binary components.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico polls both gravity-switches simultaneously.
2.  **Process**: It combines the two binary "Yes/No" signals into a "Truth Table."
3.  **Action**: It uses a logical decision tree to determine which way is "Down."
4.  **Reaction**: It maps the complex physics to a simple word like "LEFT."
5.  **Result**: Precise orientation sensing without an expensive accelerometer.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: 2-Axis Identity Mapping
pin_a = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
pin_b = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("--- ORIENTATION SENSOR ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Truth-Table Logic Engine
    # 0 = Ball connected (Tilted), 1 = Ball disconnected (Flat)
    v1 = pin_a.value()
    v2 = pin_b.value()
    
    if v1 == 1 and v2 == 1:
        pose = "HORIZONTAL"
    elif v1 == 0 and v2 == 1:
        pose = "TILTED LEFT"
    elif v1 == 1 and v2 == 0:
        pose = "TILTED RIGHT"
    elif v1 == 0 and v2 == 0:
        pose = "UPSIDE DOWN"
    else:
        pose = "UNKNOWN"
        
    # Phase C: State Change Validation
    print(f"TRUTH TABLE INDEX: {v1}{v2} | POSITION: {pose}")
    
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Angle Ambiguity**: If you tilt the board exactly 45 degrees, both balls might roll halfway, causing a "Flicker" between states. Always mount sensors at sharp 90-degree offsets.
*   **Pull-up Negation**: Forgetting that `PULL_UP` makes the pin 1 when the switch is open. Always verify if "1" means tilted or flat for your specific mounting.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Inertial Smoothing**: Add a variable that counts how long a state has been active. Only print "UPSIDE DOWN" if the sensors stay in that state for 500ms to ignore shakes.

## 1. Project 1382: Advanced Tilt Sensor Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Ergonomic Posture Watchdog & Confidence Windowing". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a wearable health-monitor that detects "Slouching" (forward tilt) and tracks it over time. You will implement a "Confidence Window" to ensure the alarm only triggers during poor posture, ignoring temporary movements like leaning over to pick up a pen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Confidence Windowing**: A technique where a state must remain active for a specific "Time-on-Threshold" before the system accepts it as a valid event.
*   **Temporal Hysteresis**: Preventing "Alarm Flutter" by requiring the user to stay upright for 2 seconds before resetting the slouch timer.
*   **Bio-Feedback Loops**: Using haptic (vibration) or audio alerts to correct human behavior in real-time.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Vibration Motor (or LED)**
*   **Battery Pack** (For wearable use)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Sensor** | GP14 | Input | Shoulder Mount |
| **Vibrator** | GP16 | Digital | Alert Output |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.

### 7. Variables
*   **slouch_start_time**: Integer (The timestamp when the user first leaned forward).
*   **is_currently_slouching**: Boolean (Set to TRUE if the sensor detects forward tilt).
*   **alert_threshold_ms**: Integer (The 5000ms "Grace Period" before the buzzer fires).
*   **total_daily_slouch_count**: Integer (Counter for the total number of posture violations).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Health Monitoring Handshake**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is the sensor CLOSED (leaning)?
3.  **Action**: If lean detected, initialize the **slouch_start_time** using `pico_ticks_ms` if it was previously zero.

**Phase B: Posture Verification logic**
4.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** how long the lean has lasted.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **duration** > **5000** (5 seconds).
6.  **Action**: If the user has been leaning for 5 seconds, the "Confidence Window" has closed—this is a valid slouch.

**Phase C: Haptic Alert Execution**
7.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Turn ON** the Vibrator (Pin 16).
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Reset** the timer immediately if the user sits up straight.
9.  **Validation**: Lean forward for 2 seconds and stand back up—nothing should happen. Now lean forward and hold it—the vibrator should kick in at the 5-second mark.
10. **Result**: A smart wearable that helps improve health without annoying false alarms during normal movement.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the wearer's angle at high resolution.
2.  **Process**: If a "Slouch" starts, the Pico starts a mental stopwatch.
3.  **Action**: It asks: "Is this a temporary reach or poor posture?"
4.  **Reaction**: If it hits the 5-second threshold, it sends a tactile "Nudge" to the user's shoulder.
5.  **Result**: Corrected posture through subtle reinforcement.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
vibe = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

slouch_start = 0
THRESHOLD = 5000 # 5 Seconds

print("--- POSTURE WATCHDOG ARMED ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Health Monitoring Handshake
    is_leaning = tilt.value() == 0
    
    if is_leaning:
        # Phase B: Posture Verification logic
        if slouch_start == 0:
            slouch_start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), slouch_start)
        
        if elapsed > THRESHOLD:
            # Phase C: Haptic Alert Execution
            vibe.value(1)
            print(f"!! SLOUCH DETECTED: {elapsed/1000:.1f}s !!")
    else:
        # User is upright, reset
        slouch_start = 0
        vibe.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

## 1. Project 1383: Advanced Tilt Sensor Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Theft Motion Arming System & Latching Sirens". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade bike alarm that "Arms" when the bike is parked on its kickstand. If the bike is moved to an upright (stolen) position, the Pico detects the state-change and triggers a latched siren that will not stop until a physical security reset is performed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Arming/Disarming Logic**: Creating a software "Armed" flag that acts as a gatekeeper for critical security functions.
*   **Snapshot State Baseline**: Capturing the initial physical state of a sensor at the moment of arming to use as a "Safe" reference point.
*   **Latched State Feedback**: Designing an alarm that stays "Active" even if the trigger condition is removed (e.g., if the thief puts the bike back down).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Buzzer**
*   **Push Button** (Security Disarm/Reset)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Sensor** | GP14 | Input | Kickstand Angle |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Digital | Siren |
| **Reset Btn** | GP15 | Input | Disarm Key |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_alarm_armed**: Boolean (Set to TRUE when the user finishes parking the bike).
*   **safe_parked_state**: Integer (The 0 or 1 value recorded at the moment of arming).
*   **is_siren_active**: Boolean (A persistent flag that keeps the buzzer screaming until a reset).
*   **alert_repetition_count**: Integer (Tracks how many times the siren has fired for logs).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: State Snapshot Snapshot**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** the current value into **safe_parked_state**.
3.  **Action**: Flash an LED 3 times to indicate the system is now "ARMED".

**Phase B: Differential Monitoring**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is **is_alarm_armed** TRUE?
5.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Compare** the live sensor to **safe_parked_state**.
6.  **Action**: If the values are NO LONGER identical, set **is_siren_active** to TRUE.

**Phase C: Latched Siren Response**
7.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **While** **is_siren_active** is TRUE, pulse the buzzer.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if Pin **15** (Reset Button) is pressed.
9.  **Validation**: Arm the system. Lift the bike. The alarm starts. Put the bike back on its stand—the alarm should keep screaming! Only pressing the button should stop it.
10. **Result**: A security system that cannot be fooled by returning the sensor to its "Safe" position.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico takes a "Picture" of the tilt sensor's current angle.
2.  **Process**: It armors the system and begins a constant comparison loop.
3.  **Action**: If the bike is moved even slightly from its "Parked" angle, the Pico detects the discrepancy.
4.  **Reaction**: It "Latches" the alarm into an permanent ON state.
5.  **Result**: The owner is alerted to the theft attempt even if the thief is long gone.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Phase A: State Snapshot Snapshot
print("--- SYSTEM ARMING: PARK BIKE NOW ---")
utime.sleep(3)
safe_state = tilt.value()
is_armed = True
is_siren_active = False
print(f"ARMED. Baseline: {safe_state}")

while True:
    # Phase B: Differential Monitoring
    if is_armed and not is_siren_active:
        current_state = tilt.value()
        if current_state != safe_state:
            is_siren_active = True
            print("!! THEFT ATTEMPT DETECTED !!")

    # Phase C: Latched Siren Response
    if is_siren_active:
        buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100)
        buzzer.value(0); utime.sleep_ms(100)
        
        # Check for Security Disarm
        if reset_btn.value() == 0:
            is_siren_active = False
            is_armed = False
            print("System Disarmed.")
            
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Power Cycle Loophole**: If a thief disconnects the battery, the alarm stops. In a real system, you would save the `is_siren_active` flag to Flash memory (Project 1344) so it resumes screaming as soon as power returns.
*   **Sensitivity Drift**: If the bike is on soft grass, it might sink and trigger a false alarm. Always add a 500ms "Confirmation Delay" (Project 1382).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Disarm Keycode**: Instead of a simple button, require the user to press a sequence (Project 1364) to stop the alarm.

## 1. Project 1384: Advanced Tilt Sensor Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Smart Hydration Interval Tracker & Inactivity Reminders". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a hydration monitor that attaches to a water bottle. The Pico will detect when you take a "Sip" (tilt event) and automatically reset an internal countdown timer. If you haven't taken a drink in over 60 minutes, the Pico will "Nag" you with a visual SOS signal until you hydrate.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Inactivity Monitoring**: The logic of triggering an event based on the *absence* of sensor data for a specific duration.
*   **Reset Triggers**: Using a physical action (tilting the bottle) as an interrupt to clear a software accumulation buffer.
*   **Positive Reinforcement Loops**: Using technology to prompt healthy habits based on real-time behavior tracking.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **RGB LED**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Bottle Tilt** | GP14 | Input | Pulse Feedback |
| **Nag LED** | GP15 | PWM | Alert Light |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **last_sip_timestamp**: Integer (The clock time in seconds when the bottle was last tilted).
*   **seconds_since_drink**: Integer (The live difference between 'now' and the last sip).
*   **hydration_threshold_s**: Integer (The 3600-second limit before the reminder starts).
*   **is_user_active**: Boolean (A flag that pauses tracking if the bottle is laid flat for storage).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Event Polling & Calibration**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the bottle is tilted (Drink detected).
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **last_sip_timestamp** to the current `utime.time()`.

**Phase B: Temporal Validation logic**
4.  From **Math**, drag `pico_subtraction`. **Calculate** the **seconds_since_drink**.
5.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`. **Show** the time remaining until the next reminder.
6.  **Action**: Ensure that a single tilt only resets the timer once to avoid double-counting.

**Phase C: Visual Notification Execution**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is **seconds_since_drink** > **3600**?
8.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_pwm`. **Blink** the LED slowly in RED to signal dehydration.
9.  **Validation**: Wait 10 seconds (simulated hour). Watch the LED start to blink. Tilt the bottle once. The LED should turn off instantly and stay off for another 10 seconds.
10. **Result**: A simple, unobtrusive health assistant that ensures you stay hydrated during long work sessions.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins a 60-minute countdown.
2.  **Process**: It watches the bottle for any movement.
3.  **Action**: If you drink, the Pico says "Great job!" and restarts the 60-minute clock.
4.  **Reaction**: If you forget for an hour, the Pico starts "Begging" for you to drink via the LED.
5.  **Result**: Better hydration through behavioral electronics.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

last_sip = utime.time()
LIMIT = 3600 # 1 Hour

print("--- HYDRATION TRACKER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Event Polling
    # Note: Tilt switch triggers when bottle is angled
    is_drinking = tilt.value() == 0
    
    if is_drinking:
        # Phase B: Temporal Validation logic
        print("Sip Detected. Refreshing Timer...")
        last_sip = utime.time()
        utime.sleep(2) # Prevent rapid re-triggers
        
    # Phase C: Visual Notification Execution
    elapsed = utime.time() - last_sip
    
    if elapsed > LIMIT:
        print(f"!! REMINDER: You haven't drank in {elapsed}s !!")
        # Pulse LED
        led.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100)
        led.value(0); utime.sleep_ms(900)
    else:
        led.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Battery Drain**: Using `ticks_ms` for long hour-scale measurements. For hour-long timers, `utime.time()` is more memory-efficient and easier to read.
*   **False Sips**: If you carry the bottle in your bag, the vibration might trigger "False Sips." In a real device, you would use an accelerometer (Project 1344) to ensure the motion matches a drinking profile.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sip Counter**: Keep a tally of how many total sips you took in a day and display it on an OLED (Project 1341).

## 1. Project 1385: Automated Advanced Tilt Sensor Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Physical Gravity-Vector Gaming & Paddle Physics". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a unique human-computer interface (HCI) that turns an entire breadboard into a physical "Game Controller." You will map binary Tilt Left/Right signals into directional movement vectors to control a Paddle in a digital Pong game on an OLED display.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HCI (Human-Computer Interaction)**: Designing input systems that go beyond traditional buttons and mice by using gravity and physical orientation.
*   **Vector Accumulation**: Translating discrete switch states into continuous movement by adding or subtracting "Velocity" values from a coordinate variable.
*   **Boundary Clamping**: The logic required to ensure a digital object (like a paddle) stays within the physical resolution of the display (e.g., 0 to 64 pixels).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Tilt Switches** (SW-520D)
*   **OLED Display** (SSD1306 128x64)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Up** | GP14 | Input | Paddle Up |
| **Tilt Down** | GP15 | Input | Paddle Down |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C | Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C | Clock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_addition`.
*   From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_rectangle`.

### 7. Variables
*   **paddle_y_coordinate**: Integer (The vertical position of the player's paddle).
*   **movement_speed_pixels**: Integer (How many pixels the paddle moves per loop cycle).
*   **is_up_tilted**: Boolean (The live state of the 'Up' gravity switch).
*   **is_down_tilted**: Boolean (The live state of the 'Down' gravity switch).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Input Vectoring**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** Pin **14** (Up) and Pin **15** (Down).
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **is_up_tilted** and **is_down_tilted**.
3.  **Action**: Tilt the breadboard forward and backward to verify the balls are rolling correctly in the switches.

**Phase B: Coordinate Accumulation logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if **is_up_tilted** is TRUE.
5.  From **Math**, drag `pico_subtraction`. **Decrease** **paddle_y_coordinate** by **5**.
6.  **Action**: Implement "Boundary Clamping" to ensure the paddle doesn't vanish off the top or bottom of the screen.

**Phase C: Graphics Rendering Execution**
7.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_clear_screen`. **Wipe** the old paddle position.
8.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_rectangle`. **Draw** the new paddle at the updated **paddle_y_coordinate**.
9.  **Validation**: Run the code and tilt the board. The paddle should slide smoothly up and down the side of the screen as if "Falling" in the direction you tilt.
10. **Result**: A physical gravity-based game controller that feels tactile and responsive.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters the main game loop at 60Hz.
2.  **Process**: It checks which way gravity is currently "Pulling" the tilt sensors.
3.  **Action**: It moves the digital paddle variable based on the physical angle of the board.
4.  **Reaction**: It enforces "Walls" so the paddle stays in play.
5.  **Result**: Precise physical control over digital game elements.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
# Assuming library 'ssd1306' is available
# import ssd1306

s_up = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
s_down = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# Initialization
paddle_y = 30
PADDLE_SIZE = 10
SCREEN_HEIGHT = 64

print("--- PONG GRAVITY CONTROLLER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Input Vectoring
    up_active = s_up.value() == 0
    down_active = s_down.value() == 0
    
    # Phase B: Coordinate Accumulation logic
    if up_active:
        paddle_y -= 5
    if down_active:
        paddle_y += 5
        
    # Phase B: Boundary Clamping
    if paddle_y < 0:
        paddle_y = 0
    if paddle_y > (SCREEN_HEIGHT - PADDLE_SIZE):
        paddle_y = (SCREEN_HEIGHT - PADDLE_SIZE)
        
    # Phase C: Graphics Rendering Execution
    # Simulation: Print position to serial
    print(f"PADDLE POS: {'#' * (paddle_y//2)}|")
    
    # Real OLED implementation would happen here:
    # oled.fill(0)
    # oled.rect(0, paddle_y, 4, PADDLE_SIZE, 1)
    # oled.show()
    
    utime.sleep_ms(30) # Refresh rate sync
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Invisible Paddle"**: Forgetting to clamp the coordinates. If `paddle_y` becomes -500, the user will have to tilt the board for a long time before the paddle reappears.
*   **Diagonal Conflict**: If both tilt switches trigger at once, the paddle will stutter. Always prioritize one direction or cancel them out.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Inertia Physics**: Instead of moving a fixed 5 pixels, make the paddle "Speed Up" the longer you hold the tilt.

## 1. Project 1386: Advanced Tilt Sensor Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Industrial Tip-Over Deadman Interlock". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a fail-safe safety system for high-power appliances (Heaters, Drills, or Fans). You will implement a "Security Latch" that immediately cuts power to a relay if the device is tilted more than 30 degrees and requires a human reset to prevent accidental re-starts.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fail-Safe Architecture**: Designing a circuit where the "Natural" state (no power/broken wire) is the safest possible outcome (Power Cut).
*   **Deadman Interlocking**: A logic gate that prevents operation unless a specific safety condition (Upright position) is met.
*   **Service Requirement Lockout**: A software state that permanantly blocks execution after an error occurs until a "Service Mode" button is pressed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Relay Module** (Simulated with LED)
*   **Push Button** (Safety Reset)
*   **Active Buzzer** (Fault Alarm)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP14 | Input | Tip Sensor |
| **Relay/LED** | GP16 | Digital | Power Load |
| **Reset Button**| GP15 | Input | Tech Reset |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Control**, drag `pico_wait_until`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_power_allowed**: Boolean (The master gate that allows the relay to energize).
*   **fault_tripped**: Boolean (A latching flag that keeps the system dead after a tip).
*   **current_tilt_status**: Integer (Reading from the tip sensor).
*   **rearm_button_state**: Integer (Reading from the reset key).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Fault Detection Handshake**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Monitor** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the sensor is 0 (Tipped over).
3.  **Action**: If tipped, immediately set **is_power_allowed** to FALSE and **fault_tripped** to TRUE.

**Phase B: Security Interlock logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Set** Pin **16** (Relay) to match **is_power_allowed**.
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Block** all operation while **fault_tripped** is TRUE.
6.  **Action**: Sound a "Service Alarm" on the buzzer to alert the user that the device has failed safely.

**Phase C: Safe Service Restoration**
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if Pin **15** (Reset) is pressed.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_and`. **Ensure** the tilt sensor is *also* back in the "Upright" (1) position.
9.  **Validation**: Turn the device on. Knock it over—the LED must turn OFF instantly. Stand it back up—it must stay OFF. Confirm that only the button restores power.
10. **Result**: A safety system that meets industrial standards for personell protection.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico energizes the appliance relay and monitors gravity.
2.  **Process**: If the device falls, the Pico "Breaks" the internal logical chain.
3.  **Action**: It cuts power to the load and logs a "Fatal Fault."
4.  **Reaction**: It blocks the user from simply standing it up to "Fix" it, requiring an intentional reset.
5.  **Result**: Zero accidental fires or injuries from tipped appliances.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
relay = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

is_faulted = False

print("--- INDUSTRIAL SAFETY INTERLOCK ARMED ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Fault Detection Handshake
    current_angle = tilt.value()
    
    if current_angle == 0:
        # Phase B: Security Interlock logic
        is_faulted = True
        relay.value(0) # KILL POWER
        print("!! FATAL FAULT: TIP-OVER DETECTED - POWER CUT !!")
        
    if is_faulted:
        # Phase C: Safe Service Restoration
        # Must be UPRIGHT AND Button Pressed to reset
        if tilt.value() == 1 and reset_btn.value() == 0:
            print("Technician Reset Confirmed. Restoring Power...")
            is_faulted = False
            utime.sleep(1) # Debounce
            
    else:
        # Standard Operation
        relay.value(1) # Power Active
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Soft" Reset**: Allowing the system to reset when still tilted. If the heater is lying on its face, resetting it will immediately start a fire. Always require `Tilt == Upright` in the reset logic.
*   **Latency Delay**: Putting long `utime.sleep(10)` calls in the loop. Safety checks must happen at least 20 times per second (50ms).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Heartbeat LED**: Pulse a Green LED when running safely, and a fast Red LED during a Fault state to differentiate from a dead battery.

## 1. Project 1387: Advanced Tilt Sensor Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Package Delivery Drop Logger & Flash Archiving". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Black-Box" forensic recorder for fragile shipping. You will learn to use the Pico's internal flash memory to persistently log "Shock Events" (rapid tilts or inversions) providing a permanent record of package mishandling that survives battery failure.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Forensic Archiving**: The practice of storing sensor data in non-volatile memory so it can be retrieved later for auditing or legal proof.
*   **Flash File System (OS Module)**: Learning to interface with the internal MicroPython file system using `open()`, `write()`, and `close()` to manage data persistence.
*   **Event-Based Throttling**: Implementing logic to ensure a single physical drop doesn't write 1,000 identical lines to the log file in a single second.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Red LED**
*   **Battery Pack** (For portable logging)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP14 | Input | Shock Sensor |
| **Status LED** | GP15 | Digital | Write Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_open_file`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **recorded_event_id**: Integer (A serialized counter for every drop detected).
*   **log_file_handle**: File (The pointer to the internal text file on the Pico flash).
*   **is_inverted**: Boolean (True if the package is currently upside down).
*   **boot_timestamp_s**: Integer (Seconds elapsed since the logger was powered on).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Shock Detection Handshake**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Sample** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the state is 0 (Shock/Inversion).
3.  **Action**: Identify the exact moment the metal ball moves, indicating the package has been tossed or flipped.

**Phase B: Flash Archive Execution logic**
4.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_to_file`. **Open** "shocks.txt".
5.  **Action**: Write a string containing the current `utime.time()` and an "EVENT DETECTED" message.
6.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Blink** the Red LED (Pin 15) to confirm the write was successful.

**Phase C: Forensics Verification**
7.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Wait** until the sensor is upright again before allowing another log.
8.  **Validation**: Shake the breadboard once. Connect the Pico to your computer. Open the "Thonny" file explorer and check if "shocks.txt" contains the timestamp.
9.  **Result**: A tamper-proof record of transit quality that proves exactly when a package was mishandled.
10. **Result**: Your logger is now a certified "Black Box" for industrial logistics.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters "Stealth Mode" and monitors internal gravity.
2.  **Process**: If a shock occurs, the Pico wakes up the flash memory controller.
3.  **Action**: It "Commits" the crime (the drop) to a permanent digital file.
4.  **Reaction**: It waits for the sensor to stabilize to prevent log files from growing too large.
5.  **Result**: Reliable, persistent evidence for shipping disputes.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime
import os

# Phase A: Shock Detection Handshake
tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
FILENAME = "transit_audit.log"

print("--- FRAGILE SENSOR ARMED: LOGGING TO FLASH ---")

# Ensure the file exists
if FILENAME not in os.listdir():
    with open(FILENAME, "w") as f:
        f.write("LOG START\n")

while True:
    # Monitor for shock (0 = active)
    if tilt.value() == 0:
        # Phase B: Flash Archive Execution logic
        led.value(1) # Visual confirmation of write
        try:
            with open(FILENAME, "a") as log:
                log.write(f"SHOCK EVENT: {utime.time()}s since boot\n")
            print("Drop Recorded to Flash.")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Write Error: {e}")
            
        # Throttling Phase: Wait until upright to prevent log flooding
        while tilt.value() == 0:
            utime.sleep_ms(100)
            
        led.value(0)
    
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Power-Cut Corruption**: If the battery dies *exactly* while the Pico is writing to the file, the file could become corrupted. In a real project, use `f.flush()` and `os.sync()` to minimize this risk.
*   **The "Infinite Log"**: Forgetting the `while tilt.value() == 0` check. Without it, the Pico will write 1,000 lines for a single 1-second drop, filling up the memory in minutes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Deletion**: Add logic that deletes the oldest log file if the Pico's memory is more than 90% full (Project 1353).
