## 1. Project 1388: Smart Advanced Tilt Sensor System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Self-Leveling Solar Inclinometer & Mode Switching". At the end of this project, you will be able to build an intelligent orienting system for solar energy harvesting. You will use a tilt switch as a "Mode Gate" to determine if a solar panel is in its "Deployed" state (producing power) or "Stowed" state (safe from wind).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Inclinometer Mapping**: Converting a binary gravity switch into an approximate angle-of-tilt for mechanical structures.
*   **State Machine Mode-Switching**: Using sensor inputs to toggle between different operational profiles (e.g., Performance Mode vs. Safety Mode).
*   **Energy-Aware Orchestration**: Disabling heavy mechanical motors (like solar trackers) if the panel is not in the correct physical orientation.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **OLED Display**
*   **Servo Motor** (Simulated Solar Pivot)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Panel Tilt**| GP14 | Input | Angle Monitor |
| **Tracker Mtr**| GP16 | PWM | Servo Control |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C | Status Panel |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Control**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_string`.
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_set_servo_angle`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_operational_mode**: String (Either "PROTECTED" or "HARVESTING").
*   **is_stowed_safe**: Boolean (True if the panel is at a 90-degree vertical angle).
*   **harvesting_efficiency**: Integer (A calculated % based on the tilt angle).
*   **tracker_angle**: Integer (The current position of the solar tracking motor).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Mechanical Sampling Handshake**
1.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** Pin **14**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the switch is 1 (Upright).
3.  **Action**: If Upright, set **current_operational_mode** to "STOWED" (Safe from wind).

**Phase B: Harvest Mode Orchestration logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the switch is 0 (Angled towards sun).
5.  From **Motion & Motors**, drag `pico_set_servo_angle`. **Enable** the sun-tracking logic only while tilted.
6.  **Action**: Shutdown the motor if the mode is "STOWED" to save battery power during storms.

**Phase C: Status Dashboard Execution**
7.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_string`. **Print** the current mode and "EFFICIENCY: 100%" on the screen.
8.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`. **Update** the display every 500ms.
9.  **Validation**: Lay the panel flat—the screen should say "ACTIVE". Lift it to a 90-degree standing position—the screen must switch to "STOWED" and the motor should stop moving.
10. **Result**: An smart solar controller that protects its own hardware based on physical orientation.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the solar panel's current angle against the horizon.
2.  **Process**: It decides if the panel is in the correct "Work Zone" for solar harvesting.
3.  **Action**: If tilted, it begins the precision sun-tracking math.
4.  **Reaction**: If the panel is moved to a vertical position (by a user or wind), it kills the motors immediately.
5.  **Result**: Optimized power generation with built-in storm protection.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
# Simulation: LED represents the tracker motor
tracker = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

def update_display(mode):
    print(f"SYSTEM STATUS: {mode}")
    if mode == "HARVESTING":
        print("[####----] 50% Efficiency")
    else:
        print("[--------] 0% (Stowed)")

print("--- SOLAR INCLINOMETER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Mechanical Sampling
    is_upright = tilt.value() == 1
    
    if is_upright:
        # Phase B: Mode Orchestration (Stowed)
        current_mode = "MODE: STOWED (STORM SAFE)"
        tracker.value(0) # Disable motors
    else:
        # Phase B: Mode Orchestration (Harvesting)
        current_mode = "MODE: HARVESTING"
        tracker.value(1) # Enable motors
        
    # Phase C: Status Dashboard Execution
    update_display(current_mode)
    
    utime.sleep(1) # Slow sampling for battery saving
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Motor Twitch**: Rapidly switching modes if the panel is vibrating. Always add a 2-second "Decision Hysteresis" (Project 1382).
*   **Servo Friction**: Attempting to move a servo when the structure is in "Stowed" mode. This can burn out the motor. Always verify physical clearance in code.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Axis tracking**: Add a second tilt switch (Project 1381) to detect both Pitch and Roll for 3D sun tracking.

## 1. Project 1389: Advanced Tilt Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Axis Leveling & Gradient Estimation". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-style digital spirit level. You will learn to use multiple tilt sensors as a "Balance Matrix" to identify which side of a surface is too high, and use an OLED to provide "Gradient Instructions" (Arrows) to help a user level a table or machine.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Balance Matrixing**: Using pairs of sensors mounted in opposition to divide the physical world into "Too High", "Too Low", or "Perfect" regions.
*   **Gradient Indicator UI**: Designing a user interface that provides directional corrections (e.g., "Raise Left side") based on raw digital sensor triggers.
*   **Logical Null-Point**: Defining a physical state where multiple sensors are triggered simultaneously as the "Zero Point" or "Home" position.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x Tilt Switches**
*   **Buzzer**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Left** | GP14 | Input | Left Slope |
| **Tilt Right** | GP15 | Input | Right Slope |
| **Level Buzzer**| GP16 | Digital | Balance Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_arrow`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_boolean_and`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_left_high**: Boolean (True if the left side of the table is raised).
*   **is_right_high**: Boolean (True if the right side of the table is raised).
*   **is_perfectly_level**: Boolean (Calculated by checking if both sensors are in balance).
*   **correction_arrow_dir**: Integer (0 for None, 1 for Left, 2 for Right).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Level Sampling Handshake**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`. **Read** Pin **14** and **15**.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **is_left_high** and **is_right_high**.
3.  **Action**: Mount the sensors at 5-degree angles to the left and right of the center.

**Phase B: Alignment Logic logic**
4.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Check** if both sensors are 1.
5.  **Action**: If both are open, the board is within the 10-degree "Safe Zone" and is considered "LEVEL".
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if Left is 0. This means the board is tilted too far left.

**Phase C: Gradient Indication Execution**
7.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_string`. **Print** "<- LIFT LEFT SIDE" if the left side is low.
8.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Play** a high-pitched beep only when **is_perfectly_level** is TRUE.
9.  **Validation**: Place the board on a flat table. The buzzer should sound. Slide a coin under one side—the buzzer should stop and an arrow should point to the coin.
10. **Result**: A high-precision digital tool that replaces traditional bubble levels.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters "Calibration Mode" and checks for horizontal balance.
2.  **Process**: It compares the two "Gravity Slopes" to find the peak.
3.  **Action**: It translates the physical imbalance into a human-readable correction.
4.  **Reaction**: It provides audio confirmation once the "Null Point" is achieved.
5.  **Result**: Perfectly level installations every time.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

s_left = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
s_right = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("--- DIGITAL LEVEL ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase A: Level Sampling Handshake
    l = s_left.value()
    r = s_right.value()
    
    # Phase B: Alignment Logic logic
    # In this mounting, 1 = Upright/Level
    if l == 1 and r == 1:
        # Phase C: Gradient Indication Execution
        print("[   PERFECTLY LEVEL   ]")
        buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(10) # Chirp
        buzzer.value(0)
    elif l == 0:
        print("[ << LIFT LEFT SIDE ]")
    elif r == 0:
        print("[ LIFT RIGHT SIDE >> ]")
        
    utime.sleep_ms(200)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Hysteresis" Jitter**: If the sensors are mounted too close to 0 degrees, mechanical vibration will make the buzzer stutter. Always mount them at a small 5-10 degree "V" shape to create a stable "Dead Zone."
*   **Gravity Lag**: Movement can cause the metal ball to bounce. Always add a small `utime.sleep_ms(100)` to let the ball settle before reading.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **360 Degree Level**: Add two more sensors (Project 1381) to level both the X and Y axes for table legs.

## Batch 140: Advanced Laser Tripwire 4 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1391: Advanced Laser Tripwire Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Differential Daylight Rejection & Ambient Cancellation". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a laser receiver that works in direct sunlight. You will learn to measure the ambient light (Laser Off), measure the signal (Laser On), and calculate the difference to create a robust, high-contrast trigger that ignores background noise.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential Measurement**: Measuring a signal relative to a changing background (ambient light) to isolate the desired data.
*   **Ambient Cancellation**: Subtracting environmental noise from a reading to increase the signal-to-noise ratio.
*   **Dynamic Thresholding**: Adjusting your trigger sensitivity automatically based on current lighting conditions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Output| Signal source |
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Sunlight Sensor|

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_subtraction`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **ambient_lux_level**: Integer (The LDR reading while the laser is OFF).
*   **active_signal_level**: Integer (The LDR reading while the laser is ON).
*   **differential_contrast**: Integer (The calculated difference between the two states).
*   **is_beam_blocked**: Boolean (True if the differential contrast drops below a threshold).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Ambient Light Sampling**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Turn** the laser OFF.
2.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Wait** 10ms for the ADC to settle.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** the **LDR Signal** and save to **ambient_lux_level**.

**Phase B: Differential Analysis logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Turn** the laser ON.
5.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_ms`. **Wait** 10ms.
6.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Read** the **LDR Signal** and save to **active_signal_level**.
7.  From **Math**, drag `pico_subtraction`. **Find** the **differential_contrast** (abs(**active_signal_level** - **ambient_lux_level**)).

**Phase C: Adaptive Thresholding Execution**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is **differential_contrast** < 10000?
9.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print_labeled`. **Display** "TRIPWIRE BROKEN" if the contrast is low.
10. **Validation**: Shine a bright flashlight at the LDR while the program is running. The "Signal" should stay the same (because the flashlight affects both the ON and OFF readings), proving the system rejects ambient light.
11. **Result**: A security sensor that works in any environment, from dark basements to sunny backyards.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico cycles the laser power at high frequency (50Hz).
2.  **Process**: It captures two snapshots: "Environment Only" and "Environment + Laser."
3.  **Action**: It subtracts the environment to find the "Pure Laser" intensity.
4.  **Reaction**: It ignores slow changes in sunlight and only reacts to sudden beam breaks.
5.  **Result**: High-reliability, sunlight-immune security detection.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Ambient Light Sampling
laser = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
ldr = machine.ADC(26)

THRESHOLD = 5000

print("--- DIFFERENTIAL LASER ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Read Ambient
    laser.value(0)
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
    ambient = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Phase B: Differential Analysis logic
    # Read Signal
    laser.value(1)
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
    active = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Calculate Contrast
    contrast = abs(active - ambient)
    
    # Phase C: Adaptive Thresholding Execution
    # If contrast is small, it means the laser isn't reaching the sensor
    if contrast < THRESHOLD:
        print(f"!! ALERT: BEAM BROKEN !! (Contrast: {contrast})")
    else:
        # print(f"Signal Strength: {contrast}") # For debugging
        pass
        
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Inversion Trap**: Some LDR circuits decrease ADC values as light increases. Using `abs()` ensures your math works regardless of circuit polarity.
*   **Sampling Speed**: If you cycle too fast, the LDR won't have time to "Recover" between pulses. 10ms is the minimum reliable window for standard CDS cells.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Calibration**: Add a button that records the "Perfect Beam" contrast and sets the threshold to 50% of that value automatically.

## 1. Project 1392: Advanced Laser Tripwire Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Precision Sports Finish Line & Race Timing". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a millisecond-accurate race timer. You will learn to use two laser gates (Start and Finish) and implement "State-Locked Timing" to ensure that the race only ends if it has properly started.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Elapsed Time Delta**: Using `ticks_diff` to calculate the exact duration between two IRQ events.
*   **Hot-Start Logic**: A state machine that ignores the finish line until the start line has been crossed.
*   **Millisecond Resolution**: Converting raw CPU ticks into human-readable seconds with 3 decimal places.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Lasers**
*   **2x LDRs**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Start** | GP26 | ADC | Gate 1 |
| **LDR Finish**| GP27 | ADC | Gate 2 |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **start_timestamp_ms**: Integer (The timestamp when the first beam was broken).
*   **finish_timestamp_ms**: Integer (The timestamp when the second beam was broken).
*   **is_race_running**: Boolean (Tracks if a runner is currently between the gates).
*   **final_elapsed_seconds**: Float (The final recalculated race time).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Monitoring Status Handshake**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Monitor** **LDR Start**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is the beam broken?
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the current `utime.ticks_ms()` in **start_timestamp_ms**.
4.  **Action**: Set **is_race_running** to TRUE and beep the buzzer.

**Phase B: Temporal Validation logic**
5.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Wait** while **is_race_running** is TRUE.
6.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Monitor** **LDR Finish**.
7.  **Action**: Filter out any tiny "Glitches" by ensuring the finish beam is broken for at least 10ms.

**Phase C: Scoring Execution**
8.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** the difference between now and **start_timestamp_ms**.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Convert** to seconds by dividing by 1000.
10. **Validation**: Walk through Gate 1. Wait 2 seconds. Walk through Gate 2. The console should print "TIME: 2.05s".
11. **Result**: A professional-grade sports timing system for sprinting, model cars, or drones.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters a "Ready" state, watching the start gate.
2.  **Process**: It captures the exact microsecond the runner leaves the blocks.
3.  **Action**: It locks out the start gate and arms the finish gate.
4.  **Reaction**: It captures the finish time and calculates the speed delta.
5.  **Result**: Precise race results displayed instantly on the serial monitor.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Monitoring Status Handshake
gate_start = machine.ADC(26)
gate_finish = machine.ADC(27)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

t_start = 0
running = False

print("--- LASER RACE TIMER READY ---")

while True:
    # Wait for race start
    if not running:
        if gate_start.read_u16() < 40000: # Adjust based on ambient
            t_start = utime.ticks_ms()
            running = True
            print(">> GO! GO! GO! <<")
            buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(100); buzzer.value(0)
            utime.sleep(0.5) # Prevent double trigger
            
    # Phase B: Temporal Validation logic
    else:
        if gate_finish.read_u16() < 40000:
            t_finish = utime.ticks_ms()
            
            # Phase C: Scoring Execution
            elapsed = utime.ticks_diff(t_finish, t_start)
            seconds = elapsed / 1000
            
            print(f"** FINISH! ** Time: {seconds:.3f} seconds")
            buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(500); buzzer.value(0)
            
            running = False
            print("System Resetting in 3s...")
            utime.sleep(3)
            print("READY...")
            
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Hot-Start Trap**: Forgetting to add a small delay (0.5s) after the start. Without it, the runner's back leg might trigger the start line a second time, resetting the clock.
*   **LDR Thresholds**: Ambient light levels change. If your race starts in the morning and ends in the afternoon, you must re-calibrate your 40000 threshold.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reaction Timer**: Add a Green LED. Make the Pico wait a random time, then turn on the LED and start the clock. See how fast the user can break the laser!

## 1. Project 1393: Advanced Laser Tripwire Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Rhythmic Laser Passcode & Temporal Pattern Matching". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a secure optical lock that only opens if the beam is broken in a specific rhythm (like Morse code). You will learn to measure the duration of "Blocks" and "Clear Air" using high-speed timing to create a temporal secret key for secure entry systems.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Temporal Pattern Matching**: The science of recognizing a "Secret Knock" or rhythm in the time domain rather than a static password.
*   **Interval Quantization**: Classifying analog time durations into discrete binary symbols (e.g., "Short" vs. "Long" pulses).
*   **Buffer Windowing**: Implementing a timeout that automatically submits or clears the password buffer after a period of inactivity.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Pulse Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Digital | Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Lists**, drag `pico_append_to_list`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **beam_break_start**: Integer (The millisecond timestamp when the beam was first interrupted).
*   **pulse_duration_ms**: Integer (The total time the laser was blocked).
*   **input_pattern_buffer**: List (A sequence of 0s and 1s representing the user's rhythm).
*   **is_unlock_successful**: Boolean (True if the input buffer matches the secret master key).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Observation & Pulse Capture**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Poll** the **LDR Signal**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is the beam currently blocked?
3.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`. **Capture** the **beam_break_start** time.
4.  **Action**: Wait for the beam to clear, then calculate **pulse_duration_ms**.

**Phase B: Pattern Mapping logic**
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Check**: Is **pulse_duration_ms** > 500ms?
6.  From **Lists**, drag `pico_append_to_list`. **Add** a '1' (Long) or '0' (Short) to the **input_pattern_buffer**.
7.  **Action**: Beep the buzzer briefly for every recorded tap to provide haptic feedback.

**Phase C: Access Control Execution**
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the buffer length is 4.
9.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_boolean_and`. **Compare** **input_pattern_buffer** to the secret code `[0, 1, 0, 1]`.
10. **Validation**: Tap the beam: "Short, Long, Short, Long". The Pico should print "ACCESS GRANTED". Tap any other rhythm and it should print "ACCESS DENIED" and reset.
11. **Result**: A highly secure "Invisible Key" system that cannot be picked or stolen, only performed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the "Optical Silence" of the laser beam.
2.  **Process**: It translates physical interruptions into a binary array based on time.
3.  **Action**: It validates the timing against a pre-authorized "Temporal Signature."
4.  **Reaction**: It triggers a lock/buzzer or resets the security buffer if the window expires.
5.  **Result**: Time-based rhythmic authentication for specialized security gates.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Observation & Pulse Capture
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

SECRET_CODE = [0, 1, 0, 1] # Short, Long, Short, Long
buffer = []

print("--- RHYTHMIC LOCK ACTIVE ---")

def get_pulse():
    # Wait for break
    while ldr.read_u16() > 40000:
        utime.sleep_ms(10)
    
    t_start = utime.ticks_ms()
    buzzer.value(1) # Feedback
    
    # Wait for release
    while ldr.read_u16() < 40000:
        utime.sleep_ms(10)
    
    buzzer.value(0)
    return utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), t_start)

while True:
    duration = get_pulse()
    
    # Phase B: Pattern Mapping logic
    # Classify as 0 (Short) or 1 (Long)
    if duration > 500:
        buffer.append(1)
        print("Pulse: LONG")
    else:
        buffer.append(0)
        print("Pulse: SHORT")
        
    # Phase C: Access Control Execution
    if len(buffer) == len(SECRET_CODE):
        if buffer == SECRET_CODE:
            print(">> ACCESS GRANTED <<")
            # Rapid chirp
            for _ in range(3):
                buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(50); buzzer.value(0); utime.sleep_ms(50)
        else:
            print("!! ACCESS DENIED !!")
            buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep_ms(1000); buzzer.value(0)
            
        buffer = [] # Reset for next attempt
        print("\nReady for Code...")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Quantization Error**: If you tap at exactly 500ms, the system might flip-flop. Always make your rhythm "Exaggerated" (e.g., Short=200ms, Long=800ms) to ensure reliability.
*   **Ambient Jitter**: In low light, the LDR might "Flicker" near the threshold. Ensure your laser is focused and your `40000` ADC threshold is confirmed in Section 1391.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Timeout Auto-Reset**: Add a timer that clears the `buffer` if the user takes more than 3 seconds between taps, preventing someone from "Guessing" the code over an hour.

## 1. Project 1394: Advanced Laser Tripwire Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Beam Health & Predictive Optical Maintenance". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a diagnostic monitor that tracks the "Brightness Decay" of your laser path. You will learn to establish a "Day 1 Baseline" and use it to notify the user if a lens is dusty or the laser is losing alignment *before* the security system fails.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Baseline Referencing**: The process of comparing current sensor performance against a "Perfect State" recorded during installation.
*   **Diagnostic Decay**: Recognizing the slow, gradual decrease in signal strength caused by environmental factors like dust or thermal expansion.
*   **Predictive Maintenance**: Using software to schedule hardware cleaning or alignment tasks before an actual system failure occurs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Monitor source|
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Health sensor |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C | Status Panel |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C | Status Panel |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_division`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_text`.

### 7. Variables
*   **calibration_baseline_adc**: Integer (The 'Perfect' reading when the system was first installed).
*   **current_optical_health**: Float (A percentage from 0-100% representing beam quality).
*   **is_maintenance_required**: Boolean (True if the health drops below a safety threshold, e.g., 70%).
*   **diagnostic_sample_buffer**: List (A rolling average of the last 10 readings to prevent false alarms).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Baselining Handshake**
1.  From **User Input**, drag `pico_on_button_press`. **Action**: The user presses a button when the laser is perfectly aligned.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** the current **LDR Signal** into **calibration_baseline_adc**.
3.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_text`. **Show** "BASELINE LOCKED".

**Phase B: Diagnostic Analysis logic**
4.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_every_60_seconds`.
5.  From **Math**, drag `pico_division`. **Calculate** **current_optical_health** as `(Current ADC / Baseline) * 100`.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else_chain`. **Check** if health is < 80%.

**Phase C: Status Signaling Execution**
7.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_text`. **Condition**: If < 50%, Display "CRITICAL: REALIGN NOW".
8.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_text`. **Condition**: If 50-80%, Display "WARN: CLEAN LENS".
9.  **Validation**: Run the system. Slowly slide a semi-transparent piece of plastic (like a grocery bag) in front of the LDR. Watch the Health drop from 100% to 40% and observe the maintenance warnings change.
10. **Result**: A smart maintenance system that ensures 24/7 reliability for long-range security perimeters.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico records the maximum possible signal strength of the installed system.
2.  **Process**: It periodically compares the "Ideal" light intensity to the "Live" intensity.
3.  **Action**: It identifies performance bottlenecks (dust, vibrations, power drop).
4.  **Reaction**: It provides early-warning notifications before the tripwire becomes non-functional.
5.  **Result**: Professional-grade uptime monitoring for optical infrastructure.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Baselining Handshake
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
# Simulation: In a real app, 'baseline' would be saved to Flash/EEPROM
baseline = 62000 

print("--- OPTICAL HEALTH MONITOR ACTIVE ---")

def get_health():
    current = ldr.read_u16()
    # Phase B: Diagnostic Analysis logic
    health = (current / baseline) * 100
    if health > 100: health = 100
    return health

while True:
    health_pct = get_health()
    
    # Phase C: Status Signaling Execution
    print(f"Current Beam Health: {health_pct:.1f}%")
    
    if health_pct < 50:
        print("!! ALERT: System Compromised. Check alignment immediately !!")
    elif health_pct < 85:
        print("Warning: Signal Degraded. Clean lenses or check battery.")
    else:
        print("Status: Optimal.")
        
    print("-" * 20)
    utime.sleep(5) # Slow sampling for stability
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Over-Saturation Error**: If you calibrate while a flashlight is hitting the sensor, your baseline will be impossible to reach with just the laser. Always calibrate in the final lighting environment.
*   **Sampling Jitter**: Atmospheric shimmer (heat waves) can make the beam "Wobble" at long distances. Use a rolling average of 10 samples to prevent the Health % from jumping around.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Thresholding**: Connect this project to Project 1391. If the health drops to 90%, automatically update the tripwire threshold to stay sensitive but reliable.

## 1. Project 1395: Automated Advanced Laser Tripwire Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Conveyor Sorting Logic & Object Sizing". At the end of this project, you will be able to build an industrial quality control system. You will learn to use a laser tripwire to measure the physical length of passing objects (by calculating break duration vs. belt speed) and use a high-speed servo to "Kick" oversized items off the line.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dimensional Logic**: Inferring the physical size of an object based on the time it takes to pass through a fixed point (Shadow Measurement).
*   **Actuator Integration**: Driving physical mechanical hardware (Servo) based on dynamic sensor calculations.
*   **Sorting Throughput**: Implementing counters to track "Pass" vs. "Reject" metrics for industrial efficiency reporting.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **Micro Servo** (MG90S)
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Size sensor |
| **Servo PWM** | GP15 | PWM | Kick arm |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Digital | Rejection alert|

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_pwm`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_ticks_ms`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **object_entry_time**: Integer (The timestamp when the front of the object breaks the beam).
*   **object_exit_time**: Integer (The timestamp when the beam clears).
*   **shadow_length_ms**: Integer (The total time the object was under the laser).
*   **rejection_count**: Integer (Total number of objects kicked off the belt).
*   **size_threshold_ms**: Integer (The 'cutoff' time; objects longer than this are rejected).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Observation & Edge Detection**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Poll** **LDR Signal**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is the beam broken (ADC < 40000)?
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** the **object_entry_time** using `utime.ticks_ms()`.
4.  **Action**: Stay in a loop until the beam is clear again.

**Phase B: Dimensional Analysis logic**
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Capture** the **object_exit_time**.
6.  From **Math**, drag `pico_ticks_diff`. **Calculate** **shadow_length_ms**.
7.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`. **Compare**: Is **shadow_length_ms** > 1000ms?

**Phase C: Sorting & Rejection Execution**
8.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_pwm`. **Move** the Servo to 90 degrees to "Kick" the object.
9.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Sound** the buzzer and increment **rejection_count**.
10. **Validation**: Pass a small matchbox through the laser (quickly). It should pass. Pass a long shoe box through (slowly). The servo should snap out and "Kick" it, accompanied by a beep.
11. **Result**: A functional industrial sorting algorithm that can be adapted for any size-based manufacturing task.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico monitors the "Leading Edge" of objects on the conveyor.
2.  **Process**: It measures the "Time-of-Flight" for each object passing the sensor.
3.  **Action**: It differentiates between "Small Parts" and "Large Obstructions."
4.  **Reaction**: It triggers a mechanical diverter (Servo) to remove non-compliant items.
5.  **Result**: Automated quality control with zero human supervision.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Observation & Edge Detection
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

SIZE_LIMIT = 800 # Milliseconds
rejected = 0

print("--- INDUSTRIAL SORTER ACTIVE ---")

def set_servo(angle):
    # Standard 50Hz Servo mapping
    duty = int((angle / 180 * 2 + 0.5) / 20 * 65535)
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

set_servo(0) # Retract arm

while True:
    if ldr.read_u16() < 40000:
        # Object Entered
        t0 = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Wait for object to exit
        while ldr.read_u16() < 40000:
            utime.sleep_ms(1)
            
        # Phase B: Dimensional Analysis logic
        t1 = utime.ticks_ms()
        length = utime.ticks_diff(t1, t0)
        
        print(f"Object Detected: {length}ms")
        
        # Phase C: Sorting & Rejection Execution
        if length > SIZE_LIMIT:
            print("!! OVERSIZE DETECTED - REJECTING !!")
            rejected += 1
            buzzer.value(1)
            set_servo(90) # Kick
            utime.sleep_ms(500)
            set_servo(0)  # Reset
            buzzer.value(0)
        else:
            print("Size OK - Passing.")

    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Belt Speed Jitter**: If your conveyor belt doesn't move at a constant speed, the `ms` length will be wrong. For real machines, we use an encoder to measure distance, not time.
*   **Servo Stall**: If the object is too heavy, the servo might jam. Use a "High Torque" servo for heavy items.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Categorization**: Add two rejection bins. Objects < 500ms go to Bin A, Objects 500-1000ms go to Bin B, and anything larger is a failure.

## 1. Project 1396: Advanced Laser Tripwire Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Non-Volatile Hazard Latching & Memory persistence". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a security system that "Remembers" it was tripped even if the power is cut by an intruder. You will learn to save the alarm state to the Pico's internal flash memory, ensuring the siren resumes immediately upon reboot until a master "Reset Key" is provided.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistent Latching**: Storing security states in non-volatile memory (NVM) so they survive a complete power loss or system reboot.
*   **Memory Synchronization**: The process of ensuring the physical world state (Beam Break) is immediately mirrored in the digital disk state (JSON file).
*   **Tamper-Resistant Logic**: Designing software that assumes a "Fault" state on boot if the memory indicates the system was previously compromised.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **Buzzer**
*   **Reset Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Status Flash** | Internal | Storage | JSON Format |
| **Laser Beam** | GP26 | ADC | Hazard Sensor |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Digital | Alert Output |
| **Reset Key** | GP14 | Input | Admin Reset |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_save_json`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_digital`.

### 7. Variables
*   **is_latched_in_flash**: Boolean (True if the 'alarm_state.json' file contains a 'tripped' flag).
*   **current_beam_adc**: Integer (The live intensity of the laser signal).
*   **is_reset_requested**: Boolean (True if the secret admin button is being held).
*   **file_write_counter**: Integer (Tracks cumulative writes to monitor flash wear-and-tear).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Persistent Scoping & Setup**
1.  From **Files**, drag `pico_read_json`. **Load** 'alarm_state.json' at startup.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Initialize** **is_latched_in_flash** based on the file contents.
3.  **Action**: If the flag is TRUE, skip directly to the Alarm state without checking the laser.

**Phase B: Verification & Trigger logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Poll** the **Laser Beam**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is the beam broken (ADC < 40000)?
6.  From **Files**, drag `pico_save_json`. **Overwrite** the file with `{"tripped": true}` immediately.

**Phase C: Memory Synchronization Execution**
7.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Scream** the buzzer and light the alert LED.
8.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Condition**: Is the **Reset Key** held AND is the beam clear?
9.  From **Files**, drag `pico_save_json`. **Clear** the flash flag and reset the system.
10. **Validation**: Trip the laser. Unplug the Pico. Wait 10 seconds. Plug it back in. The buzzer should start screaming *before* any other check is performed, proving the memory persistence.
11. **Result**: A professional, industrial-grade security system that cannot be bypassed by simply pulling the plug.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico reads its "Memory Log" to see if it was in an alarm state before losing power.
2.  **Process**: If an alarm was active, it resumes the alert immediately.
3.  **Action**: It continuously synchronizes the physical security state with the internal flash disk.
4.  **Reaction**: It only disarms if a specific combination of "Hardware Clear" and "Software Reset" is met.
5.  **Result**: High-integrity security logging with non-volatile persistence.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import json
import os
import utime

# Phase A: Persistent Scoping & Setup
FILE_NAME = "alarm_state.json"
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
ldr = machine.ADC(26)

def load_latch():
    try:
        with open(FILE_NAME, "r") as f:
            data = json.load(f)
            return data.get("tripped", False)
    except:
        return False

def save_latch(state):
    with open(FILE_NAME, "w") as f:
        json.dump({"tripped": state}, f)

# Elite Standard: Check memory BEFORE entering main loop
is_tripped = load_latch()

print("--- PERSISTENT SECURITY ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    # Phase B: Verification & Trigger logic
    if not is_tripped:
        if ldr.read_u16() < 40000:
            print("!! TRIPWIRE BREACHED !!")
            is_tripped = True
            # Phase C: Memory Synchronization Execution
            save_latch(True)
    
    if is_tripped:
        buzzer.value(1) # ALARM
        # Attempt Reset
        if reset_btn.value() == 0 and ldr.read_u16() > 40000:
            print("Resetting Latch...")
            is_tripped = False
            save_latch(False)
            buzzer.value(0)
            utime.sleep(1)
            
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flash Wear-and-Tear**: Writing to the flash every loop will kill the Pico! Only call `save_latch()` when the state *changes* (e.g., from False to True).
*   **The Boot Loop**: If your JSON file gets corrupted, the Pico might crash on boot. Always wrap `json.load()` in a `try/except` block to ensure the system can still start.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Timestamped Logs**: Use the RTC (Project 1347) to save the exact *Time* the break occurred into the JSON file, so you can see when the intruder arrived.

## 1. Project 1397: Advanced Laser Tripwire Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Bi-Directional Visitor Analytics Counter". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a sophisticated retail footfall counter. You will learn to use two laser tripwires to determine "Vector Direction" (Inbound vs. Outbound) and log hourly totals to a CSV file on the Pico's flash memory for performance analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Vector Directional Logic**: Using the sequence of two triggers (A then B vs. B then A) to determine the physical direction of travel.
*   **Time-Series Bucketing**: Aggregating occurrences into fixed time intervals (e.g., Hourly) to identify peak traffic trends.
*   **CSV Data Streamlining**: Formatting raw integers into standardized Comma-Separated Values for export to data analysis tools like Excel.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Laser Diodes**
*   **2x Photoresistors (LDR)**
*   **RTC Module** (DS3231)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |
| **Gate A (LDR)** | GP26 | ADC | Entrance sensor|
| **Gate B (LDR)** | GP27 | ADC | Exit sensor |
| **RTC SDA** | GP4 | I2C | Timestamping |
| **RTC SCL** | GP5 | I2C | Timestamping |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_csv`.
*   From **Real Time Clock**, drag `pico_get_time_struct`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **inbound_count**: Integer (Cumulative number of people who entered the zone).
*   **outbound_count**: Integer (Cumulative number of people who left the zone).
*   **last_gate_triggered**: Integer (0 for none, 1 for Gate A, 2 for Gate B).
*   **current_iso_timestamp**: String (The current date/time from the RTC).
*   **gate_sequence_timeout**: Integer (The millisecond window allowed between A and B).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Observation & Vector Capture**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Monitor** **Gate A** and **Gate B**.
2.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: If Gate A is broken while **last_gate_triggered** is 0.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **last_gate_triggered** to 1.
4.  **Action**: Wait for Gate B to be broken within 500ms to confirm a "Vector In".

**Phase B: Temporal State Mapping logic**
5.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Increment** **inbound_count** if the B-after-A sequence completes.
6.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check** if the current hour has changed using the **RTC Module**.
7.  **Action**: Prepare a data row: `[Timestamp, In, Out]`.

**Phase C: Data Finalization Execution**
8.  From **Storage**, drag `pico_append_csv`. **Commit** the hourly counts to 'traffic_log.csv'.
9.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Reset** **inbound_count** and **outbound_count** to zero for the next hour.
10. **Validation**: Walk through the beams towards the Pico. The "Inbound" count should increment. Walk away. The "Outbound" count should increment. Open the CSV file to see the formatted log.
11. **Result**: A powerful business intelligence tool that provides actionable data on customer behavior.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the RTC and creates a new CSV header if the file is missing.
2.  **Process**: It analyzes the sequence of beam breaks to differentiate between entries and exits.
3.  **Action**: It stores these events in volatile memory (RAM) until a time-bucket is full.
4.  **Reaction**: At the end of every hour, it flushes the RAM counts to the physical "Hard Drive" (Flash).
5.  **Result**: Long-term directional traffic analytics with minimal storage overhead.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Observation & Vector Capture
gate_a = machine.ADC(26)
gate_b = machine.ADC(27)

in_count = 0
out_count = 0
active_gate = 0 # 0=none, 1=A, 2=B
t_trigger = 0

print("--- DIRECTIONAL ANALYTICS ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    a_val = gate_a.read_u16()
    b_val = gate_b.read_u16()
    
    # Entrance Detection (A then B)
    if active_gate == 0:
        if a_val < 30000:
            active_gate = 1
            t_trigger = utime.ticks_ms()
        elif b_val < 30000:
            active_gate = 2
            t_trigger = utime.ticks_ms()
            
    # Phase B: Temporal State Mapping logic
    else:
        # Check for completion
        if active_gate == 1 and b_val < 30000:
            print(">> PERSON ENTERED")
            in_count += 1
            active_gate = 0
            utime.sleep(0.5) # Avoid double count
        elif active_gate == 2 and a_val < 30000:
            print("<< PERSON LEFT")
            out_count += 1
            active_gate = 0
            utime.sleep(0.5)
            
        # Timeout if they just stood in one beam
        if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), t_trigger) > 2000:
            active_gate = 0
            
    # Phase C: Data Finalization Execution (Simulation)
    # real code would check RTC here
    if utime.ticks_ms() % 60000 == 0: # Every minute for demo
        with open("log.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(f"ENTRY,{in_count},{out_count}\n")
        print(f"Logged: In:{in_count} Out:{out_count}")
        in_count = 0; out_count = 0
        
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sequential Blindness**: If Gate A and Gate B are too far apart (e.g., 2 meters), a person might change direction in the middle, breaking your logic. Keep the sensors within 10-20cm for the best accuracy.
*   **Double-Tripping**: A person's swinging arm might trigger the beam twice. Use a "Cooldown" timer of 500ms after a successful count to ignore noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Capacity Alert**: Subtract Outbound from Inbound. If the result is > 50, sound an alarm (Building at maximum occupancy).

## 1. Project 1398: Smart Advanced Laser Tripwire System

### 2. Learning Objective
"IoT Cloud-Synced Perimeter Security". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a global notification system. You will learn to connect the Pico W to WiFi and send an encrypted HTTPS Webhook to a service (like Discord, Slack, or IFTTT) the instant a perimeter is breached, providing real-time mobile alerts anywhere in the world.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HTTPS POST Handshake**: The secure protocol used by IoT devices to send JSON data to modern web servers and cloud APIs.
*   **Asynchronous Webhooks**: Triggering an external action (like a phone notification) without blocking the local security siren.
*   **Connection Resilience**: Designing a "Reconnection Loop" that ensures the Pico stays online even if the WiFi router reboots.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Wireless version)
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Perimeter Sensor|
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Digital | Local Alert |
| **WiFi Status**| Internal | SPI | Connection Monitor|

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Communication**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`.
*   From **Communication**, drag `pico_http_post`.
*   From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_milliseconds`.
*   From **Logic**, drag `pico_if_else`.

### 7. Variables
*   **wifi_ssid_string**: String (Your network name).
*   **webhook_api_url**: String (The destination URL for the mobile alert).
*   **is_cloud_connected**: Boolean (True if the Pico has a valid IP address).
*   **alert_payload_json**: Dictionary (The data packet: `{"status": "BREACH", "location": "Backyard"}`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Scaffolding & WiFi Setup**
1.  From **Communication**, drag `pico_wifi_connect`. **Enter** your credentials.
2.  From **Loops**, drag `pico_while`. **Condition**: While NOT connected, flash the onboard LED.
3.  From **Text**, drag `pico_print`. **Show** the assigned IP address once online.

**Phase B: Translation & Logic logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Monitor** the **LDR Signal**.
5.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is the beam broken (ADC < 40000)?
6.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_digital`. **Scream** the local buzzer immediately.

**Phase C: Remote Handshake Execution**
7.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Format** the **alert_payload_json** string.
8.  From **Communication**, drag `pico_http_post`. **Send** the data to your **webhook_api_url**.
9.  **Validation**: Break the laser. Ensure the local buzzer sounds instantly. Then, check your smartphone—you should receive a push notification within 2 seconds.
10. **Result**: A state-of-the-art IoT security system that provides global situational awareness.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico joins the local network and establishes a secure socket tunnel.
2.  **Process**: It maintains a high-speed local loop for immediate alarm response.
3.  **Action**: Upon intrusion, it spawns a background task to "Phone Home" to the cloud.
4.  **Reaction**: It waits for a 200 OK response from the server before resuming detection.
5.  **Result**: Hybrid local/remote defense that works even if the local internet is slow.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import network
import urequests
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Scaffolding & WiFi Setup
SSID = "Your_WiFi"
PASS = "Your_Password"
WEBHOOK_URL = "http://your-server.com/api/v1/alert"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Connecting to WiFi...")
while not wlan.isconnected():
    utime.sleep(1)

print(f"Online! IP: {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")

def send_alert():
    # Phase C: Remote Handshake Execution
    print("Breach! Sending Cloud Alert...")
    payload = {"device": "PicoLaser", "event": "Perimeter Breach", "time": utime.time()}
    try:
        # Use urequests for simple HTTP POST
        response = urequests.post(WEBHOOK_URL, json=payload, timeout=5)
        print(f"Server Response: {response.status_code}")
        response.close()
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Cloud Error: {e}")

while True:
    # Phase B: Translation & Logic logic
    if ldr.read_u16() < 40000:
        buzzer.value(1)
        send_alert()
        utime.sleep(5) # Cooldown to avoid spamming the cloud
        buzzer.value(0)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **HTTP Blocking**: Sending a web request can take 1-2 seconds. During this time, the Pico is "Frozen". If the intruder runs past quickly, the buzzer might not stop! Always use short timeouts (e.g., 5s).
*   **Open SSID**: Never hardcode your WiFi password in an unencrypted file if you plan to share your project. Use a separate `secrets.py` file.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Status Heartbeat**: Every hour, send a "System OK" message to the cloud so you know the Pico hasn't lost power or crashed.

## 1. Project 1399: Advanced Laser Tripwire Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Optical Alignment Optimizer & Peak Signal Finder". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade installation tool for long-range laser perimeters. You will use an OLED display to visualize "Signal Strength" in real-time and implement a "Peak Hold" algorithm to find the absolute center of an LDR over distances exceeding 50 meters.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Feedback Systems**: Providing real-time visual telemetry to aid in physical hardware installation and alignment.
*   **Peak Tracking**: Storing and maintaining the maximum recorded value in a session to verify if a current adjustment is better or worse than previous ones.
*   **UI Gauge Design**: Translating raw 16-bit ADC values into dynamic, human-readable bar graphs on a 128x64 OLED display.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **OLED Display** (SSD1306)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | Power | Continous |
| **LDR Signal** | GP26 | ADC | Alignment sensor|
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | I2C | Real-time GUI |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | I2C | Real-time GUI |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`.
*   From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_rect`.
*   From **Math**, drag `pico_map_range`.
*   From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`.

### 7. Variables
*   **current_signal_raw**: Integer (The live 0-65535 ADC reading from the LDR).
*   **peak_signal_attained**: Integer (The highest value recorded since the Pico was turned on).
*   **gui_bar_width_px**: Integer (The width of the signal gauge in pixels, e.g., 0 to 120).
*   **alignment_quality_pct**: Float (current_signal_raw / 65535 * 100).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Sensing & Signal Ingestion**
1.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Poll** the **LDR Signal**.
2.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Store** into **current_signal_raw**.
3.  From **Logic**, drag `pico_if`. **Check**: Is **current_signal_raw** > **peak_signal_attained**?

**Phase B: Analysis logic**
4.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Update** **peak_signal_attained** if a new maximum is reached.
5.  From **Math**, drag `pico_map_range`. **Translate** the ADC value into a 0-128 pixel range for the **OLED Display**.
6.  **Action**: Calculate the percentage of "Perfect Alignment" for a numeric readout.

**Phase C: Feedback Execution**
7.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_fill_rect`. **Render** a horizontal bar representing the live signal.
8.  From **OLED**, drag `pico_draw_vline`. **Mark** the position of the Peak signal on the gauge.
9.  **Validation**: Point the laser at the sensor. Wiggle it slightly left and right. Watch the OLED bar move. The Peak marker should stay at the maximum point reached, helping you return the laser to the perfect center.
10. **Result**: A precise calibration tool that turns a "Guessing Game" into a scientific alignment process.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the I2C bus and clears the OLED screen.
2.  **Process**: It captures high-speed light intensity data at 100Hz.
3.  **Action**: It identifies the "Optical Hotspot" of the laser beam.
4.  **Reaction**: It provides immediate visual feedback and locks the "Record Best" signal for reference.
5.  **Result**: Rapid installation of complex, long-range security architectures.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import ssd1306
import utime

# Phase A: Sensing & Signal Ingestion
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
ldr = machine.ADC(26)

best_signal = 0

print("--- ALIGNMENT TOOL ACTIVE ---")

while True:
    current = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Phase B: Analysis logic
    if current > best_signal:
        best_signal = current
        
    # Map raw ADC to pixels
    bar_width = int((current / 65535) * 128)
    peak_px = int((best_signal / 65535) * 128)
    percent = (current / 65535) * 100
    
    # Phase C: Feedback Execution
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("ALIGNMENT", 0, 0)
    oled.text(f"Quality: {percent:.1f}%", 0, 10)
    
    # Draw Background Gauge
    oled.rect(0, 25, 128, 15, 1)
    # Draw Live Signal
    oled.fill_rect(0, 25, bar_width, 15, 1)
    # Draw Peak Marker (White line)
    oled.vline(peak_px, 20, 25, 1)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep_ms(50)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Atmospheric Distortion**: Over long distances (100m+), heat shimmer can make the signal oscillate. Use a "Peak over 5 seconds" instead of "Peak over 1 second" for more stable alignment.
*   **LDR Lens Effect**: Photoresistors have a plastic lens that is slightly curved. The "Peak" might not be in the exact geometric center. Trust the OLED readout, not your eyes.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Audio Pitch Feedback**: Connect a buzzer (Project 1341). Change the pitch of the beep based on the signal strength—high pitch = perfect match. This allows you to align the laser without looking at the screen.

## 1. Project 1400: Advanced Advanced Laser Tripwire Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Optical Audio Communicator (Laser-Comms)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a cutting-edge communication system that transmits high-speed data (audio or digital text) over a visible laser beam. You will learn to use "Pulse Width Modulation" (PWM) to encode sound waves and decode them back into audio at the receiver.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Optical Transceivers**: Using light as a transmission medium for complex information rather than just a simple binary trigger.
*   **PWM Audio Encoding**: Mapping sound wave frequencies and amplitudes into the Duty Cycle of a high-frequency switching laser.
*   **Signal Demodulation**: Converting a fluctuating light intensity (Analog/ADC) back into a physical sound wave via a speaker.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2x Raspberry Pi Picos** (Transmitter & Receiver)
*   **Laser Diode**
*   **Photoresistor (LDR)**
*   **Amplified Speaker**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Type | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser Pulse** | GP15 | PWM | Transmit Signal |
| **Optical RX** | GP26 | ADC | Receive Signal |
| **Audio Out** | GP16 | PWM | Speaker Driver |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **Communication**: `pico_set_pin_pwm`
*   **GPIO**: `pico_get_pin_adc`
*   **Math**: `pico_map_range`
*   **Wait**: `pico_wait_microseconds`

### 7. Variables
*   **carrier_frequency_hz**: Integer (Base frequency of the laser, e.g., 20kHz).
*   **audio_sample_raw**: Integer (The analog sound wave value).
*   **reconstructed_duty**: Integer (The value sent to the receiver speaker).
*   **is_link_established**: Boolean (True if the receiver detects the laser light).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Scaffolding & TX Initialization**
1.  From **Communication**, drag `pico_set_pwm_freq`. **Set** to 20,000Hz (Carrier).
2.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Action**: Sample the incoming audio signal.
3.  From **Variables**, drag `set_variable`. **Map** the audio value to the laser PWM Duty Cycle.

**Phase B: Translation & RX logic**
4.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_get_pin_adc`. **Poll** the **Optical RX** (LDR) at the receiver.
5.  From **Math**, drag `pico_map_range`. **Remove** the ambient light offset from the signal.
6.  **Action**: Capture the high-speed "Flicker" of the laser beam.

**Phase C: Orchestration & Audio Execution**
7.  From **GPIO**, drag `pico_set_pin_pwm`. **Drive** the **Audio Out** speaker with the reconstructed wave.
8.  From **Wait**, drag `pico_wait_microseconds`. **Set** the loop to 100us (10kHz sample rate).
9.  **Validation**: Play a song into the Transmitter. Point the laser at the Receiver LDR. The music should play from the Receiver's speaker.
10. **Result**: A secure, line-of-sight communication link that works across rooms at the speed of light.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Transmitter Pico starts oscillating the laser at a frequency above human hearing.
2.  **Process**: It encodes sound waves into the "Brightness Pattern" of the photons.
3.  **Action**: The light carries the music through the air as invisible data.
4.  **Reaction**: The Receiver Pico converts the flickering photons back into electric sound waves.
5.  **Result**: Wireless audio communication with zero radio interference.

### 10. Generated Code (Reference Only)
```python
import machine
import utime

# Phase A: Scaffolding & TX Initialization (Transmitter Role)
def run_transmitter():
    audio_in = machine.ADC(28) 
    laser = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
    laser.freq(40000)
    
    while True:
        val = audio_in.read_u16()
        laser.duty_u16(val)
        utime.sleep_us(100)

# Phase B: Translation & RX logic (Receiver Role)
def run_receiver():
    ldr = machine.ADC(26)
    speaker = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
    speaker.freq(40000)
    
    while True:
        # Phase C: Orchestration & Audio Execution
        light_val = ldr.read_u16()
        speaker.duty_u16(light_val)
        utime.sleep_us(100)

# Implementation Note: In the final prototype, use two separate Picos
# run_transmitter() # Uncomment to use as TX
run_receiver() # Uncomment to use as RX
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sample Rate Aliasing**: If your code is too slow, the audio will sound like static noise. Avoid using `print()` statements inside the high-speed loop.
*   **LDR Response Speed**: Standard LDRs are slow. For high-fidelity audio, use a Photodiode (Project 1391) which can respond to light changes in nanoseconds.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Optical Network**: Instead of audio, transmit digital files by converting bytes into "Long" and "Short" light pulses.
